\mathbb{R}\times Y
carries a natural
\mathbb{R}
-action via
\phi_{t}(s,z)=(t+s,z)
. This induces a natural
\mathbb{R}
-action on
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(\mathbb{R}\times Y,\mathbb{R}\times c;\alpha,\beta)
by pullback and parallel transport, with

\tau_{t}[\mathbf{A},\Phi]=[\phi_{t}^{*}\mathbf{A},\text{Hol}_{(0,y)\to(-t,y)}\Phi].

The endpoint maps to
\alpha
and
\beta
are invariant under this action, as is the section
F_{\pi}
itself, as both metric and perturbation are translation-invariant.

It follows that the moduli space
M(\mathbb{R}\times Y;\alpha,\beta)
carries a natural
\mathbb{R}
-action for which the endpoint maps are invariant. We write the quotient as

\breve{M}(Y;\alpha,\beta)=M(\mathbb{R}\times Y;\alpha,\beta)/\mathbb{R};

then
\breve{M}(Y;\alpha,\beta)
is an
SO(3)
-space with equivariant endpoint maps to
\alpha
and
\beta
.

The set of components

\pi_{0}\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}\times Y,\mathbb{R}\times w;\alpha,\beta)

is in canonical bijection with
\pi_{0}\mathcal{G}(Y,w)\cong\mathbb{Z}.
When
M_{\zeta}(\mathbb{R}\times Y;\alpha,\beta)
consists of nonconstant instantons — that is, either
\alpha\neq\beta
or at least
\zeta
is not the component of the constant trajectory — the
\mathbb{R}
-action is free.

When
\zeta:\alpha\to\beta
is a given trajectory, the quantity
i(\zeta)
depends modulo
8
only on
\alpha
and
\beta
. We define

i(Y,w;\alpha,\beta):=i(\zeta)\mod 8,\quad\text{and similarly}\quad\widetilde{i}(Y,w;\alpha,\beta):=\widetilde{i}(\zeta)\mod 8.

We say that a perturbation
\pi
on
(Y,w)
is regular if all instantons with
i(\zeta)\leq 8
are regular. Regular perturbations exist and in fact are open and dense
6
6

6

If one wanted to achieve transversality for all instantons, not merely those of index bounded above by a fixed quantity, then this would rather be a countable intersection of open and dense sets
in the space of all perturbations on
(Y,w)
by
[
25
, Theorem 4.34]
. It follows by Proposition
3.1
that there exist regular perturbations on
(Y,w)
with arbitrary signature data (first choose a nice perturbation with desired signature data, then choose a nearby regular perturbation).

Suppose
Y
is a rational homology sphere and
w=\varnothing
. Then for any cobordism
(W,\varnothing):(Y,\varnothing)\to(S^{3},\varnothing)
, we have
\widetilde{i}(W,\varnothing;\theta,\theta)=-3(\chi(W)+\sigma(W))/2
, and

\widetilde{i}(W,\varnothing;\alpha,\theta)=\widetilde{i}(Y;\alpha,\theta)+\widetilde{i}(W,\varnothing;\theta,\theta).

It follows that when
w=\varnothing
, the absolute
\mathbb{Z}/8
-grading on
\mathfrak{C}(Y,\varnothing)
coincides with
\widetilde{i}(Y;\alpha,\theta)
.

3.4
Compactifications of the moduli spaces

We move on to discussing the (framed) Uhlenbeck compactification of our moduli spaces. These are only well-behaved in a small dimension range (sufficient for our purposes); even then, the result is not quite a smooth manifold. We will define the compactifications and describe the extra structure they carry.

For the remainder of this section, we will abuse notation and write
\mathbf{A}
to denote an equivalence class of framed connection (so, properly, an equivalence class
[\mathbf{A},\Phi]
, where
\Phi
is a framing above a chosen basepoint). We suppose that the weakly admissible pair
(Y,w)
has been equipped with a regular perturbation
\pi
and a basepoint
y
, so that the framing is chosen at
y
.

Definition 3.14
.

We say that a broken framed
\pi
-instanton on
(Y,w)
between
\alpha
and
\beta
is a sequence
\mathbf{A}_{i}\in\breve{M}(Y,w;\alpha_{i},\alpha_{i+1})
of nonconstant framed
\pi
-instantons modulo translation for
0\leq i\leq n
, with
\alpha_{0}=\alpha
and
\alpha_{n+1}=\beta
, so that

e_{+}(\mathbf{A}_{i})=e_{-}(\mathbf{A}_{i+1})

for
0\leq i\leq n
; that is, the framings agree when parallel transported to each intermediate critical orbit. If
\mathbf{A}_{i}
lies in the homotopy class of path
\zeta_{i}:\alpha_{i}\to\alpha_{i+1}
, we say that the homotopy class of the broken framed instanton
\mathbf{A}
is
\zeta=\zeta_{1}*\cdots*\zeta.

We write
\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta}(Y,w;\alpha,\beta)
for the set of broken framed
\pi
-instantons running from
\alpha
to
\beta
in the homotopy class
\zeta
.
∎

Since
(Y,w)
is fixed, we suppress them from notation for the rest of this section.

The moduli space
\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta}(\alpha,\beta)
carries the the structure of a stratified set, with strata corresponding bijectively to sequences

\alpha\xrightarrow{\zeta_{0}}\alpha_{1}\xrightarrow{\zeta_{1}}\cdots\xrightarrow{\zeta_{n-1}}\alpha_{n}\xrightarrow{\zeta_{n}}\beta;

the corresponding stratum is given by the iterated fiber product

\breve{M}_{\zeta_{0}}(\alpha,\alpha_{1})\times_{\alpha_{1}}\cdots\times_{\alpha_{n}}\breve{M}_{\zeta_{n}}(\alpha_{n},\beta).

The moduli space
\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta}(\alpha,\beta)
has a natural topology so that each stratum has the fiber product topology, given by the notion of
chain-convergence
. We say that a sequence
(\mathbf{A}_{k})
of framed instantons for
k=1,2,\cdots
is
chain-convergent
to a broken trajectory
(\mathbf{B}_{i})_{1\leq i\leq n}
if there is a sequence
t_{k}=(t^{1}_{k},\cdots,t^{n}_{k})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
with each
\lim_{k\to\infty}t^{i}_{k}-t^{i-1}_{k}=\infty
and so that the translates
\tau_{t^{i}_{k}}(\mathbf{A}_{k})
converge in the appropriate Sobolev topology on compact sets to
\mathbf{B}_{i}
.

There is a straightforward extension of this notion to chain-convergence of broken framed instantons (possibly with different numbers of components). If
\mathbf{A}_{n}
is a sequence of broken instantons in the homotopy class
\zeta
which chain-converge to
\mathbf{A}
, then
\mathbf{A}
also lies in the homotopy class
\zeta
.

We have the following theorem on the structure of this topological space; this is stated in slightly different form as
[
25
, Proposition 4.43]
. The result can be straightforwardly obtained by either the implicit function theorem methods of
[
20
, Chapter 19]
(the method of proof preferred in
[
25
]
), or by the Banach fixed-point methods of
[
8
, Chapter 7.2]
, which we find more convenient when studying obstructed gluing theory in Section
5
below.

Proposition 3.15
.

If
\zeta:\alpha\to\beta
is a homotopy class with
i(\zeta)\leq 8
, then
\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta}(\alpha,\beta)
is compact. Further, whenever
\zeta_{0},\cdots,\zeta_{n}
is a sequence of homotopy classes between the critical points whose concatenation is
\zeta
, there are continuous
SO(3)
-equivariant gluing maps

(L,\infty]^{n}\times\breve{M}_{\zeta_{1}}(\alpha,\alpha_{1})\times_{\alpha_{1}}\cdots\times_{\alpha_{n}}\breve{M}_{\zeta_{n}}(\alpha_{n},\beta)\to\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta}(\alpha,\beta)

which are open embeddings onto a neighborhood of the corresponding stratum; these maps are local diffeomorphisms on each stratum separately.

In particular, when
i(\zeta)\leq 8
, the moduli space of possibly broken framed instantons
\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta}
is a compact topological manifold equipped with a stratification and a smooth structure on each stratum, as well as gluing maps giving continuous charts near the corners points which are smooth on each stratum. This is nearly the structure of a smooth manifold, but not quite, and we will need to take special care when taking transverse intersections or regular values of maps defined on such objects. Before discussing that, we record the structure on
\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta}
in the following definition.

Definition 3.16
.

A stratified-smooth manifold of dimension
n
consists of the following data:

(i)

A partially ordered set
\Delta
with an order-preserving
dimension
function
d:\Delta\to\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
with the maximum value
n
,

(ii)

A topological space
P
stratified by subsets
P_{s}
for
s\in\Delta
, with
\overline{P_{s}}=\bigcup_{t<s}P_{t}
,

(iii)

A smooth structure on each stratum
P_{s}
that turns it into a smooth manifold of dimension
d(s)
.

We demand that for each
x\in P_{s}
, there exists an open, stratum-preserving embedding

\phi:[0,\varepsilon)^{n-d(s)}\times\mathbb{R}^{d(s)}\to P,

with
\phi(0)=x
, so that
\phi
induces an isomorphism of posets
\{0,1\}^{n-d(s)}\to\Delta_{\geq s}
, and so that
\phi
is a local diffeomorphism on each stratum.

If
M
is a smooth manifold and
P
is a stratified-smooth manifold, we say
f:P\to M
is stratified-smooth if it is continuous and smooth on each stratum. We say that
f
is stratified-transverse to
S\subset M
if
f|_{P_{s}}
is transverse to
S
for all
s\in\Delta
.
\diamondsuit

∎

In particular, a stratified-smooth manifold is a topological manifold with corners. Proposition
3.4
above asserts that
\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta}(\alpha,\beta)
is a stratified-smooth
SO(3)
-manifold, where
\Delta
is the set of sequences
(\zeta_{0},\cdots,\zeta_{n})
of homotopy classes of paths between various critical orbits
\zeta_{j}:\alpha_{j}\to\alpha_{j+1}
, with
\alpha_{0}=\alpha
while
\alpha_{n+1}=\beta
and
\zeta_{0}*\cdots*\zeta_{k}=\zeta
. We say
t<s
if
s
is obtained from
t
by concatenating some of its constituent homotopy classes.

The stratum corresponding to
s=(\zeta_{0},\cdots,\zeta_{k})
is

\breve{M}_{\zeta_{0}}(\alpha,\alpha_{1})\times_{\alpha_{1}}\cdots\times_{\alpha_{n}}\breve{M}_{\zeta_{k}}(\alpha_{k},\beta),

with
d(s)=\sum_{j=0}^{n}\left(\widetilde{i}(\zeta_{j})-1\right)+\dim\alpha.

In our work later, we will need to look at transverse intersections of a stratified-transverse map with a smooth submanifold
S\subset M
. Unfortunately, such transverse intersections are
no longer
stratified-smooth manifolds; they need not even be topological manifolds in general. Nevertheless, the resulting structure (called a
stratified-smooth space
) is sufficient for all of our purposes. In the definition of the instanton chain complex, we will use a chain complex which calculates singular homology, whose generators are stratified-smooth spaces up to a certain notion of equivalence. We rapidly develop the foundations of stratified-smooth spaces in Appendix
A
. These are similar to, but simpler than, the notion of
\delta
-chain; this notion has appeared in a handful of references, including
[
20
,
22
,
25
]
.

Remark 3.17
.

The notation chosen here differs from the notation chosen for the same spaces in
[
25
]
, where
\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta}(\alpha,\beta)
would have been written as
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{E,z,k,\delta}(\alpha,\beta)
. We prefer the current notation both for brevity and for consistency with previous literature.
∎

We now move on to a similar discussion for general cobordisms. Suppose
(W,c):(Y,w)\to(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime})
is equipped with a regular perturbation
\pi_{W}
, extending choices of regular perturbations
\pi
and
\pi^{\prime}
on the ends, as well as a path
\gamma
from
y\in Y
to
y^{\prime}\in Y^{\prime}
; we choose the framing in
W
above
\gamma(0)
, and parallel transport it along the path
\gamma
as necessary.

Definition 3.18
.

We say that a broken framed instanton on
(W,c)
between
\alpha
and
\alpha^{\prime}
is a triple
(\mathbf{A}_{i},\mathbf{B},\mathbf{A}_{j}^{\prime})
:

•

A (possibly empty) sequence
\mathbf{A}_{i}\in\breve{M}_{\zeta_{i}}(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{i+1})
of nonconstant framed
\pi
-instantons (modulo translation) for
0\leq i\leq n
, with
\alpha_{0}=\alpha
so that

e_{+}(\mathbf{A}_{i})=e_{-}(\mathbf{A}_{i+1})

for
0\leq i<n
;

•

A framed
\pi_{W}
-instanton
\mathbf{B}\in M_{z}(W,c;\alpha_{n+1},\beta_{0})
, with
e_{-}(\mathbf{B})=e_{+}(\mathbf{A}_{n})\in\alpha_{n+1}
;

•

A (possibly empty) sequence
\mathbf{A}^{\prime}_{i}\in\breve{M}_{\zeta^{\prime}_{i}}(\alpha^{\prime}_{i},\alpha^{\prime}_{i+1})
of nonconstant framed
\pi^{\prime}
-instantons (modulo translation) for
0\leq i\leq m
, with
\alpha^{\prime}_{m+1}=\alpha^{\prime}
, so that

e_{+}(\mathbf{A}^{\prime}_{i})=e_{-}(\mathbf{A}^{\prime}_{i+1})

for
0\leq i<m
, and
e_{-}(\mathbf{A}^{\prime}_{0})=e_{+}(\mathbf{B})\in\alpha^{\prime}_{0}
.

We say this broken framed instanton represents the path of connections along
(W,c)
given by
\zeta_{1}*\cdots*\zeta_{n}*z*\zeta_{1}^{\prime}*\cdots*\zeta_{m}^{\prime}.

We write
M^{+}_{z}(W,c;\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})
for the set of broken framed instantons on
(W,c)
running from
\alpha
to
\alpha^{\prime}
in the homotopy class
z
.
∎

Once again, this is a stratified set, stratified by iterated fiber products of the relevant moduli spaces; thus all strata inherit a natural smooth structure. The topology is similar to the case of a cylinder: we say that a sequence
\mathbf{A}_{k}
of framed instantons on
(W,c)
is chain-convergent to a broken framed instanton
(\mathbf{B}_{i},\mathbf{A},\mathbf{C}_{j})
if there are sequences
t_{k}=(t_{k}^{1},\cdots,t_{k}^{n})\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n}
and
s_{k}=(s_{k}^{1},\cdots,s_{k}^{m})\in\mathbb{R}_{-}^{m}
whose sequential differences go to
+\infty
and
-\infty
, respectively, so that the framed instantons
\mathbf{A}_{k}
converge to
\mathbf{A}
on compact sets, and so that upon restricting
\mathbf{A}
to the respective ends,
\tau_{t_{k}^{i}}(\mathbf{A})
converges on compact subsets
7
7

7

Notice that the assumptions on
t
and
s
imply that for sufficiently large
k
, the domain of definition of these translates contains any particular compact subset of
\mathbb{R}\times Y
or
\mathbb{R}\times Y^{\prime}
.
of
\mathbb{R}\times Y_{1}
to
\mathbf{B}_{i}
while
\tau_{s_{k}^{j}}(\mathbf{A}_{k})
converges on compact subsets of
\mathbb{R}\times Y_{2}
to
\mathbf{C}_{j}
(both modulo gauge, and everything in sight equipped with a framing; in restricting to the ends we parallel transport the framing along a base-path
\gamma
to the basepoint on the end).

Then for
i(z)\leq 7
there once again exist
SO(3)
-equivariant gluing maps, stated explicitly in
[
25
, Proposition 4.42]
. We record this as a proposition below.

Proposition 3.19
.

Suppose
(W,c)
is a cobordism equipped with a regular perturbation. If
i(z)\leq 7
, then
M^{+}_{z}(W,c;\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})
is compact with the topology induced by chain-convergence. Furthermore, with the stratification described above,
M^{+}_{z}(W;\alpha,\beta)
is equipped with the structure of a stratified-smooth manifold.

3.5
Reducibles and regularity

We would like to construct regular perturbations
\pi_{W}
on cobordisms
(W,c):(Y,w,\pi)\to(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime},\pi^{\prime})
, where
\pi,\pi^{\prime}
are fixed regular perturbations on the boundary components. Unfortunately, this is not always possible, and in this section we will discuss the obstruction.

Achieving regularity at irreducible connections is standard. A generic perturbation has all irreducible instantons cut out regularly; one may see this by the arguments of
[
7
, Section 5.5.1]
except for those irreducible flat connections which are central on the ends, where one needs more care (and to introduce interior perturbations). It is more difficult to ensure regularity at the reducibles. If one of the boundary components is admissible, there are no reducible instantons whatsoever, so we may as well assume that both boundary components are rational homology spheres.

Convention 3.20
.

Unless otherwise specified, for the duration of this section,
(W,c,\pi_{W}):(Y,w,\pi)\to(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime},\pi^{\prime})
is a cobordism between rational homology spheres equipped with regular perturbations
\pi,\pi^{\prime}
, while
\pi_{W}
is a perturbation on the cobordism extending the chosen perturbations on the ends. The signature data functions of these perturbations are written
\sigma_{\pi}
and
\sigma_{\pi^{\prime}}
, respectively.
∎

First we recall the enumeration of reducible instantons on a cobordism
(W,c):(Y,w,\pi)\to(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime},\pi^{\prime})
. Consider the set of components of the space of reducible connections on
(W,c)
; we write
\mathfrak{A}(W,c)
for the set of components which include no central connections, and
\mathfrak{Z}(W,c)
for the set of components which include some central connection. The following enumeration of these components given in
[
25
, Proposition 1.7]
.

Proposition 3.21
.

Let
(W,c,\pi_{W}):(Y,w,\pi)\to(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime},\pi^{\prime})
be a cobordism between rational homology spheres. Then there are canonical bijections

\displaystyle\mathfrak{Z}(W,c)

\displaystyle\cong\{x\in H^{2}(W;\mathbb{Z})\mid 2x={\rm PD}(c)\},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{A}(W,c)

\displaystyle\cong\big\{\{x,y\}\subset H^{2}(W;\mathbb{Z})\mid x+y={\rm PD}(c),\;\;x\neq y\big\}.

The central connections are regular if and only if
b^{+}(W)=0
. As discussed in Section
3.2
, for an abelian instanton
\Lambda
to be regular, its
normal ASD operator

D^{\nu}_{\Lambda,\pi_{W}}
must be surjective. This is a complex linear operator, so its index is an even integer; by homotopy invariance of index, this quantity depends only on
\Lambda
and the choice of
\pi,\pi^{\prime}
on the ends.

Definition 3.22
.

Suppose
\Lambda
is an abelian connection on the cobordism
(W,c,\pi_{W})
. We write
N(\Lambda;\pi,\pi^{\prime})
for the index of the normal ASD operator
D^{\nu}_{\Lambda,\pi_{W}}
.
∎

If
\Lambda
is to be cut out transversely, we need at the very least that
N(\Lambda;\pi,\pi^{\prime})\geq 0
. (We may need more than this, as we will discuss later.) To make this discussion more concrete, we can compute these indices with the Atiyah-Patodi-Singer index theorem.

To state the calculation, we set some notation. For a cohomology class
x\in H^{2}(W)
, write
x_{i}\in H^{2}(Y_{i})
for its restriction to each boundary component. Notice that because the boundary components are rational homology spheres, the map
j:H^{2}_{c}(W;\mathbb{Q})\to H^{2}(W;\mathbb{Q})
is an isomorphism. Whenever
x\in H^{2}(W;\mathbb{Z})
we write
x^{2}\in\mathbb{Q}
to mean the rational number obtained as

x^{2}=\langle(j^{-1}x)^{2},[W]\rangle,

where
[W]\in H_{4}^{\text{lf}}(W;\mathbb{Q})
is the fundamental class in locally finite homology.

The following calculation is the main result of
[
25
, Section 4.5]
.

Proposition 3.23
.

If
\Lambda:\alpha\to\alpha^{\prime}
is an abelian instanton in the component labeled by
\{x,y\}
, then
N(\Lambda;\pi,\pi^{\prime})
is the even integer

\displaystyle N(\Lambda;\pi,\pi^{\prime})=

\displaystyle-2(x-y)^{2}+2(b_{1}(W)-b^{+}(W))

\displaystyle+\frac{\sigma_{\pi^{\prime}}(\alpha^{\prime})+\rho(\textup{ad}_{\alpha^{\prime}})}{2}-\frac{\sigma_{\pi}(\alpha)+\rho(\textup{ad}_{\alpha})}{2}

\displaystyle+1-\frac{r(\alpha)+r(\alpha^{\prime})}{2},

(3.24)

where
\rho(\textup{ad}_{\alpha})
is the Atiyah–Patodi–Singer rho invariant of the extended Hessian operator
\widehat{\textup{Hess}}_{\alpha}(CS)
,

r(\alpha)=3
if
\alpha
is a central connection and
r(\alpha)=1
if
\alpha
is abelian. Similar comments apply to
\rho(\textup{ad}_{\alpha^{\prime}})
and
r(\alpha^{\prime})
.

The quantity
-2(x-y)^{2}
may be understood as eight times the
topological energy

\left(c_{2}(\mathbf{E}_{c})-\frac{1}{4}c_{1}(\mathbf{E}_{c})^{2}\right)=\frac{1}{8\pi^{2}}\int_{W}\text{tr}F_{\Lambda}^{2},

where
\mathbf{E}_{c}
is the bundle associated to the geometric representative
c
.

The expression in the second line of (
3.24
) can be expressed in terms of the rho invariant of the perturbed extended Hessian
\widehat{\text{Hess}}_{\alpha}(CS+\pi)
, and the corresponding
\rho
-invariant for
\alpha^{\prime}
; while this is simpler than the above expression, the statement of the proposition makes clear that
\pi
enters in the form of its signature data. Still, we record this perturbed
\rho
invariant as a definition.

Definition 3.25
.

Let
(Y,w,\pi)
be a rational homology sphere with an oriented
1
-cycle and a nice perturbation. Then the perturbed rho invariant
\rho_{\pi}:\mathfrak{A}(Y,w)\to\mathbb{Q}
is defined as

\rho_{\pi}(\alpha)=\rho(\text{ad}_{\alpha})+\sigma_{\pi}(\alpha).\qed

Remark 3.26
.

In Section
7
, we will use
\rho_{\pi}
to give an invariant of rational homology spheres which conjecturally computes twice the Casson-Walker invariant, in the spirit of Taubes’ interpretation of the Casson invariant
[
35
]
as a signed count of irreducible flat connections. For rational homology spheres, one must use the perturbed rho invariant as a correction term, analogous to Walker’s definition
[
36
]
of his invariant.
∎

Next, we seek to find perturbations
\pi_{W}
so that all instantons are regular. To guarantee that this is possible, we will need to make assumptions on the normal index. Later in this article we will seek to weaken these assumptions in two different ways, which are both recorded in the definition below.

Definition 3.27
.

Suppose
\Lambda
is a component of abelian connections on
(W,c):(Y,w,\pi)\to(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime},\pi^{\prime})
corresponding to the pair of cohomology classes
\{x,y\}
.

•

We say
\Lambda
is unobstructed if either
-2(x-y)^{2}<0
or
b_{1}(W)-b^{+}(W)\leq N(\Lambda;\pi,\pi^{\prime})
; otherwise we say
\Lambda
is obstructed.

•

We say
\Lambda
is pseudocentral if
\Lambda
is central on both ends and has
(x-y)^{2}=0
.

Quantifying over all components, there are three types of cobordism of interest to us.

•

If every component of abelian connections over
(W,c)
is unobstructed, and either
b^{+}(W)=0
or
(W,c)
supports no central connections, then we say
(W,c)
is an unobstructed cobordism.

•

If every component of abelian connections over
(W,c)
is either unobstructed or pseudocentral, and either
b^{+}(W)=0
or
(W,c)
supports no central connections, we say that
(W,c)
is a pseudo-unobstructed cobordism.

•

If
b_{1}(W)=b^{+}(W)=0
and all but one component of abelian connections over
(W,c)
is unobstructed, while the one obstructed reducible is abelian on both ends and has
N(\Lambda;\pi,\pi^{\prime})=-2
, we say that
(W,c)
is a nearly unobstructed cobordism.∎

Remark 3.28
.

The condition on
b^{+}(W)
and central connections is to ensure that any central connection (which necessarily remains an instanton after perturbation) are automatically cut out transversely, so that we can focus our analysis on the more intricate case of abelian reducibles. If the perturbation
\pi_{W}
has small interior part, a
\pi_{W}
-instanton necessarily has
\int\text{tr}(F_{\Lambda}^{2})>-\varepsilon
, so that
-2(x-y)^{2}\geq 0
, which is why we ignore those components with
-2(x-y)^{2}<0
.

We will see in Section
4.3
that unobstructed cobordisms induce chain maps between the tilde complexes.

A pseudocentral connection should be understood to be as close to central as an abelian connection can possibly be; a central connection on the cobordism restricts to central connections on the ends, and is also automatically flat, so that the quantity
(x-y)^{2}
is automatically zero. We will see in Section
6.1
that pseudo-unobstructed cobordisms also induce maps on the tilde complexes, for which each pseudocentral connection contributes as if it were two central connections.

The last type of cobordisms — nearly unobstructed cobordisms — are analyzed in detail in Section
5
, where we show that they also induce maps on instanton homology, but not between the tilde complexes (one must pass to a ‘suspension’). Using this, we will show that the only type of obstructed reducible we actually have to deal with are the pseudocentral connections.
∎

It follows immediately from Proposition
3.5
and the fact that
\rho(\theta)=\sigma_{\pi}(\theta)=0
for central connections
\theta
that the pseudocentral connections have particularly simple normal index.

Corollary 3.29
.

If
\Lambda
is a pseudocentral connection on the cobordism
(W,c):(Y,w,\pi)\to(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime},\pi^{\prime})
, then
N(\Lambda;\pi,\pi^{\prime})=2(b_{1}(W)-b^{+}(W)-1)
.

For each type of cobordism named in Definition
3.5
, we can find special types of perturbations: either we can find regular perturbations, or at least perturbations which are sufficiently regular for the analysis we need to carry out.

Theorem 3.30
.

Suppose we are given a cobordism
(W,c):(Y,w,\pi)\to(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime},\pi^{\prime})
.

(i)

If
(W,c)
is unobstructed, there exists a regular perturbation
\pi_{W}
on
(W,c)
extending
\pi
and
\pi^{\prime}
; the part of
\pi_{W}
supported in the interior of
W
may be taken to be arbitrarily small. Given any two regular perturbations
\pi_{W}
with the same restrictions to the ends, there is a regular family
\pi_{W}(t)
of perturbations interpolating between them.

(ii)

If
(W,c)
is pseudo-unobstructed, there exists a regular perturbation
\pi_{W}
on
(W,c)
extending
\pi,\pi^{\prime}
with arbitrarily small interior part so that all instantons are regular, except possibly for the pseudocentral connections; the same is true for 1-parameter families of perturbations.

(iii)

If
(W,c)
is nearly unobstructed, there exist perturbations
\pi_{W}
with arbitrarily small interior part, so that all irreducible instantons are regular, all reducible instantons
\Lambda
with
N(\Lambda;\pi,\pi^{\prime})\geq 0
are regular, and so that for the reducible instanton with
N(\Lambda;\pi,\pi^{\prime})=-2
, we have
\textup{ker }D^{\nu}_{\Lambda,\pi_{W}}=0
; the same is true for 1-parameter families of perturbations. We call such perturbations nearly regular.

Sketch of proof.

First, fix a perturbation
\pi_{W}
with small interior part for which abelian instantons are cut out transversely within the reducible locus; arguing along the lines of
[
2
, Section 3.3.1]
, one may guarantee that the central connections are isolated among reducible
\pi_{W}
-instantons (and that there are exactly
2^{b_{1}(W)}
of them). In doing so, we can focus on the reducible components which contain no central connections (henceforth, abelian components). A
\pi_{W}
-instanton has
\int\text{tr}(F_{\Lambda}^{2})\approx\|F_{\mathbf{A}}\|^{2}
(the error term coming from the perturbation on the interior; recall that the perturbations on the ends are assumed to vanish on the reducible locus itself), so that the topological energy of an instanton is nearly non-negative; because
-2(x-y)^{2}
lies in
\frac{2}{|H_{1}(\partial W)|}
, so long as the interior part is taken sufficiently small, the quantity
-2(x-y)^{2}
is non-negative for any abelian
\pi_{W}
-instanton. So we may focus our attention on those components with
-2(x-y)^{2}\geq 0
.

Generically, the space of reducible
\pi_{W}
-instantons in each abelian component has dimension
b_{1}(W)-b^{+}(W)
; if
\pi_{W}(t)
is a one-parameter family of perturbations, the space of abelian connections which are
\pi_{W}(t)
-instantons for some
t
generically has dimension
b_{1}(W)-b^{+}(W)+1
. Now

\Lambda\mapsto D^{\nu}_{\Lambda,\pi_{W}}

defines a map from this space of abelian instantons to the space of complex linear Fredholm operators of index
N=N(\Lambda;\pi,\pi^{\prime})
, and we want this map to miss the subspace of operators with either nontrivial kernel or nontrivial cokernel, depending on whether
N
is negative or non-negative. By
[
25
, Lemma 4.35]
, the space of operators with non-trivial kernel and cokernel has codimension
|N|+2
, and the argument of
[
25
, Theorem 4.37]
shows that for
\pi_{W}
sufficiently small, the map from the space of pairs
(\mathbf{A},\pi_{W})
of perturbations and abelian
\pi_{W}
-instantons is transverse to this locus.

Thus for generic small
\pi_{W}
and generic small 1-parameter families
\pi_{W}(t)
, we can avoid the locus of operators with nontrivial kernel and cokernel so long as
b_{1}(W)-b^{+}(W)+1<|N|+2
, or equivalently,
b_{1}(W)-b^{+}(W)\leq|N|
. This is guaranteed for unobstructed and nearly unobstructed cobordisms by definition; further, in the unobstructed case, either
b_{1}(W)-b^{+}(W)\geq-1
so that
N\geq 0
and all normal ASD operators have trivial cokernel, or
b_{1}(W)-b^{+}(W)\leq-2
and reducibles do not generically appear even in one-parameter families. The same argument applies in the case of pseudo-unobstructed cobordisms, except that one ignores the pseudocentral components.
∎

We conclude this section with a more detailed discussion of pseudocentral connections. First, we can obtain more precise control over the perturbations on the pseudocentral connections than described above.

Proposition 3.31
.

If
(W,c)
is a pseudo-unobstructed cobordism, we can obtain a perturbation
\pi_{W}
(and paths of perturbations between them) which is regular at all instantons except possibly the pseudocentral instantons, with the following properties at the pseudocentral instantons. The behavior depends on the quantity
c=b_{1}(W)-b^{+}(W)
.

(i)

If
c<-1
, then
b^{+}(W)>1
, so that for a generic perturbation (and a generic path of perturbations), there are no abelian instantons whatsoever.

(ii)

If
c=-1
, then
b^{+}(W)>0
, so that for a generic perturbation there are no abelian instantons whatsoever; for a generic path of perturbations, there will generically be an isolated set of pseudocentral instantons, which are such that the normal Fredhom operator has trivial kernel.

(iii)

If
c=0
, then for a generic perturbation there are finitely many pseudocentral instantons, which may be assumed to have normal Fredholm operator with trivial kernel; the same is true in families.

(iv)

If
c=1
, then for a generic perturbation there is a 1-dimensional family of pseudocentral instantons, which are all regular; however, for a generic 1-parameter family of perturbations, there is a 2-dimensional family of pseudocentral instantons,

(v)

If
c>1
, for a generic perturbation and path of perturbations, all pseudocentral instantons are regular.

In any of these cases, we call such a perturbation pseudo-regular.

Proof.

The condition needed to ensure that all abelian instantons are cut out with trivial kernel or cokernel is
c\leq|N|+1
, whereas the condition needed to ensure that all abelian instantons are cut out transversely in 1-parameter families is
c\leq|N|
. Corollary
3.5
asserts that
N=2c-2
. The inequality
c\leq|2c-2|+1
holds for all
c
, whereas the inequality
c\leq|2c-2|
holds for all integers
c\neq 1
.

So for
c\neq 1
generically one of the kernel and cokernel will be trivial for all pseudocentral instantons appearing in 1-parameter families. As for which it is, the cokernel is trivial if
N\geq 0
, while the kernel is trivial if
N\leq 0
; in terms of
c
, the former happens when
c\geq 1
and the latter when
c\leq 1
. This gives the pentachotomy above.
∎

As mentioned earlier, we will show in Section
6.1
that all pseudo-unobstructed cobordisms define well-defined induced maps on tilde complexes. Cases (i) and (v) require no further care; case (iv) will be shown irrelevant by index considerations; cases (ii)-(iii) will require the most work, but fall to the techniques of Section
5
.

We conclude this section by pointing out that if one is free to change the perturbations on the ends, every cobordism is pseudounobstructed. We will use this in Section
6.1
to define induced maps on equivariant isntanton homology for
any
cobordism
(W,c)
for which
b^{+}(W)=0
or
(W,c)
supports no central connections. We phrase the sattement below slightly more generally (including the case when
(Y,w)
is non-trivial admissible, where it is tautologically true) for ease of reference.

Lemma 3.32
.

Let
(W,c):(Y,w)\to(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime})
be a cobordism between weakly admissible pairs. Then there exist regular perturbations
\pi,\pi^{\prime}
on
(Y,w)
and
(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime})
respectively so that
(W,c):(Y,w,\pi)\to(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime},\pi^{\prime})
is a pseudo-unobstructed cobordism.

Proof.

We saw in Proposition
3.1
that there exist regular perturbations with arbitrary signature data. Because the normal index only depends on the signature data on the ends, this is really a lemma about signature data functions. Recall that the set of signature data functions on
Y
is affine over the abelian group
F_{Y,w}=\text{Map}(\mathfrak{A}(Y,w),4\mathbb{Z})
. Pick arbitrarily signature data functions
\sigma_{0},\sigma_{0}^{\prime}
on
Y
and
Y^{\prime}
. Write
r:\mathfrak{A}(W,c)\to\mathfrak{A}(Y,w)
for the restriction and similarly for
r^{\prime}
.

The normal index function computed in Proposition
3.5
may be considered as a function
N:\mathfrak{A}(W,\mathbf{E})\times F_{Y,w}\times F_{Y^{\prime},w^{\prime}}\to 2\mathbb{Z}
, with

N(\Lambda,f,f^{\prime})=N(\Lambda;\sigma_{0}+f,\sigma_{0}^{\prime}+f^{\prime})=N(\Lambda;\sigma_{0},\sigma_{0}^{\prime})+\frac{f^{\prime}(r^{\prime}(\Lambda))-f(r(\Lambda))}{2}.

Here, if
r(\Lambda)
or
r^{\prime}(\Lambda)
are central, we interpret
f(r(\Lambda))=0
and similarly
f^{\prime}\big(r^{\prime}(\Lambda)\big)=0
.

Notice that
N(\Lambda,0,0)
takes on a set of values which is bounded below, because for fixed
\pi,\pi^{\prime}
, the formula given in Proposition
3.5
has only one unbounded term: the first one. (The sets
r\mathfrak{A}(W,c)
and
r^{\prime}\mathfrak{A}(W,c)
are subsets of finite sets, so the latter terms have only finitely many output values.) The first term is always non-negative for instantons
\Lambda
. As a result, there is some nonnegative integer
n
so that
N(\Lambda,0,0)-\big(b_{1}(W)-b^{+}(W)\big)\geq-2n
for all components of reducible connections
\Lambda
which might possibly contain an instanton.

So set
f(\alpha)=-4n
for all
\alpha\in\mathfrak{A}(Y,w)
and
f^{\prime}(\alpha^{\prime})=4n
for all
\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{A}(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime})
. If
\Lambda
is not a pseudocentral reducible, then one of
r(\Lambda)
or
r^{\prime}(\Lambda)
is non-central,
f^{\prime}(r^{\prime}(\Lambda))-f(r(\Lambda))
is either
4n
or
8n
. As a result,
N(\Lambda,f,f^{\prime})\geq\big(b_{1}(W)-b^{+}(W)\big)
for all reducibles other than pseudocentral connections.

Therefore
\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma_{0}^{\prime}+f
and
\sigma=\sigma_{0}+f
satisfy the desired hypotheses.
∎

3.6
Families of metrics

The previous discussion will be enough to show that each
(Y,w,\pi)
gives rise to an instanton chain complex, and each unobstructed cobordism with regular perturbation
(W,c,\pi_{W})
gives rise to a chain map between the instanton complexes. Even ignoring the ‘unobstructed’ caveat, this is not adequate. First, we want to ensure that the induced map is independent of the perturbation
\pi
on the interior up to chain-homotopy. Second, we want to ensure that the induced maps compose correctly.

We will need additional moduli spaces and structure theorems for them to prove these claims. Instead of dealing with the two issues separately, we introduce families of possibly
broken
metrics to handle both cases at once. Because we will be focused on independence and we are not worried about our homotopies being well-defined up to homotopy, we only discuss the case of metrics and perturbations parameterized by the interval, which we choose to write as
I=[0,\infty]
. If one chooses any regular metric and perturbation
g(0),\pi(0)
and
g(\infty),\pi(\infty)
with the interior part of the perturbation sufficiently small, there is always a regular family of metrics and perturbations
g(t),\pi(t)
extending these (regulular in the sense that a certain parameterized derivative operator is surjective for each
\pi(t)
-instanton
\mathbf{A}
; it need not be the case that each
\pi(t)
is a regular perturbation).

Suppose

(W,c):(Y_{0},w_{0})\to(Y_{1},w_{1}),\quad(W^{\prime},c^{\prime}):(Y_{1},w_{1})\to(Y_{2},w_{2})

are cobordisms. We may define a family of composites

W\cup_{L}W^{\prime}=W\cup_{Y_{1}}[-L,L]\times Y_{1}\cup W^{\prime}

where
L\in[0,\infty)
is a length parameter. The resulting manifold has a natural smooth structure, metric, and perturbation, which agrees on the left end with that of
W
and on the right end with that of
W^{\prime}
. So long as
L
is sufficiently large, if
\pi
and
\pi^{\prime}
are regular perturbations, so is the composite perturbation
\pi\cup_{L}\pi^{\prime}
.

We model the case
L=\infty
slightly differently, as
W\cup_{Y_{1}}[0,\infty)\times Y_{1}\sqcup(-\infty,0]\times Y_{1}\cup_{Y_{1}}W^{\prime}
, and we call this a broken metric on the composite
W\cup_{Y_{1}}W^{\prime}
. In this way the family of composites above defines a smooth family of (possibly broken) cylindrical end metrics on the composite, indexed by the compact interval
[0,\infty]
. We say that a
\pi(\infty)
-instanton from
\alpha_{0}
to
\alpha_{2}
is an element of

\bigsqcup_{\alpha_{0}\xrightarrow{z}\alpha_{1}\xrightarrow{z^{\prime}}\alpha_{2}}M_{z}(W,c;\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1})\times_{\alpha_{1}}M_{z^{\prime}}(W^{\prime},c^{\prime};\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}),

and its homotopy class is
z*z^{\prime}
. More generally, a broken
\pi(\infty)
-instanton consists of a sequence
(\mathbf{A}^{0}_{i},\mathbf{A}_{W},\mathbf{A}^{1}_{j},\mathbf{A}_{W^{\prime}},\mathbf{A}^{2}_{k})
, where
\mathbf{A}^{j}_{i}
are broken framed instantons on
(Y_{j},w_{j})
for
1\leq i\leq n_{j}
(possibly
n_{j}=0
), and
\mathbf{A}_{W}
and
\mathbf{A}_{W^{\prime}}
are instantons on
W
and
W^{\prime}
, respectively. As usual, we demand that for each framed instanton in this sequence, the parallel transport of each framing to
+\infty
coincides with the parallel transport of the next framing to
-\infty
.

Now if
W
is equipped with a family
g(t)
of metrics (with
g(\infty)
possibly broken) and
\pi(t)
of perturbations (with
\pi(\infty)
possibly broken), one may define a moduli space
M_{z}(W,c,I;\alpha,\beta)
, whose elements are pairs
(t,\mathbf{A})
, where
t\in[0,\infty]
and
\mathbf{A}
is a (gauge equivalence class of) framed
\pi(t)
-instanton in the path
z
of connections along
(W,c)
. Precisely, the moduli space for the family is

M^{+}_{z}(W\cup W^{\prime},c\cup c^{\prime},I;\alpha,\beta)=\bigcup_{L\in I}M^{+}_{z}(W\cup_{L}W^{\prime},c\cup_{L}c^{\prime},\pi\cup_{L}\pi^{\prime};\alpha,\beta),

topologized as usual using chain-convergence.

Remark 3.33
.

The subspace

M^{+}_{z}(W\sqcup W^{\prime},c\sqcup c^{\prime},\pi(\infty);\alpha,\beta)\subset M^{+}_{z}(W\cup W^{\prime},c\cup c^{\prime},I;\alpha,\beta)

is not a stratified-smooth space, and is not the same as the fiber product
M^{+}_{z_{+}}(W_{+};\alpha,\gamma)\times_{\gamma}M^{+}_{z_{-}}(W_{-};\gamma,\beta)
. This is analogous to the fact that the subset of
\Delta^{2}
given by the union of positive-codimension strata is not a stratified-smooth space, and this subset is not the same as
\partial\Delta^{2}
; the natural map
\partial\Delta^{2}\to\Delta^{2}
is not one-to-one, but rather two-to-one on the corners of
\Delta^{2}
.
∎

Then the main result of
[
25
, Section 4.8]
is that the same structure results carry through for this parameterized moduli space.

Proposition 3.34
.

If
I
is a regular family of (possibly broken) metrics and perturbations on
(W,c)
, and
z:\alpha\to\alpha^{\prime}
is a homotopy class with
i(z)\leq 6
, then
M^{+}_{z}(W,c,I;\alpha,\beta)
carries the natural structure of a compact stratified-smooth manifold of dimension
\widetilde{i}(z)+\dim\alpha+1
.

The boundary strata of this stratified-smooth manifold are as expected: two of them correspond to restricting the family to its endpoints, while the others correspond to factorizations on the two ends of
(W,c)
. In Section
4.3
below, these will be used to construct homotopies between the induced chain maps of cobordisms.

3.7
Orientations

We conclude our discussion by introducing orientations on the moduli spaces introduced thusfar. Any discussion of orientations is somewhat tedious, so we content ourselves to quoting the results of
[
25
, Section 5]
in a way which is hopefully accessible and easy to use, while making our orientation conventions clear.

First, there is the question of what data is needed to define an orientation on each of the relevant moduli spaces. This uses the notion of
homology orientation
: if
(W,c)
is a cobordism
(Y,w)\to(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime})
, a homology orientation is an orientation of the real vector space
H^{1}(W)\oplus H^{2,+}(W)\oplus H^{1}(Y)
.

Associated to each critical orbit
\alpha
on
(Y,w)
and
\alpha^{\prime}
on
(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime})
are two-element sets
\Lambda(\alpha)
and
\Lambda(\alpha^{\prime})
. In Morse theory, these would correspond to an orientation of the unstable manifold at
\alpha
; the infinite-dimensional nature of our problem means this does not make sense. These are defined using orientations of determinant line bundles over the space of connections on a 4-manifold bounding
(Y,w,\alpha)
.

Given a choice of elements of both
\Lambda(\alpha),\Lambda(\beta)
on
(Y,w)
, this induces what is called a
fiber-orientation
on the moduli space
M_{\zeta}(Y;\alpha,\beta)
: an orientation of the fibers of the map
M_{\zeta}(Y;\alpha,\beta)\to\alpha
, which negates if we change either the element of
\Lambda(\alpha)
or the element of
\Lambda(\beta)
. This choice is such that whenever we are given a choice of elements of
\Lambda(\alpha),\Lambda(\beta)
, and
\Lambda(\gamma)
the gluing map

M_{\zeta}(Y;\alpha,\beta)\times_{\beta}M_{\zeta^{\prime}}(Y;\beta,\gamma)\supset U\to M_{\zeta*\zeta^{\prime}}(Y;\alpha,\gamma)

is a fiber-orientation preserving diffeomorphism from an open subset of the domain. Notice that here we do not reduce by the
\mathbb{R}
-action. Our convention is that
\breve{M}_{\zeta}
is given a fiber-orientation so that

\mathbb{R}\times\breve{M}_{\zeta}^{\text{Fiber}}\cong M^{\text{Fiber}}_{\zeta}

is a fiber-orientation-preserving diffeomorphism.

If
(W,c):(Y,w)\to(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime})
is a cobordism equipped with a homology orientation and a chosen element of
\Lambda(\alpha)
and
\Lambda(\alpha^{\prime})
, then the moduli spaces
M_{z}(W;\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})
are all equipped with fiber-orientations; these fiber-orientations are negated if you swap any one of the given choices of element. These fiber-orientations have the property that given further choices of element
\Lambda(\beta)
and
\Lambda(\beta^{\prime})
, the maps

\displaystyle M_{\zeta}(Y,w;\beta,\alpha)\times_{\alpha}

\displaystyle M_{z}(W,c;\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})\supset U\to M_{\zeta*z}(W,c;\beta,\alpha^{\prime}),

\displaystyle M_{z}(W,c;\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})\times_{\alpha^{\prime}}

\displaystyle M_{\zeta^{\prime}}(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime};\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})\supset U\to M_{z*\zeta^{\prime}}(W,c;\alpha,\beta^{\prime})

are fiber-orientation preserving diffeomorphisms.

In the next section, we will find it somewhat more convenient to recast the notion of fiber-orientation; a fiber-orientation is the same as an orientation of
M_{\zeta}(Y;\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})
which depends on a choice of orientation of
\alpha
, and which negates if we negate the orientation on
\alpha
. In this language, our convention is that all moduli spaces are oriented as
\alpha\times M_{\zeta}^{\text{Fiber}}
. In particular, our convention about reduction by the
\mathbb{R}
-action has us orient the reduced moduli spaces
\breve{M}_{\zeta}
so that there is an orientation-preserving open embedding from an open subset

\alpha\times\mathbb{R}\times\breve{M}_{\zeta}^{\text{Fiber}}\supset U\to\alpha\times M_{\zeta}^{\text{Fiber}}.

When determining the orientation of boundary components of
\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta}(Y;\alpha,\beta)
below, this convention introduces an additional sign of
(-1)^{\dim\alpha}
, corresponding to commuting the factor of
\mathbb{R}
past the factor of
\alpha
.

Equipped with orientations, we may explicitly give boundary relations for all of the moduli spaces relevant to us, including their natural signs. Here ‘stratified diffeomorphism’ means an order-preserving bijection between the posets of strata and a homeomorphism between the spaces which covers said bijection and which is a diffeomorphism on each stratum; ‘orientation-preserving’ means that it is an oriented diffeomorphism on the top strata. When
M
is a stratified-smooth manifold, the closure
\overline{M_{s}}
of each face
M_{s}
is again a stratified-smooth manifold; the expression
\partial M
means the disjoint union over
\overline{M_{s}}
, where
M_{s}
is a codimension-1 stratum equipped with the boundary orientation.

The following proposition determines the induced orientations on the boundary components of the moduli spaces that we introduced earlier. It should be understood that all moduli spaces are taken so that
i(z)
is in the range where these are stratified-smooth manifolds (less than or equal to
8,7,6
respectively in the three cases), and the sums are taken over those moduli spaces in homotopy classes which concatenate to
z
.

Proposition 3.35
.

Suppose
(Y,w)
and
(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime})
are weakly admissible pairs with regular perturbations
\pi
and
\pi^{\prime}
. For each
\alpha\in{\mathfrak{C}}_{\pi}(Y,w)
, suppose an element of
\Lambda(\alpha)
and an orientation for the orbit
\alpha
is given.
For each
\alpha^{\prime}\in{\mathfrak{C}}_{\pi}(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime})
, pick an element of
\Lambda(\alpha^{\prime})
. Suppose
(W,c):(Y,w)\to(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime})
is an unobstructed cobordism.

(i)

Given
\alpha,\beta,\gamma\in{\mathfrak{C}}_{\pi}(Y,w)
and paths
\zeta_{0}
from
\alpha
to
\beta
and
\zeta_{1}
from
\beta
to
\delta
with
\zeta=\zeta_{0}*\zeta_{1}
,
the boundary orientation and the fiber product orientation on the boundary component

\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta_{0}}(Y,w;\alpha,\beta)\times_{\beta}\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta_{1}}(Y,w;\beta,\delta)

of
\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta}(Y,w;\alpha,\delta)
are related by
(-1)^{\widetilde{i}(\zeta_{0})+\dim(\alpha)}
.

(ii)

Suppose a regular perturbation
\pi_{W}
for the ASD equation on
(W,c)
is fixed.
Given
\alpha,\beta\in{\mathfrak{C}}_{\pi}(Y,w)
,
\alpha^{\prime}\in{\mathfrak{C}}_{\pi}(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime})
and paths
\zeta_{0}
from
\alpha
to
\beta
and
z_{1}
from
\beta
to
\alpha^{\prime}
(along
(W,c)
) with
z=\zeta_{0}*z_{1}
,
the boundary orientation and the fiber product orientation on the boundary component

\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta_{0}}(Y,w;\alpha,\beta)\times_{\beta}M^{+}_{z_{1}}(W,c;\beta,\alpha^{\prime})

of
M^{+}_{z}(W,c;\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})
are related by
(-1)^{\dim(\alpha)}
. Similarly, given
\alpha\in{\mathfrak{C}}_{\pi}(Y,w)
,
\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}\in{\mathfrak{C}}_{\pi}(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime})
and paths
z_{0}
from
\alpha
to
\beta^{\prime}
(along
(W,c)
) and
\zeta_{1}^{\prime}
from
\beta^{\prime}
to

\alpha^{\prime}
with
z=z_{0}*\zeta_{1}^{\prime}
, then the boundary orientation and the fiber product orientation on the boundary component

M^{+}_{z_{0}}(W,c;\alpha,\beta^{\prime})\times_{\beta^{\prime}}\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta_{1}^{\prime}}(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime};\beta^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})

(3.36)

of
M^{+}_{z}(W,c;\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})
are related by
(-1)^{\widetilde{i}(z_{0})+\dim(\alpha)+1}
. Note that an orientation of
\beta^{\prime}
is necessary to define the orientation of
M^{+}_{\zeta_{1}^{\prime}}(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime};\beta^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})
and to define
the fiber product orientation. However, the resulting orientation on (
3.36
) is independent of the orientation of
\beta^{\prime}
.

(iii)

Suppose a regular 1-parameter family of perturbations
\{\pi_{W}(t)\}_{t\in I}
with
I=[0,1]
are chosen and
M^{+}_{z}(W,c,I;\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})
is the corresponding family moduli space.
Then
M^{+}_{z}(W,c,\pi(0);\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})
and
M^{+}_{z}(W,c,\pi(1);\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})
give two boundary components of the family moduli space whose orientations respectively differs from the boundary orientation by a factor of
(-1)^{\dim\alpha-1}
and
(-1)^{\dim\alpha}
.
Given
\alpha,\beta\in{\mathfrak{C}}_{\pi}(Y,w)
,
\alpha^{\prime}\in{\mathfrak{C}}_{\pi}(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime})
and paths
\zeta_{0}
from
\alpha
to
\beta
and
z_{1}
from
\beta
to
\alpha^{\prime}
with
z=\zeta_{0}*z_{1}
,
the boundary orientation and the fiber product orientation on the boundary component

\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta_{0}}(Y,w;\alpha,\beta)\times_{\beta}M^{+}_{z_{1}}(W,c,I;\beta,\alpha^{\prime})

of
M^{+}_{z}(W,c,I;\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})
are related by
(-1)^{\widetilde{i}(\zeta_{0})+\dim(\alpha)}
. Similarly, given
\alpha\in{\mathfrak{C}}_{\pi}(Y,w)
,
\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}\in{\mathfrak{C}}_{\pi}(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime})
and paths
z_{0}
from
\alpha
to
\beta^{\prime}
and
\zeta^{\prime}_{1}
from

\beta^{\prime}
to
\alpha^{\prime}
with
z=z_{0}*\zeta_{1}^{\prime}
, then the boundary orientation and the fiber product orientation on the boundary component

M^{+}_{z_{0}}(W,c,I;\alpha,\beta^{\prime})\times_{\beta^{\prime}}\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta_{1}^{\prime}}(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime};\beta^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})

(3.37)

of
M^{+}_{z}(W,c;\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})
are related by
(-1)^{\widetilde{i}(z_{0})+\dim(\alpha)}
.

Remark 3.38
.

If one picks a complex line bundle
\eta
and a base connection
A
on it, then replacing the bundle
E_{c}
with
E_{c}\otimes\eta
, the map
A^{\text{det}}\mapsto A^{\text{det}}+A
identifies the corresponding moduli spaces and all of their extra structure,
except for orientations
. If
\eta
is trivial on the ends of
W
, the orientations of our moduli spaces change by a uniform sign of
(-1)^{c_{1}^{2}(\eta)}
; this may be seen by reducing to the Kähler case using excision. See
[
19
, Section 4.2]
for more details. It follows that up to sign, the induced map only depends on the associated
SO(3)
-bundle
E_{c}
and not the geometric representative
c
.
∎

4
Geometric chains and instanton homology

4.1
Stratified-smooth spaces and the geometric chain complex

The language we use to describe instanton homology below is the language of
geometric chains
. A theory of geometric chains is a flexible machine which, for each smooth manifold
M
, outputs a chain complex
C_{*}^{gm}(M)
whose homology groups are naturally isomorphic to singular homology. This chain complex is freely generated by
probing maps
: smooth maps
\phi:P\to M
so that
P
is a space of a certain pre-determined type, modulo certain pre-determined relations. Given a smooth map
f:M\to N
, we have an induced map
C_{*}^{gm}(M)\to C_{*}^{gm}(N)
, given at the level of generators by composition; at the level of homology, this agrees with the induced map in singular homology. Finally, given a correspondence
M\leftarrow W\to N
between closed smooth manifolds with
r:W\to M
a submersion, we can define chain-level
fiber-product maps

\times_{M}W:C_{*}^{gm}(M)\to C_{*+\dim W-\dim M}^{gm}(N).

The differential of our instanton chain complex will include both the boundary operator on geometric chains as well as fiber-products with certain moduli spaces. Thus having some form of chain-level fiber product maps is absolutely crucial.

The flexibility to choose the class of probing spaces
P
and the relations satisfied by probing maps mean that it is easy to define a great many seemingly different theories of geometric chains. Already in
[
13
,
23
,
22
,
25
]
, there are four different theories of geometric chains, which are all canonically isomorphic to singular homology. We will need another, for a technical reason outlined below.

In Appendix
A
, we construct the particular class of probing spaces and equivalence relations used in this paper, called
compact oriented stratified-smooth spaces modulo collapse-equivalence
. In this section, we outline the salient features of our relevant geometric homology theory. The stated facts below are proven in the appendices. First, we’ll define stratified-smooth spaces, our generalization of the notion of stratified-smooth manifolds from Definition
3.4
.

Definition 4.1
.

A naive stratified-smooth space of dimension
n
consists of the following data:

(i)

A partially ordered set
\Delta
with an order-preserving grading function
d:\Delta\to\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
with the maximum value
n
,

(ii)

A topological space
P
stratified by subsets
P_{s}
for
s\in\Delta
, with
\overline{P_{s}}=\bigcup_{t<s}P_{t}
,

(iii)

A smooth structure on each stratum
P_{s}
.

An
oriented
naive stratified-smooth space is the additional data of an orientation on all top-dimensional strata.
∎

When
P
is compact, as all examples of interest to us will be, the set
\Delta
is necessarily finite. Given a continuous map
\psi:[0,\varepsilon)^{n-d(s)}\times\mathbb{R}^{d(s)+\ell}\to\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
which, on each stratum, is smooth and has zero as a regular value, the zero set
Z(\psi)=\psi^{-1}(0)
is naturally a naive stratified-smooth space.

Definition 4.2
.

A stratified smooth space is a naive stratified-smooth space so that each point
p\in P
has a neighborhood which is
locally isomorphic
to a naive stratified-smooth space of the form
Z(\psi)
.

We say this stratified-smooth space is oriented if it is also equipped with an orientation on the top strata so that these local isomorphisms can be chosen to be orientation-preserving.
∎

The notion of ‘locally isomorphic’ is intricate, and includes the data of the stratification. The interested reader can find a significantly more detailed discussion, as well as some examples and non-examples, in Appendix
A.1
. Orientations are discussed in Appendix
A.2
. Because these are mostly a
technical necessity
, we do not go into further detail here. The moduli spaces of instantons
\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta}(\alpha,\beta)
are always stratified-smooth
manifolds
, and the first time stratified-smooth spaces make an appearance geometrically is in Section
5.2
. For now, they are only used to define a geometric chain complex whose generators include stratified-smooth manifolds, and there is little harm in imagining all ‘stratified-smooth spaces’ below are smooth manifolds with corners.

We shall define a
geometric chain complex
using stratified-smooth maps
\phi:P\to M
from compact, connected, oriented stratified-smooth spaces as our probes into
M
, much the same as simplicial homology is constructed by probing
M
with continuous maps from simplices. We call such a pair
(P,\phi)
a probe (in
M
). We will use the term
stratified-smooth chain (in
M
)
for a formal linear combination of probes.

When
P
is a stratified-smooth space, we will verify in Lemma
A.1
that the closure
\overline{P_{s}}
of each stratum is also stratified-smooth. Then the boundary of a stratified-smooth probe is given as

\partial P=\sum_{d(s)=\dim P-1}\overline{P_{s}},

where each boundary stratum is given an appropriate boundary orientation. This is a finite sum by compactness of
P
.

While the detailed foundations of stratified-smooth spaces and the geometric chain complex are carried out in the appendix, the following key points are all that is needed for the main text:

•

If two probes
\phi:P\to M
and
\eta:Q\to M
are stratum-preserving diffeomorphic by an orientation-preserving diffeomorphism
f:P\to Q
with
\eta f=\phi
,
then we set
\phi=\eta
as elements of the geometric chain complex
C_{*}^{gm}(M)
.

•

If a probe
\phi:P\to M
supports an orientation-
reversing
diffeomorphism
f:P\to P
with
\phi f=\phi
, then we set
\phi=0

as an element of the geometric chain complex
C_{*}^{gm}(M)
.

•

If a probe
\phi:P\to M
has
\dim P>0
and

\text{rank}(d_{x}\phi_{s}:T_{x}P_{s}\to T_{\phi(x)}M)<\text{dim}(P_{s})

for all strata with
\dim P_{s}>0
and all
x\in P_{s}
, we say
\phi
is degenerate, and we set
\phi=0
as an element of
C_{*}^{gm}(M)
.

•

The most interesting relation, novel to this article and crucial in our definition of the proof of invariance of instanton homology, is
collapse-equivalence
. Let
(P,\phi)
and
(Q,\eta)
be probes. Suppose that there are loci
Z_{P}\subset P
and
Z_{Q}\subset Q
which are unions of embedded stratified-smooth subspaces of positive codimension, and
degenerate
boundary strata of
P
and
Q
, and suppose that there exists an orientation-preserving isomorphism
f:P\setminus Z_{P}\to Q\setminus Z_{Q}
with
\eta f=\phi
.

Then we say
(Q,\eta)
is
collapse-equivalent
to
(P,\phi)
and set them equal in
C_{*}^{gm}(M)
.

•

We use a truncation procedure to ignore high-dimensional chains, so that
C_{*}^{gm}(M)
is concentrated in degrees
[0,\dim M]
; in degrees
d<\dim M
, this is simply
C_{*}^{gm}(M)
, while in degree
d=\dim M
, this is the quotient by the subgroup
\partial C_{\dim M+1}^{gm}
.

•

Given smooth manifolds
M
and
N
, a probe
(f,g):W\to M\times N
is called an
oriented correspondence from
M
to
N
. If
f
is a submersion and
M
is oriented, then the assignment

(P,\phi)\mapsto(P\times_{M}W,g\pi_{2})

gives a map
\times_{M}W:C^{gm}_{*}(M)\to C^{gm}_{*+\dim W-\dim M}(N)
which satisfies the relation

\partial(\phi\times_{M}W)=(\partial\phi)\times_{M}W+(-1)^{\dim P+\dim M}\phi\times_{M}(\partial W).

If either one of the orientation on
M
or
W
is negated, then the induced map
\times_{M}W
is negated. Given a pair of correspondences
V\to L\times M
and
W\to M\times N
(with
V\to L
and
W\to M
submersions), the composite
(\phi\times_{L}V)\times_{M}W
is equal to
\phi\times_{L}(V\times_{M}W)
. That is, the fiber product operation is associative.

•

If
\phi:W\to M\times N
is zero as an element of
C_{*}^{gm}(M\times N)
, then
\phi\times_{M}W=0
identically on the chain level. In particular, if
\dim W>\dim M+\dim N
, then
\phi\times_{M}W=0
, and if
W
is collapse-equivalent to
W^{\prime}
, then
\phi\times_{M}W=\phi\times_{M}W^{\prime}
. It also follows that if
V
and
W
are composable correspondences (given by geometric chains in
L\times M
and
M\times N
, respectively) so that

\dim V+\dim W-\dim M>\dim L+\dim N,

then
\phi\times_{L}(V\times_{M}W)
is the zero operator at the chain level.

Example 4.3
.

The most important example of collapse-equivalence for our applications is obtained by real blowup, discussed formally in Appendix
A.3
. If
\psi:P\to E
is a stratified-smooth section of a vector bundle which
is transverse to the zero section, one may take the real blowup
B(\psi)
of this section, replacing the zero locus with a sphere bundle over said zero locus. There is a natural map
B(\psi)\to P
which is a collapse-equivalence.
This will play an important role in Section
5
of this article, and we introduce collapse-equivalence so that we can consider a chain equal to its blowup.
∎

In Appendix
A.4
, we verify that stratified-smooth spaces satisfy all the necessary requirements to give a suitable theory of geometric chains, canonically isomorphic to singular homology. We use the corresponding geometric chain complex freely below. In trying to understand the arguments of this article, it is almost entirely harmless to imagine that ‘stratified-smooth space’ may be read ‘smooth manifold with corners’. There is a perfectly good notion of collapse-equivalence for smooth manifolds with corners, for instance. In Appendix
A.5
we add the truncation mentioned above.

Henceforth when
M
is a smooth manifold, we suppress
gm
from notation and write
C_{*}(M)
for
tC_{*}^{gm}(M)
, the truncated geometric chains of Appendix
A.5
.

Remark 4.4
.

It seems plausible that with significant analytic work, we could prove that the instanton moduli spaces (and the modifications we discuss later) can be given the structure of smooth manifolds with corners. Assuming this, the theory of stratified-smooth chains would be wholly unnecessary, and we would be able to carry out our constructions with a more standard notion of geometric chains (like smooth manifolds with corners). However, all that we’ve actually proved is that these moduli spaces carry the structure of stratified-smooth manifolds. If we do not want to attempt to construct smooth atlases for the instanton moduli spaces, the development of the stratified-smooth theory is then forced upon us by our need for transverse intersections. This is a tradeoff, but we believe that in this case accepting stratified-smooth chains is the path of least resistance.
∎

4.2
Flow categories and bimodules

With a suitable notion of geometric chains in hand, we will now define
flow categories and bimodules of geometric chains
. These are almost precisely the data rising from the moduli spaces of flowlines of a Morse–Bott function, though we make the drastic assumption that all endpoint maps are submersions. This is not necessary, but simplifies discussions of orientation and of the domains of various maps; it is also necessarily true in all of our cases of interest (which arise from
G
-equivariant Morse theory, where the critical submanifolds are orbits and endpoint maps are equivariant).

A flow category is precisely what we need to construct a Morse-Bott type complex in geometric chains.

Definition 4.5
.

A finitely-graded flow category
\mathcal{C}
in geometric chains is the following data, subject to the following restrictions:

•

A finite set of objects
\mathsf{Ob}(\mathcal{C})
, each equipped with the structure of a closed, connected, orientable smooth manifold. (Orientability is not strictly necessary, and is completely irrelevant over
\mathbb{F}_{2}
, but makes our discussion of orientations on the moduli spaces simpler.)

•

For each object
X\in\mathsf{Ob}(\mathcal{C})
a 2-element set
\Lambda(X)
called the
orientation set
.

•

A grading function
\widetilde{i}:\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C}\to\mathbb{Z}/2N
so that
\widetilde{i}(X,Y)+\widetilde{i}(Y,Z)=\widetilde{i}(X,Z)
; when multiple flow categories are under consideration we will write
\widetilde{i}_{\mathcal{C}}
for this function. This is called the ‘fiber-dimension’, or ‘fiber-index’.

•

For each pair
X,Y
of objects, for each orientation of
X
, and for each element of
\Lambda(X)
and
\Lambda(Y)
, we have a geometric chain, written as

\mathcal{C}(X,Y)=\mathcal{C}_{XY}\in C_{\dim X+\widetilde{i}(X,Y)-1}(X\times Y),

so that the map
\mathcal{C}(X,Y)\to X
is a submersion. We require that if we reverse any one of the orientation on
X
, the element of
\Lambda(X)
, or the element of
\Lambda(Y)
, the resulting geometric chain is the previous geometric chain with its orientation reversed.

•

Given a sequence
X,Y,Z
of objects, we demand that we have equality

(-1)^{\dim X}\partial\mathcal{C}_{XY}=\sum_{Z\in\mathsf{Ob}(\mathcal{C})}(-1)^{\widetilde{i}(X,Z)}\mathcal{C}_{XZ}\times_{Z}\mathcal{C}_{ZY}

as elements of
C_{*}(X\times Y)
.∎

Let us try to clarify a few points about this definition.

In Morse-Bott theory, to orient the moduli spaces of flowlines from
X
to
Y
, one needs to orient a number of things:
X
, the unstable bundle of
X
, and the stable bundle of
Y
. In the second bullet point, the sets
\Lambda(X)
should be understood as analogous to the set of orientations on the stable bundle of
X
. While these are not canonical, once one makes a choice for each
X
(and orients each
X
) we obtain induced orientations on all moduli spaces of flowlines, with predictable change as we change any of our choices.

By the orientation assumption, and the way fiber product maps
\times_{X}\mathcal{C}_{XY}
behave under orientation-reversal of
X
, one obtains a well-defined map

\times_{X}\mathcal{C}_{XY}:C_{*}(X)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}/2}\mathbb{Z}[\Lambda(X)]\to C_{*}(Y)[\widetilde{i}(X,Y)-1]\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}/2}\mathbb{Z}[\Lambda(Y)],

where
\mathbb{Z}/2
acts on the complex
C_{*}(X)
and
C_{*}(Y)
by negation/orientation-reversal and on
\Lambda(X)
and
\Lambda(Y)
by swapping the two elements; this map is independent of the orientation on
X
and does not require any choice of an element of
\Lambda(X)
or
\Lambda(Y)
.
8
8

8

Equivalently but less canonically, one may suppress the sets
\Lambda(X)
by choosing an element of each from the getgo.

In the fourth bullet point, the index should be read as an integer mod
2N
, so that
\mathcal{C}(X,Y)
is an element of

\bigoplus_{k\equiv_{2N}\dim X+\widetilde{i}(X,Y)-1}C_{k}(X\times Y),

noting that this group vanishes when
k>\dim X+\dim Y
so there are only finitely many nonzero terms in this sum. Here and in what follows
\equiv_{2N}
is shorthand for ‘equal modulo
2N
’.

Remark 4.6
.

This definition is a weakening of the standard definition
[
37
, Definition 2.8]
of flow category in three ways. First, we only pay attention to these moduli spaces
\mathcal{C}_{XY}
up to a certain dimension,
\dim X+\dim Y
. Second, when looking at the fiber product
\mathcal{C}_{XZ}\times_{Z}\mathcal{C}_{ZY}
, we ignore all fiber product chains larger than
\dim X+\dim Y
. Third, usually one demands that the term
\bigsqcup_{Z}\mathcal{C}_{XZ}\times_{Z}\mathcal{C}_{ZY}
is oriented diffeomorphic to
\partial\mathcal{C}_{XZ}
. Here, we only ask that they are equal as geometric chains; this means that if one is diffeomorphic to a
collapse
of the other, or differs from another by a degenerate chain, they still give the same element of the geometric chain complex. On the other hand, the submersive condition is more strict than Zhou’s definition. If desired, it is not hard to remove this assumption in what follows, but all flow categories arising from
G
-invariant Morse theory (for compact Lie group
G
) satisfy this condition.
∎

The point of Definition
4.2
is that, using the fiber product constructions above with an appropriate theory of geometric chains, a flow category gives rise to a chain complex, the ‘flow complex’.

Definition 4.7
.

Suppose one has a finitely-graded flow category
\mathcal{C}
in geometric chains. There is an associated
flow complex

CM_{*}(\mathcal{C})
which comes equipped with a relative
\mathbb{Z}/2N
-grading

CM_{*}(\mathcal{C})=\bigoplus_{X\in\mathsf{Ob}(\mathcal{C})}C_{*}(X)[\widetilde{i}(X)]\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}/2}\mathbb{Z}[\Lambda(X)],

where the shift denotes that if
(P,\phi)\in C_{*}(X)
and
(Q,\eta)\in C_{*}(Y)
then their relative grading is

\text{gr}[(P,\phi),(Q,\eta)]=\dim P-\dim Q+\widetilde{i}(X,Y).

One imagines that
\widetilde{i}(X,Y)=\widetilde{i}(X)-\widetilde{i}(Y)
for some illusory function
\widetilde{i}(X)
.

The boundary operator on
CM_{*}(\mathcal{C})
is
d:=\partial+d_{M}
, where
\partial
is the boundary operator on geometric chains and

d_{M}\phi=\sum_{Y}(-1)^{\dim\phi}\phi\times_{X}\mathcal{C}_{XY}.\qed

When
\mathcal{C}
arises as the flow category of a Morse-Bott-Smale function on a Riemannian closed smooth manifold, this complex should be called the Morse-Bott complex, and its homology recovers the homology of said manifold. The idea for this complex originates from a differential forms version used to develop equivariant cohomology in
[
1
]
. Not long after, Fukaya
[
13
]
used a variant defined over the integers to discuss Morse-Bott homology and develop a connected-sum theorem for instanton homology; our version here resembles Fukaya’s, who also discussed geometric chains. The notation
CM_{*}(\mathcal{C})
is chosen to reflect the Morse-theoretic origin of this complex.

Proposition 4.8
.

The above construction defines a chain complex.

Proof.

We will suppress the sets
\Lambda(X)
from the proof below by, for instance, picking an element of each from the start.

Observe that the differential has relative grading
-1
: the geometric boundary operator
\partial
has degree
-1
, and because
\times_{X}\mathcal{C}_{XY}:C_{*}(X)\to C_{*}(Y)
has degree
\widetilde{i}(X,Y)-1
, it has degree
-1
when considered as a map

C_{*}(X)[\widetilde{i}(X)]\to C_{*}(Y)[\widetilde{i}(Y)],

so that
d_{M}
has degree
-1
.

Next we should verify that the differential squares to zero. We have

\displaystyle d^{2}\phi=\partial^{2}\phi+

\displaystyle\sum_{Y}(-1)^{\dim\phi}\partial\mathopen{}\left(\phi\times_{X}\mathcal{C}_{XY}\right)\mathclose{}+(-1)^{\dim\phi-1}\partial\phi\times_{X}\mathcal{C}_{XY}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\sum_{Y,Z}(-1)^{\dim\phi+\widetilde{i}(X,Z)-1}(-1)^{\dim\phi}\phi\times_{X}\mathcal{C}_{XZ}\times_{Z}\mathcal{C}_{ZY}.

The first term is zero, and the sign in the last term can be simplified to
\sum(-1)^{\widetilde{i}(X,Z)-1}
. Using the formula for the boundary of a fiber product boundary relation in the definition of flow category, the second term may be expanded out as

\displaystyle(-1)^{\dim\phi}

\displaystyle\left(\partial\phi\times_{X}\mathcal{C}_{XY}+(-1)^{\dim\phi+\dim X}\phi\times_{X}\partial\mathcal{C}_{XY}\right)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\dim\phi}\partial\phi\times_{X}\mathcal{C}_{XY}+\sum_{Z}(-1)^{\widetilde{i}(X,Z)}\phi\times_{X}\mathcal{C}_{XZ}\times_{Z}\mathcal{C}_{ZY},

using our formulas for the boundary of a fiber product and for the boundary of
\mathcal{C}_{XY}
. Thus the second term of the sum is precisely the negative of the third plus fourth terms, and therefore
d^{2}\phi=0
.
∎

Remark 4.9
.

When we work with
\mathbb{Z}
-graded flow categories, the filtration by index
\widetilde{i}
gives rise to a spectral sequence computing
HM_{*}(\mathcal{C})
. In the
{\bf Z}/2N
-graded case, the discussion is slightly more subtle.

Again we suppress
\Lambda(X)
. Choose (arbitrarily) a lift of the relative grading to an absolute grading
\widetilde{i}(X)
. This gives us a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded lift

\widetilde{CM}_{*}(\mathcal{C})=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}X\in\mathsf{Ob}(\mathcal{C})\\
n\equiv_{2N}\widetilde{i}(X)\end{subarray}}C_{*}(X)[n],

with the appropriate differential.

We may equip
\widetilde{CM}_{*}(\mathcal{C})
with a
periodic filtration
: a filtration so that

F_{k+2N}\widetilde{CM}_{*+2N}=F_{k}\widetilde{CM}_{*}

This filtration is obtained by taking the sum only over those components with
n\leq k
. Because
\mathcal{C}_{XY}\to X
is a submersion,
\dim\mathcal{C}_{XY}\geq\dim X
for all
X,Y
, so that every nonzero term in the above sum has
\widetilde{i}_{\mathcal{C}}(X,Y)\geq 1
. It follows that the differential preserves the filtration, and the associated graded differential is the geometric boundary operator (with no fiber product terms).
9
9

9

If we drop the submersive assumption, this spectral sequence fails to exist unless
\dim\mathcal{C}_{XY}\geq\dim X-1
for all
X,Y
, and the
E^{1}
page has a twisted differential unless
\dim\mathcal{C}_{XY}\geq\dim X
for all
X,Y
.

As discussed in
[
25
, Section A.8]
, this gives rise to a
\mathbb{Z}/2N\times\mathbb{Z}
-graded (relatively graded in the first coordinate, absolutely graded in the second) spectral sequence
(E^{r},d^{r})\Rightarrow HM^{*}(\mathcal{C})
with

E^{1}\cong\bigoplus_{X\in\mathsf{Ob}(\mathcal{C})}H_{*}(X).

The differential on the
E^{1}
page is given by fiber product with those moduli space components
\mathcal{C}_{XY}
of dimension
\dim X
(that is, where
\widetilde{i}_{\mathcal{C}}(X,Y)=1
, so that
\text{Fiber}(\mathcal{C}_{XY}\to X
) is a finite set). This filtration is degreewise finite, so the spectral sequence is strongly convergent, meaning that its
E^{\infty}
page is the associated graded group of the periodic filtration on
HM_{*}(\mathcal{C})
.
∎

In practice, one constructs finitely-graded flow categories by providing actual stratified-smooth spaces (not their representatives in the chain complex, which involves a quotient by additional relations), and only doing so through a given dimension range. This use of a dimension range is precisely why we truncate our complex of geometric chains, so that the following lemma holds.

Lemma 4.10
.

Suppose one has a set of objects
X
, a grading function
\widetilde{i}
, and a collection of compact oriented stratified-smooth spaces
\mathcal{C}_{XY}
, satisfying the first four bullet points of Definition
4.2
and so that the boundary relation holds when
\dim\mathcal{C}_{XY}\leq\dim X+\dim Y+1
. Then the construction of Proposition
4.2
still defines a chain complex.

Proof.

We suppress signs from this discussion, and write
\mathcal{C}_{XZ,k}
to refer to the components of dimension
k
. The proof that
d^{2}=0
amounts to showing that for all
X,Z
, the following two maps
C_{*}(X)\to C_{*+k-\dim X}(Z)
coincide: the first given by fiber product with
\partial\mathcal{C}_{XZ,k}
, and the second given by fiber product with
\sum_{Y,i}\mathcal{C}_{XY,i}\times_{Y}\mathcal{C}_{YZ,k+\dim Y-i}
. When
k-\dim X\leq\dim Z+1
, these coincide by hypothesis. When
k-\dim X>\dim Z+1
, these maps are identically zero, as our model for
C_{*}(Z)
is supported in degrees
[0,\dim Z]
.
∎

In the instanton setting,
\dim X+\dim Y
is at most
6
and
2N=8
, so this means we need those moduli spaces of dimension between
0
and
7
, and the relevant moduli spaces have constant dimension.

Just as flow categories give rise to chain complexes, it is natural to imagine that a similar structure gives rise to chain maps between flow complexes. This leads us to the following structure, which form the morphisms in a category
\mathsf{sFlow}
(the ‘s’ for ‘submersive’).

Definition 4.11
.

Suppose
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
are both finitely graded flow categories in geometric chains. A bimodule
\mathcal{M}
between these, written
\mathcal{M}:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
, is the following data, subject to the following restrictions.

•

We are given a function
\widetilde{i}_{\mathcal{M}}:\mathsf{Ob}(\mathcal{C})\times\mathsf{Ob}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime})\to\mathbb{Z}/2N
, which is affine in the sense that given
X,Z\in\mathcal{C}
and
Y,W\in\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
we have

\widetilde{i}_{\mathcal{C}}(X,Z)+\widetilde{i}_{\mathcal{M}}(Z,W)+\widetilde{i}_{\mathcal{C}^{\prime}}(W,Y)=\widetilde{i}_{\mathcal{M}}(X,Y).

•

We also have, for each
(X,Y)\in\mathsf{Ob}(\mathcal{C})\times\mathsf{Ob}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime})
and each orientation on
X
and choice of element of
\Lambda(X)
and
\Lambda(Y)
, a geometric chain

\mathcal{M}(X,Y)=\mathcal{M}_{XY}\in C_{\dim X+\widetilde{i}_{\mathcal{M}}(X,Y)}(X\times Y).

We demand that
\mathcal{M}_{XY}\to X
is a submersion for all
X,Y
. Once again we demand that the orientation of
\mathcal{M}_{XY}
should reverse if we reverse any one of the orientation of
X
, the element of
\Lambda(X)
, or the element of
\Lambda(Y)
.

•

We have an equality at the level of geometric chains in
X\times Y

(-1)^{\dim X}\partial\mathcal{M}_{XY}=\sum_{Z\in\mathsf{Ob}(\mathcal{C})}\mathcal{C}_{XZ}\times_{Z}\mathcal{M}_{ZY}+(-1)^{\widetilde{i}_{\mathcal{M}}(X,W)+1}\sum_{W\in\mathsf{Ob}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime})}\mathcal{M}_{XW}\times_{W}\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{WY}.\qed

The fiber-dimension function
\widetilde{i}_{\mathcal{M}}
should be understood here as the analogue of degree of a chain map, in the absence of an absolute grading.

Remark 4.12
.

Given a generic correspondence
M\xleftarrow{f}W\xrightarrow{g}N
between closed oriented smooth manifolds with Morse-Bott functions, there are moduli spaces of points in
W
which, projected to
M
(resp
N
), flow upward (downward) to a given critical submanifold. The collection of such moduli spaces
\mathcal{M}_{W}(X,Y)
form a bimodule in the above sense. In a moment, we will see that a bimodule induces a chain map between the flow complexes. In the Morse-Bott case described here, the induced map on homology is
gf^{!}
, where
f^{!}
is the umkehr map defined using Poincare duality on
M
and
W
. See, for instance,
[
20
, Section 2.8]
for the Morse case.
∎

As expected, a bimodule induces a chain map between the flow complexes.

Proposition 4.13
.

Given a bimodule
\mathcal{M}:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
, there is a chain map

CM_{*}(\mathcal{M}):CM_{*}(\mathcal{C})\to CM_{*}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime})

induced by the map that sends
\phi\in C_{*}(X)
for
X\in\mathsf{Ob}(\mathcal{C})
to

CM_{*}(\mathcal{M})\phi=\sum_{Y\in\mathsf{Ob}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime})}\phi\times_{X}\mathcal{M}_{XY}.

The proof that this is a chain map is a straightforward definition-push akin to the proof of Proposition
4.2
, and we leave it to the reader.

Remark 4.14
.

In terms of the periodic filtration before, the chain map induced by a bimodule is a periodically filtered map; its behavior on relative gradings is described by
i_{\mathcal{M}}
. The induced map on the
E^{1}
page of the spectral sequence is given by the induced map of
\times_{X}\mathcal{M}(X,Y)_{\dim X}
on homology; here the subscript indicates that we only refer to those
\mathcal{M}(X,Y)
of dimension
\dim X
, so that
\mathcal{M}(X,Y)
has fiber-dimension zero.
∎

We have the morphisms in our category of flow categories; for this to actually form a category, we should define identity and composite morphisms, and check the associativity and identity axioms.

Definition 4.15
.

Given a flow category
\mathcal{C}
, we say its
identity bimodule

\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{C}}
has
\widetilde{i}_{\mathbf{1}}(X,Y)=\widetilde{i}(X,Y)
, and

\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{C}}(X,Y)=\begin{cases}0&X\neq Y\\
X&X=Y\end{cases}.

Given two bimodules
\mathcal{M}:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{N}:\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\to\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}
, their composition
\mathcal{N}\circ\mathcal{M}:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime}
is the bimodule with

\widetilde{i}_{\mathcal{N}\circ\mathcal{M}}(X,Z)=\widetilde{i}_{\mathcal{M}}(X,Y)+\widetilde{i}_{\mathcal{N}}(Y,Z)

for any
Y\in\mathsf{Ob}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime})
and

(\mathcal{N}\circ\mathcal{M})(X,Z)=\sum_{Y\in\mathsf{Ob}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime\prime})}\mathcal{M}(X,Y)\times_{Y}\mathcal{N}(Y,Z).\qed

Remark 4.16
.

One gets a slightly different prediction for the identity bimodule from the perspective of Morse theory. There, the identity bimodule should arises as the moduli spaces corresponding to the identity correspondence, or equivalently, the compactified trajectory space of flowlines on
M
(the space of parameterized flowlines, including constant flowlines and broken flowlines). This would assign
\mathbf{1}_{\text{Morse}}(X,X)=X
, while
\mathbf{1}_{\text{Morse}}(X,Y)=[0,1]\times\mathcal{C}(X,Y)
when
X\neq Y
. However, note that
\mathbf{1}_{\text{Morse}}(X,Y)
is degenerate as a chain in
X\times Y
: it factors through the projection
[0,1]\times\mathcal{C}(X,Y)\to\mathcal{C}(X,Y)
, and this chain supports an orientation-reversing isomorphism
(t,\gamma)\mapsto(1-t,\gamma)
, and hence is identically zero on the chain level for
X\neq Y
. So the identity bimodule above agrees with the one predicted by Morse theory, even at the chain level.

The phrasing above avoids discussion of this degenerate chain.
∎

It is straightforward to verify the bimodule relations for the bimodules introduced above; it is easy to see that composition is associative and that the identity bimodule acts as the identity, thus proving the following proposition.

Proposition 4.17
.

Submersive flow categories, with bimodules-up-to-isomorphim as morphisms, form a category
\mathsf{sFlow}
with identity and composition as described above. The identity and composite bimodules act as the identity and composite morphisms on the flow complex.

The sorts of bimodules (and thus chain maps) that arise from Morse–Bott theory depend on auxiliary data, such as metric structures. However, the induced maps on homology are well-defined. The usual argument chooses a 1-parameter family of auxiliary data, and uses this to construct a chain homotopy between the two chain maps. The definition below attempts to abstract this situation to the setting of flow categories.

Definition 4.18
.

Suppose we are given two bimodules
\mathcal{M}_{0},\mathcal{M}_{1}:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
so that
\widetilde{i}_{\mathcal{M}_{0}}=\widetilde{i}_{\mathcal{M}_{1}}
(that is, these two bimodules have the same ‘relative degree’). A homotopy
\mathcal{H}:\mathcal{M}_{0}\Rightarrow\mathcal{M}_{1}
is the data of, for each
(X,Y)\in\mathsf{Ob}(\mathcal{C})\times\mathsf{Ob}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime})
, and each triple of an orientation on
X
and element of
\Lambda(X)
and
\Lambda(Y)
, a geometric chain

\mathcal{H}(X,Y)=\mathcal{H}_{XY}\in C_{\dim X+\widetilde{i}_{\mathcal{M}}(X,Y)+1}(X\times Y)

so that
\mathcal{H}_{XY}\to X
is a submersion for all
X,Y
. As usual we demand that the orientation of
\mathcal{H}_{XY}
should reverse if we reverse any one of the orientation of
X
, the element of
\Lambda(X)
, or the element of
\Lambda(Y)
.

Finally, we demand an equality at the level of geometric chains in
C_{*}(X\times Y)

\displaystyle(-1)^{\dim X}\partial\mathcal{H}(X,Y)=

\displaystyle\;\mathcal{M}_{1}(X,Y)-\mathcal{M}_{0}(X,Y)

\displaystyle+\sum_{Z\in\mathsf{Ob}(\mathcal{C})}(-1)^{\widetilde{i}_{\mathcal{C}}(X,Z)}\mathcal{C}_{XZ}\times_{Z}\mathcal{H}_{ZY}

\displaystyle+\sum_{W\in\mathsf{Ob}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime})}(-1)^{\widetilde{i}_{\mathcal{M}}(X,W)}\mathcal{H}_{XW}\times_{W}\mathcal{C}^{\prime}_{WY}.\qed

Indeed, a straightforward calculation shows that this is precisely what is needed to define a chain homotopy between induced maps of bimodules.

Proposition 4.19
.

A homotopy
\mathcal{H}:\mathcal{M}_{0}\Rightarrow\mathcal{M}_{1}
of bimodules induces a chain homotopy

CM_{*}(\mathcal{H}):CM_{*}(\mathcal{M}_{0})\Rightarrow CM_{*}(\mathcal{M}_{1}).

It follows that the induced map of a bimodule on homology is well-defined up to bimodule homotopy.

The formula for the chain homotopy follows the same line as for the induced map, together with an extra sign factor: for
\phi\in C_{*}(X)
we write
