CM_{*}(\mathcal{H})\phi=\sum_{Y\in\mathsf{Ob}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime})}(-1)^{\dim\phi}\phi\times_{X}\mathcal{H}_{XY}.

If one likes, it follows from formal properties of this notion of homotopy that there is a well-defined homotopy category
\mathsf{hsFlow}
and that taking the flow complex gives a functor

CM_{*}:\mathsf{hsFlow}\to\mathsf{hKom}_{2N}(\mathbb{Z})

to the homotopy category of
\mathbb{Z}/2N
-graded complexes.

Suppose all of the flow categories and bimodules and so on above carry the action of a compact Lie group
G
, acting smoothly on all objects
X
and all moduli spaces
\mathcal{C}_{XY}
so that the correspondence map
\mathcal{C}_{XY}\to X\times Y
is
G
-equivariant. Then
C_{*}(G)
acts on all of the
C_{*}(X)
’s, compatibly with the fiber product maps, so that
CM_{*}(\mathcal{C})
naturally carries the structure of a dg
C_{*}(G)
-module. Further, a bimodule
\mathcal{W}
with a
G
-action compatible with those on the flow categories induces a
C_{*}(G)
-equivariant chain map, and a
G
-equivariant homotopy between bimodules induces a
C_{*}(G)
-equivariant chain homotopy between these maps.

The comment above goes through without change: there is a functor
\mathsf{hsFlow}^{G}\to\mathsf{hKom}^{G}_{2N}(\mathbb{Z})
from the homotopy category of
G
-equivariant submersive flow categories and to the homotopy category of
\mathbb{Z}/2N
-graded dg
C_{*}(G)
-modules.

4.3
The instanton chain complex and unobstructed cobordism maps

Let
(Y,w,\pi,y)
be a weakly admissible pair with regular perturbation
\pi
and basepoint
y\in Y
. Then Proposition
3.7
(i) shows that there is an associated flow category
\mathcal{I}(Y,w,\pi)
, with

•

object set the set of
SO(3)
-orbits of
\pi
-flat connections;

•

orientation sets the
\Lambda(\alpha)
discussed in Section 3.7;

•

relative grading
\widetilde{i}(Y;\alpha,\beta)=\widetilde{i}(\zeta)\mod 8
, where
\zeta:\alpha\to\beta
is any homotopy class of path of framed connections;

•

morphisms the moduli spaces
\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta}(Y;\alpha,\beta)
, where
\zeta:\alpha\to\beta
is the unique homotopy class with
0\leq\widetilde{i}(\zeta)\leq 7
.

As in Lemma
4.2
, when verifying that this defines a flow category, we only need to define those moduli spaces with
\dim\breve{M}(Y;\alpha,\beta)\leq\dim\alpha+\dim\beta+1
. Because
\dim\breve{M}_{\zeta}(Y;\alpha,\beta)=\dim\alpha+\widetilde{i}(\zeta)-1
, so we need those moduli spaces with
\widetilde{i}(\zeta)\leq\dim\beta+2
.

Because the dimension of an
SO(3)
-orbit is at most
3
, we need those moduli spaces with
\widetilde{i}(\zeta)\leq 5
. Similarly, for the moduli spaces
M_{z}(W)
on a cobordism
(W,c)
, we need those moduli spaces
M_{z}(W,c;\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})
with
\widetilde{i}(z)\leq 4
; and for the moduli spaces
M_{z}(W,c,I;\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})
for a 1-parameter family of perturbations on a cobordism, we need those moduli spaces with
\widetilde{i}(z)\leq 3
.

These are promised to us by Propositions
3.4
,
3.4
, and
3.6
respectively, so long as
i(z)\leq 8,7,6
respectively. We have the relation

\widetilde{i}(z)=i(z)+(3-\dim\alpha)\geq i(z),

so that in particular we have well-defined stratified-smooth moduli spaces whenever
\widetilde{i}(z)\leq 8,7,6
, regardless of what
\alpha
is. In all cases, this means our moduli spaces are defined through fiber dimension
7+(3-\dim\alpha)=10-\dim\alpha\geq 7
, which is more than enough for our purposes.

Notice that
\mathcal{I}(Y,w,\pi)
is an
SO(3)
-equivariant flow category, so its flow complex is a dg-module over
C_{*}SO(3)
.

Definition 4.20
.

We define the
tilde complex
to be the associated flow complex

\widetilde{C}_{*}(Y,w,\pi)=CM_{*}\big(\mathcal{I}(Y,w,\pi)\big),

and its structure as a
C_{*}SO(3)
-module.
∎

The rest of Proposition
3.7
shows that unobstructed cobordisms
(W,c):(Y,w,\pi)\to(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime},\pi^{\prime})
with homology orientation give rise to bimodules between the instanton flow categories
\mathcal{I}(Y,w,\pi)
and
\mathcal{I}(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime},\pi^{\prime})
, while 1-parameter families of (possibly broken) metrics and perturbations give rise to homotopies between these bimodules (and, in the case of broken metrics, their composites).

Thus unobstructed cobordisms give equivariant chain maps between the tilde complexes, which are well-defined up to
SO(3)
-equivariant homotopy and compose as expected. We record this as a theorem.

Theorem 4.21
.

If
(W,c,\pi_{W}):(Y,w,\pi)\to(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime},\pi^{\prime})
is an unobstructed cobordism equipped with a homology orientation and a path
\gamma
between the basepoints, then there is an induced
C_{*}SO(3)
-equivariant chain map

\widetilde{C}(W,c,\pi_{W},\gamma):\widetilde{C}_{*}(Y,w,\pi)\to\widetilde{C}_{*}(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime},\pi^{\prime}).

This map is independent of
\pi_{W}
up to equivariant chain homotopy; the induced map of the cobordism
(\mathbb{R}\times Y,p^{*}E,p^{*}\pi)
is the identity, and the composite of two unobstructed cobordism maps is equivariantly chain homotopic to the unobstructed cobordism map of the composite.

Remark 4.22
.

The statement about the cylinder deserves comment, and is related to the situation of Remark
4.2
. The claim follows from the fact that the moduli spaces on the cylinder are either
\breve{M}^{+}(Y,w;\alpha,\beta)\times[0,1]
for
\alpha\neq\beta
— which supports an orientation-reversing isomorphism, hence is zero on the chain level — or the set of constant trajectories
\alpha
when
\alpha=\beta
. This means that
\mathcal{I}(\mathbb{R}\times Y)
is the identity bimodule, hence induces the identity map on the flow complexes.∎

Thus the tilde complex is a sort of functor under cobordism from a certain category of manifolds
(Y,w,\pi,y)
with morphisms the unobstructed cobordisms (with a homology orientation and path, but without a particular choice of perturbation) to a derived category of relatively
\mathbb{Z}/8
-graded
C_{*}SO(3)
-complexes.

Because we need to assume the cobordisms are unobstructed, the theorem above does not even imply that the homology
\widetilde{I}(Y,w,\pi)
is independent of the perturbation
\pi
. If the signature data are equal
\sigma_{\pi}=\sigma_{\pi^{\prime}}
, then the complexes are equivariantly homotopy equivalent, but if
\sigma_{\pi}>\sigma_{\pi^{\prime}}
the cylinder is obstructed and we have not constructed a chain map
\widetilde{C}_{*}(Y,w,\pi)\to\widetilde{C}_{*}(Y,w,\pi^{\prime})
; in fact, we will see in Corollary
7.1
that these complexes cannot possibly be equivariantly homotopy equivalent.

We now move on to remedying this unfortunate situation; our strategy is detailed in Section 2.

5
Obstructed cobordism maps

The primary goal of this section is to prove Theorem
2
.

Convention 5.1
.

Throughout Section 5, we fix weakly admissible pairs
(Y,w)
and
(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime})
with regular perturbations
\pi
and
\pi^{\prime}
, and a nearly unobstructed cobordism
(W,c):(Y,w)\to(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime})
as in the Definition
3.5
. This means that
b_{1}(W)=b^{+}(W)=0
and
(W,c)
supports exactly one obstructed abelian instanton, which is asymptotic to abelian connections on
(Y,w)
and
(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime})
. We call this instanton
\Lambda:\rho\to\rho^{\prime}
. We fix an perturbation
\pi_{W}
of the ASD equation for which all instantons but
\Lambda
are regular and for which the deformation operator of
\Lambda
has trivial kernel, as in Theorem
3.5
. We write
\lambda:\rho\to\rho^{\prime}
for the path of connections over
(W,c)
given by
\Lambda
.
∎

Due to the presence of the obstructed abelian instanton
\Lambda
, we cannot proceed as in subsection
4.3
to define a morphism
\widetilde{C}(W,c,\pi_{W})
: the standard gluing theory does not apply, and the compactified moduli spaces
M^{+}_{z}(W,c;\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})
are not stratified-smooth spaces anymore. In Subsection
5.1
, we use
obstructed
gluing theory to analyze the moduli spaces
M^{+}_{z}(W,c;\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})
in the neighborhood of obstructed (broken) solutions.

In Section
5.2
, we show that
modified moduli spaces

N^{+}_{z}(W,c;\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})
, obtained by deleting a neighborhood of any broken instanton factoring through
\Lambda
and adding a ‘correction term’, are indeed stratified-smooth spaces which satisfy an explicit boundary relation. However, they do not define bimodules between the instanton flow categories
\mathcal{I}
: the boundary relation is not the expected one.

Motivated by this analysis, we define the suspended flow category
\mathcal{S}_{\rho^{\prime}}\mathcal{I}(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime},\pi^{\prime})
associated to
\mathcal{I}(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime},\pi^{\prime})
and
\rho^{\prime}
, and then we show that the modified moduli spaces
N^{+}_{z}(W,c;\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})
give rise to a bimodule from
\mathcal{I}(Y,w,\pi)
to
\mathcal{S}_{\rho^{\prime}}\mathcal{I}(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime},\pi^{\prime})
. The definition of the suspended flow category
\mathcal{S}_{\rho^{\prime}}\mathcal{I}(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime},\pi^{\prime})
and the bimodule associated to
(W,c)
are respectively given in Subsections
5.3
and
5.4
.

With these in hand, in Section
5.5
we apply these to
W=\mathbb{R}\times Y
, with
\pi_{0}
and
\pi_{1}
regular perturbations on
(Y,w)
with adjacent signature data
\sigma_{\pi_{0}}<\sigma_{\pi_{1}}
, in the sense of Example
2
. To show that the natural continuation map
\widetilde{C}(Y,w,\pi_{0})\to\widetilde{C}(Y,w,\pi_{1})
is a quasi-isomorphism, we use the constructions above to lift this to maps to and from from the
suspended complex

S_{\rho}\widetilde{C}(Y,w,\pi_{0})\leftrightarrow\widetilde{C}(Y,w,\pi_{1})
, where the first complex is the flow complex of the suspended flow category
\mathcal{S}_{\rho}\mathcal{I}(Y,w,\pi_{0})
. Using similar ideas, we conclude by showing that these two maps are chain homotopy inverse to one another. Because the continuation map described above factors as

\widetilde{C}(Y,w,\pi_{0})\to S_{\rho}(Y,w,\pi_{0})\to\widetilde{C}(Y,w,\pi_{1}),

and it is easy to verify that the first map is a quasi-isomorphism, this gives the desired result (and with only slightly more work, invariance of instanton homology).

5.1
Obstructed cobordisms and obstructed gluing theory

Before we define the modified moduli spaces, we should understand the local structure of
M^{+}_{z}(W,c;\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})
in a neighborhood of the non-smooth locus of obstructed reducibles.

The moduli space
M^{+}_{z}(W,c;\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})
has three different types of obstructed elements. An
obstructed solution of type I
is a broken solution which belongs to the subspace

\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta}(Y,w;\alpha,\rho)\times_{\rho}\mathcal{O}_{\Lambda}\cong\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta}(Y,w;\alpha,\rho)\times\{\Lambda\}

(5.2)

of a moduli space of the form
M^{+}_{z}(W,c;\alpha,\rho^{\prime})
with
z=\zeta*\lambda
. Similarly, an
obstructed solution of type II
is a broken solution in the subspace

\mathcal{O}_{\Lambda}\times_{\rho^{\prime}}\breve{M}_{\zeta^{\prime}}^{+}(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime};\rho^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})\cong\{\Lambda\}\times\breve{M}_{\zeta^{\prime}}^{+}(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime};\rho^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})

(5.3)

of a moduli space of the form
M^{+}_{z}(W,c;\rho,\alpha^{\prime})
with
z=\lambda*\zeta^{\prime}
. Finally an
obstructed solution of type III
is a broken solution in

\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta}(Y,w;\alpha,\rho)\times_{\rho}\mathcal{O}_{\Lambda}\times_{\rho^{\prime}}\breve{M}_{\zeta^{\prime}}^{+}(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime};\rho^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})

(5.4)

which is regarded as a subspace of
M^{+}_{z}(W,c;\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})
with
z=\zeta*\lambda*\zeta^{\prime}
.

In the complement of these obstructed solutions, the stratified spaces
M^{+}_{z}(W,c;\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})
have the structure of stratified-smooth manifolds (as in Definition
3.4
).

To study the behavior of the moduli space
M^{+}_{z}(W,c;\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})
in a neighborhood of an obstructed solution, we need to introduce a family of complex line bundles which are called
obstruction bundles
. The cokernel of the ASD operator for the elements of
\mathcal{O}_{\Lambda}
define a complex line bundle
\mathcal{H}_{\Lambda}^{+}
over
\mathcal{O}_{\Lambda}
with an
SO(3)
action that lifts the action on
\mathcal{O}_{\Lambda}
. Let
\mathcal{H}_{\alpha,\rho}
be the line bundle over
\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta}(Y,w;\alpha,\rho)\times_{\rho}\mathcal{O}_{\Lambda}
pulled back from
\mathcal{H}_{\Lambda}^{+}
. We call
\mathcal{H}_{\alpha,\rho}
the
obstruction bundle
associated to
\Lambda
. Similarly, we have the complex line bundle
\mathcal{H}_{\rho^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime}}
over the space
\breve{M}_{\zeta^{\prime}}^{+}(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime};\rho^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})
.

The following proposition, which is an instance of obstructed gluing theory, gives a description for a neighborhood of an obstructed solution of type I.

Proposition 5.5
.

Suppose
({\bf A}_{0},\Lambda)
is an obstructed solution of type I in
M^{+}_{z}(W,c;\alpha,\rho^{\prime})

where
{\bf A}_{0}\in\breve{M}_{\zeta}(Y,w;\alpha,\rho)
with
z=\zeta*\lambda
given as above and

i(z)\geq-1
. Then there is an
SO(3)
-invariant neighborhood
U
of
{\bf A}_{0}
in

\breve{M}_{\zeta}(Y,w;\alpha,\rho)
and an
SO(3)
-equivariant section
\psi^{\textup{an}}_{\zeta\Lambda}
of
\pi_{1}^{*}(\mathcal{H}_{\alpha,\rho})
over
U\times(0,\infty]
which vanishes on
U\times\{\infty\}
and is transverse to the zero section over
\mathcal{H}_{\alpha,\rho}\times(0,\infty)
. There is also an
SO(3)
-equivariant open embedding
\Phi_{\zeta\Lambda}:\left(\psi^{\textup{an}}_{\zeta\Lambda}\right)^{-1}(0)\to M^{+}_{z}(W,c;\alpha,\rho^{\prime})
such that
\Phi_{\zeta\Lambda}
maps
U\times\{\infty\}\subset\left(\psi^{\textup{an}}_{\zeta\Lambda}\right)^{-1}(0)
to
U\times\{\Lambda\}\subset M^{+}_{z}(W,c;\alpha,\rho^{\prime})
via the identity map on
U
.

The superscript ‘
an
’ is short for
analytic
, as these sections are defined by the analytic machinery of obstructed gluing theory.

For
i(z)=-1
, the section
\psi^{\text{an}}_{\zeta\Lambda}
is nowhere vanishing and
M^{+}_{z}(W,c;\alpha,\rho^{\prime})
is empty, so there is no content in this case. However, later we need compatibility of the sections
\psi^{\text{an}}_{\zeta\Lambda}
for different
\zeta
and
z
, and it is necessary for us to include the case that
i(z)=-1
.

Sketch of the proof.

This proposition can be verified using standard obstructed gluing theory results for which there is
a large body of literature starting with the seminal work of Taubes in
[
34
]
.
Here we sketch the general steps in the proof and refer the reader to
[
8
, Chapter 7]
(more precisely, the discussion following Theorem 7.2.24 and the discussion in Chapter 7.2.8) as well as

[
7
, Chapter 4.5]

for more details.
The reader should note that the discussion of
[
8
]
is limited to the case that

Y=S^{3}
and the arguments are worked out in a slightly different conformal model
than cylindrical ends. However, working in the setup of
4-manifolds with cylindrical ends in fact simplifies the arguments, and the arguments of

[
8
, Chapter 7]
can be adapted to the case of a weakly admissible pair
(Y,w)
where

{\mathfrak{C}}_{\pi}(Y,w)
is non-degenerate. For the ease of exposition,
we shall also assume that the perturbation terms in the definition of the critical sets
{\mathfrak{C}}_{\pi}(Y,w)
,

{\mathfrak{C}}_{\pi}(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime})

and the moduli spaces of instantons are trivial; it is straightforward to modify the arguments below to handle the general case.

As the first step in the proof, one needs to construct approximate solutions of the ASD equation
by pre-gluing elements of
\breve{M}_{\zeta}(Y,w;\alpha,\rho)
and
\mathcal{O}_{\Lambda}
.
Fix an
SO(3)
-invariant pre-compact and open neighborhood
U
of
{\bf A}_{0}
in

\breve{M}_{\zeta}(Y,w;\alpha,\rho)
. Fix once and for all a connection
\widehat{\rho}
representing the gauge equivalence class of the reducible
\rho
, and similarly a connection
\widehat{\Lambda}
representing the gauge equivalence class of obstructed reducible connection on
W
. Fix positive real numbers
T_{0}
and
\varepsilon_{0}
which are respectively
required to be large and small. For any
SO(3)
-orbit
{\bf A}
in
U
, we may find a representative connection

\widehat{{\bf A}}
such that
\widehat{{\bf A}}
is in temporal gauge,
\widehat{{\bf A}}
is asymptotic to
\hat{\rho}

and

\int_{[-T_{0},\infty)\times Y}|F_{\widehat{{\bf A}}}|^{2}=\varepsilon_{0}.

The choice of the connection
\widehat{{\bf A}}
is unique up to the action of an element of the stabilizer of
\widehat{\rho}
.

For any
T\in(0,\infty)
, we may glue the connection
\widehat{{\bf A}}
to
\widehat{\Lambda}
to
define a connection
\widehat{{\bf A}}\#_{T}\widehat{\Lambda}
on
W
. To perform this pre-gluing construction, let

\chi:\mathbb{R}\to[0,1]
be a smooth function such that

\chi(t)=0
for
t\leq-1
and
\chi(t)=1
for
t\geq 0
.
Then the connection which we call
\widehat{\bf A}\#_{T}\widehat{\Lambda}
is given over
the cylindrical end
(-\infty,0)\times Y\subset W
by

\chi(s+T)\widehat{\Lambda}+(1-\chi(s+T))\tau_{2T}^{*}(\widehat{\bf A})

where
\tau_{T}:\mathbb{R}\times Y\to\mathbb{R}\times Y
is the map given by
\tau_{T}(s,y)=(s+T,y)
. In the remaining part of
W
,

\widehat{\bf A}\#_{T}\widehat{\Lambda}
agrees with
\widehat{\Lambda}
.

Let us briefly discuss how to carry out this constructions for the whole neighborhood
U\subset\breve{M}_{\zeta}
in the space of
framed connections. First, there is an open subset
\widehat{U}\subset\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}_{\zeta}(Y,w)
, consisting
of framed connections
(\widehat{{\bf A}},\Phi)
so that the equivalence class
[\widehat{\bf A},\Phi]
lies in
U
, and
\widehat{{\bf A}}

satisfies the integral equality above, has asymptotic
\widehat{\rho}
, and is in temporal gauge. Because the framing is chosen arbitrarily,
this is an
SO(3)
-invariant subset of the space of framed connections; and because of the
\Gamma_{\widehat{\rho}}
ambiguity in
the lift
\widehat{{\bf A}}
, the projection
\pi:\widehat{U}\to U
is a
\Gamma_{\widehat{\rho}}
-bundle.

We write
\mathcal{O}_{\widehat{\rho}}
for the
SO(3)
-orbit of pairs
(\widehat{\rho},\Phi)
where
\widehat{\rho}
is the fixed lift above and

\Phi
is a chose of framing on
(Y,w)
; we similarly write
\mathcal{O}_{\widehat{\Lambda}}
for pairs
(\widehat{\Lambda},\Phi_{W})
where

\widehat{\Lambda}
is the chosen lift above and
\Phi_{W}
is chosen arbitrarily. Finally, the gluing map described above defines for each
T

a map
\widehat{U}\times_{\mathcal{O}_{\widehat{\rho}}}\mathcal{O}_{\widehat{\Lambda}}\to\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}_{z}(W,c)
,
defined by

\big((\widehat{\bf A},\Phi),(\widehat{\Lambda},\Phi_{W})\big)\mapsto(\widehat{\bf A}\#_{T}\widehat{\Lambda},\Phi_{W}).

It is transparent from the construction above that this map is equivariant under the action of

\Gamma_{\widehat{\rho}}\times_{\Gamma_{\widehat{\rho}}}\Gamma_{\widehat{\Lambda}}\cong S^{1}
,
and hence descends to an
SO(3)
-equivariant map from the quotient
U\times_{\mathcal{O}_{\rho}}\mathcal{O}_{\Lambda}\cong U
to
\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}_{z}(W,c)
.

Having described how to do this construction for families, we henceforth focus on one instanton at a time;
the rest of the discussion can be carried out in families as well.
To simplify notation, we write
{\bf A}\#_{T}\Lambda
for an element of the
S^{1}
-orbit of framed connections produced by the construction above,
suppressing the framing from notation.

The framed connection
{\bf A}\#_{T}\Lambda
is an approximate instanton, and we want to perturb it to obtain
an actual solution of the ASD equation.
To achieve this, we need to construct right inverses for the ASD operators.
Since the connection
{\bf A}
is regular, the linearization
of the ASD equation

d_{{\bf A}}^{+}:\Omega^{1}(\mathbb{R}\times Y,\text{ad}(\mathbf{E}_{w}))\to\Omega^{+}(\mathbb{R}\times Y,\text{ad}(\mathbf{E}_{w}))

(5.6)

is surjective. After taking appropriate Sobolev completions of the above function spaces,
we fix a right inverse
R_{\bf A}
for (
5.6
). On the other hand, the operator
d_{\Lambda}^{+}

has non-trivial cokernel.
We fix a linear map

\sigma_{\Lambda}:H^{+}(\Lambda)\to\Omega^{+}(W,\text{ad}(\mathbf{E}_{c}))

which is supported away from the cylindrical ends of
W
so that the operator

d_{\Lambda}^{+}+\sigma_{\Lambda}:\Omega^{1}(W,\text{ad}(\mathbf{E}_{c}))\oplus H^{+}(\Lambda)\to\Omega^{+}(W,\text{ad}(\mathbf{E}_{c})),

which maps
(a,h)
to
d_{\Lambda}^{+}(a)+\sigma_{\Lambda}(h)
is surjective. Again after taking appropriate Sobolev completions of the
space of 1-forms and self-dual forms on
W
, we may find a right inverse
R_{\Lambda}
. Linear gluing theory
(see, for example,
[
7
, Chapter 3.3]
) allows us to obtain a right inverse
Q_{\bf A}^{T}
for the
operator

d^{+}_{{\bf A}\#_{T}\Lambda}+\sigma_{\Lambda}:\Omega^{1}(W,\text{ad}(\mathbf{E}_{c}))\oplus H^{+}(\Lambda)\to\Omega^{+}(W,\text{ad}(\mathbf{E}_{c})),

which depends continuously on
T
. Note that here we use the assumption that the target of
\sigma_{\Lambda}
is away from the
gluing region and hence the above map is well-defined. We also fix the notation

Q_{{\bf A},1}^{T}:\Omega^{+}(W,\text{ad}(\mathbf{E}_{c}))\to\Omega^{1}(W,\text{ad}(\mathbf{E}_{c})),\hskip 28.45274ptQ_{{\bf A},H}^{T}:\Omega^{+}(W,\text{ad}(\mathbf{E}_{c}))\to H^{+}(\Lambda),

for the components of
Q_{\bf A}^{T}
. (As before, we need to consider appropriate Sobolev completions of the
function spaces in the domain and the co-domian of the above operators.)

To perturb the connections
{\bf A}\#_{T}\Lambda
to ASD connections, first we consider
the weaker equation

F^{+}({\bf A}\#_{T}\Lambda+Q_{{\bf A},1}^{T}(\phi))+\sigma_{\Lambda}(Q_{{\bf A},H}^{T}(\phi))=0.

A solution to this equation is equivalent to a fixed point of the operator

Z_{\bf A}^{T}(\phi)=-F^{+}({\bf A}\#_{T}\Lambda)-(Q_{{\bf A},1}^{T}(\phi)\wedge Q_{{\bf A},1}^{T}(\phi))^{+}.

One can apply the contraction mapping principle to conclude that this equation has a unique
solution for any
{\bf A}\in U
and any
T\in(0,\infty)
. (Note that we are still using the assumption
that
T_{0}
is a large constant, so that
-F^{+}({\bf A}\#_{T}\Lambda)
may be assumed to be arbitrarily small.) If
\phi_{0}
is the solution of
this equation, then
Q_{{\bf A},H}^{T}(\phi_{0})
determines an
S^{1}
-equivariant map from
\widehat{U}\times(0,\infty)
to
H^{+}(\Lambda)
.
This induces a section
\psi^{\textup{an}}_{\zeta\Lambda}

of the bundle
\mathcal{H}_{\alpha,\rho}
over
U\times(0,\infty)
, and the honest solutions of
the ASD equation are given by the zeros of this section.
This section extends continuously as zero over the space
U\times\{\infty\}
.
The desired embedding
\Phi_{\zeta\Lambda}
is given by mapping
({\bf A},T)
in the zero set of

\psi^{\textup{an}}_{\zeta\Lambda}
to
{\bf A}\#_{T}\Lambda+\phi_{0}
.
∎

We need a global version of Proposition
5.1
. In particular, we wish to consider an extension of the above proposition to the case that
{\bf A}\in\breve{M}_{\zeta}(Y,w;\alpha,\rho)
is replaced with a broken trajectory in
\breve{M}_{\zeta}(Y,w;\alpha,\rho)
. This proposition is the first place we are obligated to use stratified-smooth spaces, as here we take the zero set of a continuous section of a vector bundle which is smooth on each stratum; a priori, there is no reason to believe the result is a stratified-smooth manifold. However, the definition of stratified-smooth spaces is chosen so that such zero sets are always stratified-smooth.

Proposition 5.7
.

Suppose
z
and
\zeta
are given as above and
-1\leq i(z)\leq 6
. Then the following holds.

(i)

There is an
SO(3)
-equivariant continuous section
\psi^{\textup{an}}_{\zeta\Lambda}
of
\pi_{1}^{*}(\mathcal{H}_{\alpha,\rho})
over

\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta}(Y,w;\alpha,\rho)\times(0,\infty]
which vanishes on the subspace
\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta}(Y,w;\alpha,\rho)\times\{\infty\}
, and whose restriction to each stratum of

\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta}(Y,w;\alpha,\rho)\times(0,\infty)
is smooth and transverse to the zero section.
In particular, the intersection of
\left(\psi^{\textup{an}}_{\zeta\Lambda}\right)^{-1}(0)
and
\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta}(Y,w;\alpha,\rho)\times(0,\infty)
is again a stratified-smooth space.

(ii)

There is an injective open continuous map
\Phi_{\zeta\Lambda}:\left(\psi^{\textup{an}}_{\zeta\Lambda}\right)^{-1}(0)\to M^{+}_{z}(W,c;\alpha,\rho^{\prime})
such that

\Phi_{\zeta\Lambda}
maps the subspace

\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta}(Y,w;\alpha,\rho)\times\{\infty\}\subset\left(\psi^{\textup{an}}_{\zeta\Lambda}\right)^{-1}(0)
to
\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta}(Y,w;\alpha,\rho)\times\{\Lambda\}\subset M^{+}_{z}(W,c;\alpha,\rho^{\prime})
via the identity map.
Moreover, the restriction of
\Phi_{\zeta\Lambda}
to the intersection of
\left(\psi^{\textup{an}}_{\zeta\Lambda}\right)^{-1}(0)
and
\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta}(Y,w;\alpha,\rho)\times(0,\infty)

gives an open embedding of stratified-smooth spaces.

(iii)

For
\beta\in{\mathfrak{C}}_{\pi}(Y,w)
, let
\zeta_{0}:\alpha\to\beta
and
\zeta_{1}:\beta\to\rho
be paths of connections on
(Y,w)
with composite
\zeta_{0}*\zeta_{1}=\zeta
. For any
[x,y]
in the subspace

\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta_{0}}(Y,w;\alpha,\beta)\times_{\beta}\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta_{1}}(Y,w;\beta,\rho)

(5.8)

of
\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta}(Y,w;\alpha,\rho)
, we have

\psi^{\textup{an}}_{\zeta\Lambda}([x,y,\Lambda],t)=\psi^{\textup{an}}_{\zeta_{1}\Lambda}([y,\Lambda],t),

(5.9)

and if
\psi^{\textup{an}}_{\beta\Lambda}([y,\Lambda],t)=0
,
then

\Phi_{\zeta\Lambda}([x,y,\Lambda],t)=(x,\Phi_{\zeta_{1}\Lambda}([y,\Lambda],t)).

To interpret (
5.9
), note that the restriction of
\mathcal{H}_{\alpha,\rho}
to the subspace of
\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta}(Y,w;\alpha,\rho)
given in (
5.8
) is induced by the pull-back of
\mathcal{H}_{\alpha,\rho}
. In particular, the equivariant section
\psi^{\text{an}}_{\beta\Lambda}
of
\mathcal{H}_{\alpha,\rho}
determines a section of
\mathcal{H}_{\alpha,\rho}
restricted to (
5.8
).

Sketch of the proof.

This proposition is a global version of Proposition
5.1
and can be verified
by constructing the section
\psi^{\textup{an}}_{\zeta\Lambda}
and the map
\Phi_{\zeta\Lambda}

in a neighborhood of points in dimension
i
strata of
\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta}(Y,w;\alpha,\rho)

by induction on
i
. The induction step can be addressed by an argument similar to
Proposition
5.1
. The transversality of
\psi^{\textup{an}}_{\zeta\Lambda}
follows from our
assumption on regularity of the elements of
M^{+}_{z}(W,c;\alpha,\rho^{\prime})
away from obstructed solutions.
The compatibility assumption in (iii) can be verified as in
[
8
, Proposition 7.2.64]
.
∎

There is an analogue of Proposition
5.1
for obstructed solutions of type II in the moduli spaces of the form
M^{+}_{z}(W,c;\rho,\alpha^{\prime})
. In particular, a neighborhood of such obstructed solutions in
M^{+}_{z}(W,c;\rho,\alpha^{\prime})
are described using an
SO(3)
-equivariant section
\psi^{\text{an}}_{\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}
of
\mathcal{H}_{\rho^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime}}\times[-\infty,0)
over
\breve{M}_{\zeta^{\prime}}^{+}(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime};\rho^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})\times[-\infty,0)
and a map
\Phi_{\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}:\left(\psi^{\text{an}}_{\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}\right)^{-1}(0)\to M^{+}_{z}(W,c;\rho,\alpha^{\prime})
. These satisfy the analogues of properties (i)-(iii) in Proposition
5.1
.

The following proposition concerns the obstructed solutions of type III and describes the behavior of the moduli spaces
M^{+}_{z}(W,c;\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})
in a neighborhood of such obstructed solutions. The pull-back of the bundle
\mathcal{H}_{\Lambda}
determines a complex line bundle over

X_{\zeta,\zeta^{\prime}}:=\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta}(Y,w;\alpha,\rho)\times_{\rho}\mathcal{O}_{\Lambda}\times_{\rho^{\prime}}\breve{M}_{\zeta^{\prime}}^{+}(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime};\rho^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime}),

which is denoted by
\mathcal{H}_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}
. It is clear from the definition of
\mathcal{H}_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}
that it is the pullback of the bundles
\mathcal{H}_{\alpha,\rho}
and
\mathcal{H}_{\rho^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime}}
with respect to the obvious projection maps. Thus sections of
\mathcal{H}_{\alpha,\rho}
and
\mathcal{H}_{\rho^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime}}
can be also regarded as sections of
\mathcal{H}_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}
, and this convention is used in the statement of the following proposition about the behavior of the moduli spaces around obstructed solutions of type III.

We omit the proof, as it follows essentially the same lines as Proposition
5.1
.

Proposition 5.10
.

Suppose
z
,
\zeta
and
\zeta^{\prime}
are given as above and
0\leq i(z)\leq 7
. Then the following holds.

(i)

There is an
SO(3)
-equivariant section
\psi^{\textup{an}}_{\zeta\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}
of
\pi_{1}^{*}(\mathcal{H}_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}})
over

X_{\zeta,\zeta^{\prime}}\times(0,\infty]\times[-\infty,0)

whose restriction to
X_{\zeta,\zeta^{\prime}}\times\big((0,\infty]\times[-\infty,0)\setminus\{(\infty,-\infty)\}\big)
is transverse to the zero section. For any
[x,\Lambda,x^{\prime}]\in X_{\zeta,\zeta^{\prime}}
, we have

\psi^{\textup{an}}_{\zeta\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}([x,\Lambda,x^{\prime}],t,-\infty)=\psi^{\textup{an}}_{\zeta\Lambda}([x,\Lambda],t),\hskip 28.45274pt\psi^{\textup{an}}_{\zeta\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}([x,\Lambda,x^{\prime}],\infty,t)=\psi^{\textup{an}}_{\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}([\Lambda,x^{\prime}],t).

In particular,
\psi^{\textup{an}}_{\zeta\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}
vanishes on
X_{\zeta,\zeta^{\prime}}\times\{(\infty,-\infty)\}
and the intersection of the complement of this space and
\left(\psi^{\textup{an}}_{\zeta\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}\right)^{-1}(0)
is a stratified-smooth space.

(ii)

There is an injective open continuous map
\Phi_{\zeta\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}:\left(\psi^{\textup{an}}_{\zeta\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}\right)^{-1}(0)\to M^{+}_{z}(W,c;\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})
such that
\Phi_{\zeta\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}
maps

X_{\zeta,\zeta^{\prime}}\times\{(\infty,-\infty)\}
to
X_{\zeta,\zeta^{\prime}}\subset M^{+}(W;\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})
via the identity map. Moreover, the restriction of
\Phi_{\zeta\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}
to the intersection of

X_{\zeta,\zeta^{\prime}}\times\big((0,\infty]\times[-\infty,0)\setminus\{(\infty,-\infty)\}\big)
and
\left(\psi^{\textup{an}}_{\zeta\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}\right)^{-1}(0)
gives an open embedding of stratified-smooth spaces.

(iii)

For
\beta\in{\mathfrak{C}}_{\pi}(Y,w)
, let
\zeta_{0}:\alpha\to\beta
and
\zeta_{1}:\beta\to\rho
be paths of connections on
(Y,w)
with composite
\zeta_{0}*\zeta_{1}=\zeta
. For any
[x,y,\Lambda,x^{\prime}]

in the subspace

\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta_{0}}(Y,w;\alpha,\beta)\times_{\beta}\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta_{1}}(Y,w;\beta,\rho)\times_{\rho}\mathcal{O}_{\Lambda}\times_{\rho^{\prime}}\breve{M}_{\zeta^{\prime}}^{+}(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime};\rho^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})

of
X_{\zeta,\zeta^{\prime}}
, we have

\psi^{\textup{an}}_{\zeta\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}([x,y,\Lambda,x^{\prime}],t,t^{\prime})=\psi^{\textup{an}}_{\zeta_{1}\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}([y,\Lambda,x^{\prime}],t,t^{\prime}),

and if
\psi^{\textup{an}}_{\beta\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}([y,\Lambda,x^{\prime}],t,t^{\prime})=0
, then

\Phi_{\zeta\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}([x,y,\Lambda,x^{\prime}],t,t^{\prime})=(x,\Phi_{\zeta_{1}\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}([y,\Lambda,x^{\prime}],t,t^{\prime})).

A similar claim holds for any
\beta^{\prime}\in{\mathfrak{C}}_{\pi}(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime})
, paths of connections
\zeta_{0}^{\prime}:\rho^{\prime}\to\beta^{\prime}
and
\zeta_{1}^{\prime}:\beta^{\prime}\to\alpha^{\prime}
on
(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime})
with composite
\zeta_{0}^{\prime}*\zeta_{1}^{\prime}=\zeta^{\prime}
, and any
[x,\Lambda,x^{\prime},y^{\prime}]
in the following
subspace of
X_{\zeta,\zeta^{\prime}}
:

\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta}(Y,w;\alpha,\rho)\times_{\rho}\mathcal{O}_{\Lambda}\times_{\rho^{\prime}}\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta_{0}^{\prime}}(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime};\rho^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})\times_{\beta^{\prime}}\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta_{1}^{\prime}}(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime};\beta^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})

(iv)

Let
[x,\Lambda,x^{\prime}]\in X_{\zeta,\zeta^{\prime}}
. If
\psi^{\textup{an}}_{\zeta\Lambda}([x,\Lambda],t)=0
, we have

\Phi_{\zeta\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}([x,\Lambda,x^{\prime}],t,-\infty)=[\Phi_{\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}([x,\Lambda],t),x^{\prime}].

Similarly, if
\psi^{\textup{an}}_{\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}([\Lambda,y],t)=0
, we have

\Phi_{\zeta\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}([x,\Lambda,x^{\prime}],\infty,t)=[x,\Phi_{\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}([\Lambda,x^{\prime}],t)].

5.2
Modified moduli spaces

In the previous secction, we investigated the local structure of the obstructed reducibles in
M^{+}_{z}(W,c;\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})
. In this subsection, we modify the moduli spaces
M^{+}_{z}(W,c;\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})
so that the resulting moduli spaces are stratified-smooth, and so that we have explicit control over their boundary components. This modification involves two steps: removing a neighborhood of obstructed solutions and adding several new components to the moduli spaces. The first step of this process is performed using the description of neighborhoods of obstructed solutions given in the last subsection. The second step involves choosing some additional sections of the obstruction bundles, which we will fix next. Throughout this section
z
is a path of connections along
(W,c)
with
i(z)\leq 6
.

We write
\psi^{T}_{\zeta,\Lambda}
and
\psi^{T}_{\Lambda,\zeta^{\prime}}
for the restrictions of the sections
\psi^{\text{an}}_{\zeta,\Lambda}
and
\psi^{\text{an}}_{\Lambda,\zeta^{\prime}}
over
\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta}(Y,w;\alpha,\rho)\times\{T\}
and
\breve{M}_{\zeta^{\prime}}^{+}(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime};\rho^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})\times\{-T\}
for some large value
T\in(0,\infty)
that will be specified later. We fix an
SO(3)
-equivariant section
\psi^{\text{priv}}_{\zeta\Lambda}
of
\mathcal{H}_{\alpha,\rho}
over
\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta}(Y,w;\alpha,\rho)
.

Unlike
\psi^{T}_{\zeta\Lambda}
, the section
\psi^{\text{priv}}_{\zeta\Lambda}
is not determined by analysis, but is rather pre-chosen (and independent of the choice of
T
). In particular, we regard
\psi^{\text{priv}}_{\zeta\Lambda}
as an additional information attached to
(Y,w)
, rather than being determined by the cobordism
(W,c)
.

We also need to fix a section that interpolates between
\psi^{T}_{\zeta\Lambda}
and
\psi^{\text{priv}}_{\zeta\Lambda}
. Let
\psi^{\text{htpy}}_{\zeta\Lambda}
be a section of
[0,1]\times\mathcal{H}_{\alpha,\rho}
over
[0,1]\times\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta}(Y,w;\alpha,\rho)
whose restrictions to
\{0\}\times\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta}(Y,w;\alpha,\rho)
and
\{1\}\times\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta}(Y,w;\alpha,\rho)
are respectively equal to
\psi^{T}_{\zeta\Lambda}
and
\psi^{\text{priv}}_{\zeta\Lambda}
.

Similarly, we fix
\psi^{\text{priv}}_{\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}
and
\psi^{\text{htpy}}_{\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}
that are respectively sections of
\mathcal{H}_{\rho^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime}}
and
[0,1]\times\mathcal{H}_{\rho^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime}}
. Moreover, the restriction of
\psi^{\text{htpy}}_{\Lambda,\alpha^{\prime}}
to
\{0\}\times\breve{M}_{\zeta^{\prime}}^{+}(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime};\rho^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})
and
\{1\}\times\breve{M}_{\zeta^{\prime}}^{+}(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime};\rho^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})
are respectively equal to
\psi^{T}_{\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}
and
\psi^{\text{priv}}_{\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}
. The sections fixed in this paragraph are required to satisfy the following properties:

(s.1)

The number
T
is chosen such that
\psi^{T}_{\zeta\Lambda}
and
\psi^{T}_{\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}
, are stratum-wise transverse to the zero section for any
\zeta
and
\zeta^{\prime}
. We also demand that the various sections
\psi^{\text{priv}}
and
\psi^{\text{htpy}}
are stratum-wise transverse to the zero section.

(s.2)

For any pair
\zeta
,
\zeta^{\prime}
, we may regard
t\cdot\psi^{\text{priv}}_{\zeta\Lambda}+(1-t)\cdot\psi^{\text{priv}}_{\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}
with
t\in[0,1]
as a section of
\pi_{1}^{*}(\mathcal{H}_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}})
defined over the stratified-smooth manifold
[0,1]\times X_{\zeta,\zeta^{\prime}}
. This section is required to be transverse to the zero section on each stratum.

(s.3)

For
\beta\in{\mathfrak{C}}_{\pi}(Y,w)
, let
\zeta_{0}:\alpha\to\beta
and
\zeta_{1}:\beta\to\rho
be paths of connections on
(Y,w)
with composite
\zeta_{0}*\zeta_{1}=\zeta
. The restriction of
\psi^{\text{priv}}_{\zeta\Lambda}
to

\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta_{0}}(Y,w;\alpha,\beta)\times_{\beta}\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta_{1}}(Y,w;\beta,\rho)\subset\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta}(Y,w;\alpha,\rho)

(5.11)

is given by
\psi^{\text{priv}}_{\zeta_{1}\Lambda}
. Similarly, the restriction of
\psi^{\text{htpy}}_{\zeta\Lambda}
to

[0,1]\times\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta_{0}}(Y,w;\alpha,\beta)\times_{\beta}\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta_{1}}(Y,w;\beta,\rho)\subset[0,1]\times\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta}(Y,w;\alpha,\rho)

is given by
\psi^{\text{htpy}}_{\zeta_{1}\Lambda}
.

(s.4)

For
\beta^{\prime}\in{\mathfrak{C}}_{\pi}(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime})
, let
\zeta_{0}^{\prime}:\rho^{\prime}\to\beta^{\prime}
and
\zeta_{1}^{\prime}:\beta^{\prime}\to\alpha^{\prime}
be paths of connections on
(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime})
with composite
\zeta_{0}^{\prime}*\zeta_{1}^{\prime}=\zeta^{\prime}
. The restriction of
\psi^{\text{priv}}_{\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}
to

\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta_{0}^{\prime}}(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime};\rho^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})\times_{\beta^{\prime}}\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta_{1}^{\prime}}(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime};\beta^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})\subset\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta^{\prime}}(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime};\rho^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})

(5.12)

is given by
\psi^{\text{priv}}_{\Lambda\zeta_{0}^{\prime}}
. Similarly, the restriction of
\psi^{\text{htpy}}_{\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}
to

[0,1]\times\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta_{0}^{\prime}}(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime};\rho^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})\times_{\beta^{\prime}}\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta_{1}^{\prime}}(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime};\beta^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})\subset[0,1]\times\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta^{\prime}}(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime};\rho^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})

is given by
\psi^{\text{htpy}}_{\Lambda\zeta_{0}^{\prime}}
.

We shall also need some variations of the sections
\psi^{\text{an}}_{\zeta\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}
. First let

A_{T}=\Big\{(t,t^{\prime})\in(0,\infty]\times[-\infty,0)\;\Big|\;\frac{1}{t^{\prime}}-\frac{1}{t}=-\frac{1}{T}\Big\},

which is oriented as an inverse image of
1/t^{\prime}-1/t
, so that its boundary has the point
(\infty,-T)
with negative sign and the point
(T,-\infty)
with positive sign. (This is named
A_{T}
because it is an arc between these two points.) This is oriented diffeomorphic to the unit interval
[0,1]
via the map
(t,t^{\prime})\mapsto T/t=1+T/t^{\prime}
.

Define
\psi^{T}_{\zeta\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}
be the restriction of
\psi^{\text{an}}_{\zeta\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}
to
A_{T}\times X_{\zeta,\zeta^{\prime}}
. Let
\psi^{\text{priv}}_{\zeta\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}
be the section over
A_{T}\times X_{\zeta,\zeta^{\prime}}
defined by

\psi^{\text{priv}}_{\zeta\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}([x,y],t,t^{\prime})=\frac{T}{t}\cdot\psi^{\text{priv}}_{\zeta\Lambda}(x)-\frac{T}{t^{\prime}}\cdot\psi^{\text{priv}}_{\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}(y).

Via the diffeomorphism
A_{T}\cong[0,1]
above, this map is identified with the section
s\psi^{\text{priv}}_{\zeta\Lambda}+(1-s)\psi^{\text{priv}}_{\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}.
Our assumption (s.2) on the sections
\psi^{\text{priv}}_{\zeta\Lambda}
and
\psi^{\text{priv}}_{\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}
imply that
\psi^{\text{priv}}_{\zeta\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}
is transverse to the zero section on each stratum.

Finally, let
\psi^{\text{htpy}}_{\zeta\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}
be a section of
\pi_{1}^{*}(\mathcal{H}_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}})
over
[0,1]\times A_{T}\times X_{\zeta,\zeta^{\prime}}
that satisfies the following properties:

(c.1)

As another requirement on
T
, we demand that
\psi^{T}_{\zeta\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}
is transverse to the zero section on each stratum. We also demand that the section
\psi^{\text{htpy}}_{\zeta\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}
is also transverse to the zero section on each stratum.

(c.2)

The restriction of
\psi^{\text{htpy}}_{\zeta,\Lambda,\zeta^{\prime}}
to
\{0\}\times A_{T}\times X_{\zeta,\zeta^{\prime}}
and
\{1\}\times A_{T}\times X_{\zeta,\zeta^{\prime}}
are respectively equal to
\psi^{T}_{\zeta\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}

and
\psi^{\text{priv}}_{\zeta\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}
.

(c.3)

For any
[x,y]\in X_{\zeta,\zeta^{\prime}}
and
t\in[0,1]
, we have

\psi^{\text{htpy}}_{\zeta\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}(t,T,-\infty,[x,y])=\psi^{\text{htpy}}_{\zeta\Lambda}(t,[x]),\hskip 28.45274pt\psi^{\text{htpy}}_{\zeta\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}(t,\infty,T,[x,y])=\psi^{\text{htpy}}_{\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}(t,[y]).

(c.4)

For
\beta\in{\mathfrak{C}}_{\pi}(Y,w)
, let
\zeta_{0}:\alpha\to\beta
and
\zeta_{1}:\beta\to\rho
be paths of connections on
(Y,w)
with composite
\zeta_{0}*\zeta_{1}=\zeta
. Let
[x,y,\Lambda,x^{\prime}]
be an element of the subspace

\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta_{0}}(Y,w;\alpha,\beta)\times_{\beta}\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta_{1}}(Y,w;\beta,\rho)\times_{\rho}\mathcal{O}_{\Lambda}\times_{\rho^{\prime}}\breve{M}_{\zeta^{\prime}}^{+}(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime};\rho^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})\subset X_{\zeta,\zeta^{\prime}}.

Then for any
(s,s^{\prime})\in A_{T}
and
t\in[0,1]
, we have

\psi^{\text{htpy}}_{\zeta\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}(t,s,s^{\prime},[x,y,\Lambda,x^{\prime}])=\psi^{\text{htpy}}_{\zeta_{1}\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}(t,s,s^{\prime},[y,\Lambda,x^{\prime}]).

Similarly, for any
\beta^{\prime}\in{\mathfrak{C}}_{\pi}(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime})
, paths of connections
\zeta_{0}^{\prime}:\rho^{\prime}\to\beta^{\prime}
and
\zeta_{1}^{\prime}:\beta^{\prime}\to\alpha^{\prime}
on
(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime})
with composite
\zeta_{0}^{\prime}=\zeta_{1}^{\prime}=\zeta^{\prime}
and any element
[x,\Lambda,x^{\prime},y^{\prime}]
of

\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta}(Y,w;\alpha,\rho)\times_{\rho}\mathcal{O}_{\Lambda}\times_{\rho^{\prime}}\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta_{0}^{\prime}}(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime};\rho^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})\times_{\beta^{\prime}}\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta_{1}^{\prime}}(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime};\beta^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})\subset X_{\zeta,\zeta^{\prime}},

we have

\psi^{\text{htpy}}_{\zeta\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}(t,s,s^{\prime},[x,\Lambda,x^{\prime},y^{\prime}])=\psi^{\text{htpy}}_{\zeta\Lambda\zeta_{0}^{\prime}}(t,s,s^{\prime},[x,\Lambda,x^{\prime}]),

for any
(s,s^{\prime})\in A_{T}
and
t\in[0,1]
.

Now, we are ready to define the modified moduli spaces. Fix
\alpha\in{\mathfrak{C}}_{\pi}(Y,w)
,
\alpha^{\prime}\in{\mathfrak{C}}_{\pi}(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime})
and a path
z:\alpha\to\alpha^{\prime}
of connections along
(W,c)
with
i(z)\leq 6
. Recall that we have an obstructed abelian instanton
\Lambda:\rho\to\rho^{\prime}
, which determines a subspace of obstructed solutions in the moduli space
M^{+}_{z}(W,c;\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})
. If
\alpha^{\prime}=\rho^{\prime}
, then this subspace consists of obstructed solutions of type I given as
\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta}(Y,w;\alpha,\rho)\times_{\rho}\mathcal{O}_{\Lambda}
with
z
being the composition of
\zeta
and the path determined by
\Lambda
. A neighborhood
U_{\zeta\Lambda}
of such obstructed solutions is given as

\Phi_{\zeta\Lambda}\mathopen{}\left(\mathopen{}\left(\psi^{\text{an}}_{\zeta\Lambda}\right)\mathclose{}^{-1}(0)\cap\mathopen{}\left(\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta}(Y,w;\alpha,\rho)\times(T,\infty]\right)\mathclose{}\right)\mathclose{}\subset M^{+}_{z}(W,c;\alpha,\rho^{\prime}).

We will verify below that the complement of
U_{\zeta\Lambda}
in
M^{+}_{z}(W,c;\alpha,\rho^{\prime})
is an oriented compact stratified-smooth space; its top stratum is an open subset of
M^{+}_{z}(W,c;\alpha,\rho^{\prime})
, and we give it the same orientation.

The zeros of
\psi^{\text{htpy}}_{\zeta\Lambda}
over
[0,1]\times\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta}(Y,w;\alpha,\rho)
gives another stratified-smooth space
M^{\text{htpy}}_{\zeta\Lambda}
associated to the path
z=\zeta*\lambda
. To define an orientation on
M^{\text{htpy}}_{\zeta\Lambda}
, first fix the product orientation on
[0,1]\times\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta}(Y,w;\alpha,\rho)
. Since
\psi^{\text{htpy}}_{\zeta\Lambda}
is a transverse section of a complex bundle, its zero set inherits an orientation from
[0,1]\times\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta}(Y,w;\alpha,\rho)
. We multiply this orientation by
\pm 1
as appropriate so that
M^{\text{htpy}}_{\zeta\Lambda}
has
\left(\psi^{T}_{\zeta\Lambda}\right)^{-1}(0)
as a boundary component with the opposite orientation as the corresponding boundary component of
M^{+}_{z}(W,c;\alpha,\rho^{\prime})\setminus U_{\zeta\Lambda}.

In the case
\alpha=\rho
, the moduli space
M^{+}_{z}(W,c;\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})
consists of obstructed solutions of type II given as
\mathcal{O}_{\Lambda}\times_{\rho^{\prime}}\breve{M}_{\zeta^{\prime}}^{+}(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime};\rho^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})
. We define the neighborhood
U_{\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}
of these obstructed solutions as the image of

\Phi_{\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}\mathopen{}\left(\mathopen{}\left(\psi^{\text{an}}_{\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}\right)\mathclose{}^{-1}(0)\cap\mathopen{}\left(\breve{M}_{\zeta^{\prime}}^{+}(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime};\rho^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})\times[-\infty,T)\right)\mathclose{}\right)\mathclose{}\subset M^{+}_{z}(W,c;\rho,\alpha^{\prime}).

The compact stratified-smooth space given by the complement of
U_{\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}
is oriented following the same convention as in the unobstructed case. We also define
M^{\text{htpy}}_{\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}
to be the stratified-smooth space given as the zeros of
\psi^{\text{htpy}}_{\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}
over
[0,1]\times\breve{M}_{\zeta^{\prime}}^{+}(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime};\rho^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})
. This space is again a compact stratified-smooth space, and its top stratum is oriented using the same convention as in the previous paragraph: one of its boundary components is canonically diffeomorphic to a boundary component in the complement of
U_{\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}
, and the orientations on these two boundary components should be opposite to one another.

For
\alpha\neq\rho
and
\alpha^{\prime}\neq\rho^{\prime}
, the space
M^{+}_{z}(W,c;\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})
contains obstructed solutions of type III given as
X_{\zeta,\zeta^{\prime}}
where the composition
\zeta*\lambda*\zeta^{\prime}=z
. The index assumption
i(z)\leq 6
implies that there are unique choices of
\zeta
and
\zeta^{\prime}
such that
X_{\zeta,\zeta^{\prime}}
is non-empty. A neighborhood
U_{\zeta\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}
of these obstructed solutions can be obtained by forming the image of

\Phi_{\zeta\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}\mathopen{}\left(\mathopen{}\left(\psi^{\text{an}}\right)\mathclose{}^{-1}(0)\cap\left(\left\{(s,s^{\prime})\in(0,\infty]\times[-\infty,0)\;\Big|\;\frac{1}{s^{\prime}}-\frac{1}{s^{\prime}}\leq-\frac{1}{T}\right\}\times X_{\zeta,\zeta^{\prime}}\right)\right)\mathclose{}\subset M^{+}_{z}(W,c;\rho,\alpha^{\prime}).

The complement of this open neighborhood is a compact stratified-smooth space and can be oriented following the same conventions as in the unobstructed case. Let also
M^{\text{htpy}}_{\zeta\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}
be the stratified-smooth space given as the zero set of
\psi^{\text{htpy}}_{\zeta\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}
over
[0,1]\times A_{T}\times X_{\zeta,\zeta^{\prime}}
. If the domain of this section is given the product orientation, then we fix the orientation on
M^{\text{htpy}}_{\zeta\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}
which differs from the standard orientation on the zero set of a complex line bundle by the appropriate factor
\pm 1
so that the appropriate boundary component has the opposite sign as the corresponding boundary component of the complement of
U_{\zeta\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}.

The
modified moduli space

N^{+}_{z}(W,c;\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})
is the
SO(3)
-space given as

N^{+}_{z}(W,c;\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}):=\mathopen{}\left(M^{+}_{z}(W,c;\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})\setminus U_{\Lambda}\right)\mathclose{}\sqcup M^{\text{htpy}}_{\Lambda}.

(5.13)

For the sake of uniformity, we have dropped the notation
\zeta
and
\zeta^{\prime}
from our notations for the spaces
U_{\zeta\Lambda}
,
U_{\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}
and
U_{\zeta\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}
, as well as from the spaces
M^{\text{htpy}}_{\zeta\Lambda}
,
M^{\text{htpy}}_{\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}
and
M^{\text{htpy}}_{\zeta\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}
.

Proposition 5.14
.

The modified moduli space
N^{+}_{z}(W,c;\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})
is a compact oriented stratified-smooth space. The boundary of
N^{+}_{z}(W,c;\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})
is the union of the following compact orientable smooth-stratified spaces.

(i)

If
\beta\in{\mathfrak{C}}_{\pi}(Y,w)
, and
\zeta_{0}:\alpha\to\beta
and
z_{1}:\beta\to\alpha^{\prime}
are paths of connections along
(Y,w)
and
(W,c)
respectively with composite
\zeta_{0}*z_{1}=z
, then

\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta_{0}}(Y,w;\alpha,\beta)\times_{\beta}N^{+}_{z_{1}}(W,c;\beta,\alpha^{\prime})

is a boundary component of
N^{+}_{z}(W,c;\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})
. The boundary orientation and the fiber product orientation on this boundary component are related to each other by
(-1)^{\dim(\alpha)}
.

(ii)

If
\beta^{\prime}\in{\mathfrak{C}}_{\pi^{\prime}}(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime})
, and
z_{0}:\alpha\to\beta^{\prime}
and
\zeta_{1}:\beta^{\prime}\to\alpha^{\prime}
are paths of connections along
(W,c)
and
(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime})
respectively with composite
z_{0}*\zeta^{\prime}_{1}=z
, then

N^{+}_{z_{0}}(W,c;\alpha,\beta^{\prime})\times_{\beta^{\prime}}\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta_{1}^{\prime}}(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime};\beta^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})

is a boundary component of
N^{+}_{z}(W,c;\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})
. The boundary orientation and the fiber product orientation on this boundary component are related to each other by
(-1)^{\widetilde{i}(z_{0})+\dim(\alpha)+1}
.

Depending on the type of
\alpha
and
\alpha^{\prime}
, the remaining boundary components are given as in one of the following three cases:

(l)

If
\alpha^{\prime}=\rho^{\prime}
and
\zeta:\alpha\to\rho
is a path of connections on
(Y,w)
with composite
\zeta*\lambda=z
, then the boundary components of
N^{+}_{z}(W,c;\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})
contain

(l-i)

two copies of
\mathopen{}\left(\psi^{T}_{\zeta\Lambda}\right)\mathclose{}^{-1}(0)
with opposite orientations;

(l-ii)

a copy of
\mathopen{}\left(\psi^{\textup{priv}}_{\zeta\Lambda}\right)\mathclose{}^{-1}(0)
whose orientation differs from the standard orientation of the zero section of a complex line bundle over an oriented space by a factor of
(-1)^{\dim(\alpha)}
.

(r)

If
\alpha=\rho
and
\zeta^{\prime}:\rho^{\prime}\to\alpha^{\prime}
is a path of connections on
(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime})
with composite
\lambda*\zeta^{\prime}=z
, then the boundary components of
N^{+}_{z}(W,c;\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})
contain

(r-i)

two copies of
\mathopen{}\left(\psi^{T}_{\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}\right)\mathclose{}^{-1}(0)
with opposite orientations;

(r-ii)

a copy of
\mathopen{}\left(\psi^{\textup{priv}}_{\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}\right)\mathclose{}^{-1}(0)
whose orientation differs from the standard orientation of the zero section of a complex line bundle over an oriented space by a factor of
-1=(-1)^{\dim(\rho)+1}
.

(c)

If
\alpha\neq\rho
and
\alpha^{\prime}\neq\rho^{\prime}
, and
\zeta:\alpha\to\rho,\;\zeta^{\prime}:\rho^{\prime}\to\alpha^{\prime}
are paths of connections on
(Y,w)
and
(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime})
with composite
\zeta*\lambda*\zeta^{\prime}=z
, then the boundary components of
N^{+}_{z}(W,c;\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})
contain

(c-i)

two copies of
\mathopen{}\left(\psi^{T}_{\zeta\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}\right)\mathclose{}^{-1}(0)
with opposite orientations;

(c-ii)

a copy of
\mathopen{}\left(\psi^{\textup{priv}}_{\zeta\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}\right)\mathclose{}^{-1}(0)
, whose orientation differs from the standard orientation of the zero set of the section
s\psi^{\textup{priv}}_{\zeta\Lambda}+(1-s)\psi^{\textup{priv}}_{\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}
over
\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta}(Y,w;\alpha,\rho)\times_{\rho}\mathopen{}\left([0,1]\times\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta^{\prime}}(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime};\rho^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})\right)\mathclose{}
by a factor of
(-1)^{\dim\alpha-1}
.

Sketch of proof.

What we will verify is that
N^{+}_{z}
is a stratified-smooth space, and we will confirm the relevant boundary orientations; the actual enumeration of boundary strata is straightforward. The fact that
M^{\text{htpy}}_{\Lambda}
is an oriented stratified-smooth space is the content of Proposition
A.2
.

As for the complement of
U_{\Lambda}
, one should define a function
L:M^{+}_{z}(W,c;\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})\to(-\infty,0)
so that on a slight thickening of
U_{\Lambda}
it takes the following form in the three different cases. In the first case,
L(x,\Lambda,t)=-1/t
. In the second case,
L(\Lambda,y,t^{\prime})=1/t^{\prime}
. In the third case,
L(x,t,\Lambda,t^{\prime},y)=1/t^{\prime}-1/t
. In all cases, the set
U_{\Lambda}
is defined by the property
L>-1/T
. We extend
L
arbitrarily to the whole of
M^{+}_{z}
, so long as
L^{-1}[-1/T,0)=\overline{U_{\Lambda}}
remains true. Then Proposition
A.2
guarantees that the complement of
U_{\Lambda}
is an oriented stratified-smooth space with orientation induced as a codimension-0 subspace of
M^{+}_{z}
.

What remains is to confirm the stated boundary orientations; because
M^{\text{htpy}}
is defined so that its positive boundary component is oriented by the same conventions as
\mathopen{}\left(\psi^{T}\right)\mathclose{}^{-1}(0)
, it suffices to compute the boundary orientation on the stratum
L^{-1}(-1/T)
using Proposition
A.2
. Instead of computing the orientation on the zero locus using the convention that the last factor should map via
dL
in an orientation-preserving way to
\mathbb{R}
, the sign
(-1)^{\dim X-1}
shows that we can compute this zero set using the convention that the
first
factor maps via
dL
in an orientation-preserving way to
\mathbb{R}
(with no additional sign). We ignore the discussion of taking the zero set of
\psi^{T}
, as taking the zero set of a complex bundle induces an orientation in a canonical way which commutes with all other orientation-related operations.

Now the conventions of Section
3.7
have
M^{+}_{\zeta}(Y,w;\alpha,\rho)
oriented as

\alpha\times\mathbb{R}\times\breve{M}^{\text{Fiber}}_{\zeta}(Y,w;\alpha,\rho).

Commuting the
\mathbb{R}
factor to the front adds a sign of
(-1)^{\dim\alpha}
. The map
L
is then given by
L(t,a,x)=-1/t
; the differential of this is
dL(\dot{t},\dot{a},\dot{x})=t^{-2}dt
, so
dL
gives an orientation-preserving isomorphism from the first factor to the last. Therefore
L^{-1}(-1/T)
is oriented as

(-1)^{\dim\alpha}\alpha\times\breve{M}^{\text{Fiber}}_{\zeta}\cong(-1)^{\dim\alpha}\breve{M}_{\zeta}(Y,w;\alpha,\rho).

This justifies the first sign. The second is similar: this space is oriented as
\rho^{\prime}\times\mathbb{R}\times\breve{M}^{\text{Fiber}}_{\zeta^{\prime}}(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime};\rho^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})
. Moving the factor of
\mathbb{R}
to the front introduces a sign of
(-1)^{\dim\rho^{\prime}}=1
. The map is given by
L(t^{\prime},a^{\prime},A^{\prime})=1/t^{\prime}
, so that
dL=-(t^{\prime})^{-2}dt^{\prime}
; this is orientation-reversing, which introduces an additional sign of
-1
, giving the statement in the proposition.

Finally, the set
L^{-1}(-1/T)
over
X_{\zeta,\zeta^{\prime}}
is oriented as the zero set of a map from the following space to
\mathbb{R}
:

\alpha\times\mathbb{R}\times\breve{M}^{\text{Fiber}}_{\zeta}(Y,w;\alpha,\rho)\times\mathbb{R}\times\breve{M}^{\text{Fiber}}_{\zeta^{\prime}}(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime};\rho^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime}).

Commuting all factors of
\mathbb{R}
to the front induces a sign of
(-1)^{2\dim\alpha+\widetilde{i}(\zeta)-1}
; passing to the boundary face
L^{-1}(-1/T)
gives us

(-1)^{\widetilde{i}(\zeta)}A_{T}\times\alpha\times\breve{M}^{\text{Fiber}}_{\zeta}(Y,w;\alpha,\rho)\times\breve{M}^{\text{Fiber}}_{\zeta^{\prime}}(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime};\rho^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime}),

where the extra sign appears because
A_{T}
is oriented as the inverse image of
L^{-1}(-1/T)
, but placing the
dL
factor
first
is the opposite orientation convention on
A_{T}
. Finally, commuting
A_{T}
back to the middle (and recalling that the oriented diffeomorphism from
A_{T}
to
[0,1]
sends our section to the one stated in the Proposition) gives us the sign of
(-1)^{\dim\alpha-1}
, as stated.
∎

Remark 5.15
.

Because the map
\psi^{\text{priv}}_{\zeta\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}
may be identified with the section
t\cdot\psi^{\text{priv}}_{\zeta\Lambda}+(1-t)\cdot\psi^{\text{priv}}_{\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}
over
\breve{M}_{\zeta}(Y,w;\alpha,\rho)\times[0,1]\times\breve{M}_{\zeta^{\prime}}(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime};\rho^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})
, it can be described entirely in terms of these sections. We give an alternate description of this zero set in Lemma
A.3
in the appendix, which we will use in Section 5.4 below when verifying certain boundary relations in the definition of bimodule.
∎

5.3
The suspended flow category

In the previous section, we defined modified moduli spaces
N^{+}_{\zeta}(W,c;\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})
attached to
(W,c)
. We would like to use the machinery of Section
4.2
to say that these define a bimodule (as in Definition
4.2
) between flow categories, and hence a chain map between their flow complexes. This amounts to a boundary relation on these moduli spaces.

Inspecting the boundary relation of Proposition
5.2
, we see that these moduli spaces do not define a bimodule
\mathcal{I}(Y,w,\pi)\to\mathcal{I}(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime},\pi^{\prime})
between the framed instanton flow categories. However, we still expect to be able to extract a chain map from these moduli spaces, using a
slightly different flow category
as the codomain. We start by describing this slightly different flow category, via a construction called the
suspended flow category
.

Before doing so, we should give a brief introduction to a construction carried out in Appendix
A.3
; see Construction
A.3
for more details. Suppose we are given a stratified-smooth space with a map to a smooth manifold
\phi:P\to M
, a complex line bundle
L\to M
, and a lift
\psi:P\to L
of
\phi
to a section of
L
transverse to the zero section. Then we may define its
real blowup
, a stratified-smooth space
B(\psi)
with a natural map to the sphere bundle
S(L)
; there is a natural projection map
\pi:B(\psi)\to P
, and if considered as a geometric chain in
M
, the chain
P
is
collapse-equivalent
to its blowup
B(\psi)
(a notion described in more detail in Appendix
A.3
; what matters here is that collapse-equivalence is preserved by fiber products, and that two collapse-equivalent stratified-smooth spaces define the same element in the geometric chain complex). This chain in
S(L)
satisfies the boundary relation

\partial B(\psi)=B(\psi|_{\partial P})-(-1)^{\dim P}Z(\psi)\times_{M}S(L),

where
Z(\psi)
is the zero set of this section and the map from the second chain to
S(L)
is projection to the second factor.

This construction allows us to lift chains in
M
to chains in the sphere bundle
S(L)
, which we use in the construction of the suspended flow category below.

Construction 5.16
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be an
SO(3)
-equivariant, finitely graded flow category as in Definition
4.2
, whose objects are all
SO(3)
-orbits isomorphic to one of
SO(3),S^{2}
, or the singleton
\ast
. We write
\text{Ob}(\mathcal{C})=\mathfrak{C}^{*}\sqcup\mathfrak{A}\sqcup\mathfrak{Z}
for the decomposition of the set of objects into these three types of orbit;
\mathfrak{C}^{*}
is the set of objects isomorphic to
SO(3)
, while
\mathfrak{A}
is the set of objects isomorphic to
S^{2}
and
\mathfrak{Z}
is the set of singleton objects.

Further, assume that each
\mathcal{C}(\alpha,\beta)
is a free
SO(3)
-manifold.

Choose an element
\rho\in\mathfrak{A}
. Fix an
SO(3)
-equivariant complex line bundle
\mathcal{H}_{\rho}\to\rho
, where
SO(3)
acts freely on any nonzero vector; one may take
\mathcal{H}_{\rho}=T\rho\cong SO(3)\times_{SO(2)}\mathbb{C}
.

Choose, for each
\beta\in\mathsf{Ob}(\mathcal{C})
, a lift
\psi_{\rho\beta}:\mathcal{C}(\rho,\beta)\to\mathcal{H}_{\rho}
and which is transverse to
0
. We demand these are compatible in the sense that, on the fiber product

\mathcal{C}(\rho,\alpha)\times_{\alpha}\mathcal{C}(\alpha,\beta)\subset\partial\mathcal{C}(\rho,\beta),

the restriction of
\psi_{\rho\beta}
coincides with the pullback of
\psi_{\rho\alpha}
to the fiber product.
10
10

10

Verifying that such
\psi_{\rho\beta}
exist is a straightforward induction; the key point is that every
\mathcal{C}(\rho,\beta)
is
SO(3)
-free, so there is no trouble arising from the demand for equivariant transversality.

These choices in hand, we define the suspended flow category
\mathcal{S}_{\rho}(\mathcal{C})
(or simply
\mathcal{S}
when the flow category
\mathcal{C}
and object
\rho
are clear from context) as follows; the construction depends on the family of sections
\psi_{\rho\beta}
, but we suppress it from notation.

First we will describe the object space. Each object
\alpha\in\mathsf{Ob}(\mathcal{C})\setminus\{\rho\}
is left unchanged and gives an object of
\mathcal{S}
, with the same relative grading. The chosen orbit
\rho
gives rise to two objects of
\mathcal{S}
. The first is
S_{\rho}=S(\mathcal{H}_{\rho})
, the unit sphere bundle of
\mathcal{H}_{\rho}\to\rho
. This space is isomorphic to
SO(3)
, and will be given relative grading one lower than
\rho
. The second is
\rho
itself, but it will have relative grading 2 less than
\rho
. To distinguish, we will write this as
\overline{\rho}
. That is,

\mathsf{Ob}(\mathcal{S})=\big(\mathsf{Ob}(\mathcal{C})\setminus\{\rho\}\big)\sqcup S_{\rho}\sqcup\overline{\rho},

whe relative grading is given by the original relative grading plus a shift factor:

\widetilde{i}_{\mathcal{S}}(\alpha,\beta)=\widetilde{i}_{\mathcal{C}}([\alpha],[\beta])+s(\alpha)-s(\beta).

If
\alpha\in\mathsf{Ob}(\mathcal{S})
, one should interpret

[\alpha]=\begin{cases}\rho&\alpha=S_{\rho},\;\overline{\rho}\\
\alpha&\text{else}\end{cases}

The shift factor is
s(\alpha)=0
for
\alpha\neq\overline{\rho},S_{\rho}
but
s(\overline{\rho})=-2
and
s(S_{\rho})=-1
.

We move on to defining the morphism spaces
\mathcal{S}(\alpha,\beta)
in our suspended flow category. Let us introduce some notation:

•

Write
Z(\rho,\beta)=Z(\psi_{\rho\beta})=\psi_{\rho\beta}^{-1}(0)
for the stratified-smooth space given by the zero locus of
\psi_{\rho\beta}
; this space has dimension two less than
\mathcal{C}(\rho,\beta)
.

•

Write
B(\rho,\beta)
for the real blowup
B(\psi_{\rho\beta})
discussed above; this has the same dimension as
\mathcal{C}(\rho,\beta)
. This is equipped with canonical maps
\psi/\|\psi\|:B(\rho,\beta)\to S_{\rho}
and
e_{+}\pi:B(\rho,\beta)\to\beta
.

Then we define the morphism spaces
\mathcal{S}_{\rho}(\alpha,\beta)
as in the following matrix:

\mathcal{S}(\alpha,\beta)=\begin{pmatrix}[c|ccc]&\alpha\neq\rho&S_{\rho}&\overline{\rho}\\
\hline\cr\beta\neq\rho&\mathcal{C}(\alpha,\beta)&-B(\rho,\beta)&Z(\rho,\beta)\\
S_{\rho}&\mathcal{C}(\alpha,\rho)\times_{\rho}S_{\rho}&0&0\\
\overline{\rho}&0&S_{\rho}&0\end{pmatrix}

Here the columns correspond to the critical orbit you start at, and the rows correspond to the critical orbit you end at.
∎

Remark 5.17
.

The fiber product map
\times_{S_{\rho}}S_{\rho}:C_{*}(S_{\rho})\to C_{*}(\overline{\rho})
arising from the term in entry
(3,2)
of the above matrix is the induced map of
\pi:S_{\rho}\to\rho
; that is, if
\phi:P\to S_{\rho}
is a geometric chain, then
\phi\times_{S_{\rho}}S_{\rho}=\pi\phi:P\to\overline{\rho}
is the composite of
\phi
with projection to the base. (Thus, one can find a copy of the algebraic mapping cone of
\pi_{*}:C_{*}(S_{\rho})\to C_{*}(\overline{\rho})
inside the Morse chain complex of this flow category.)
∎

In a moment we will verify that this does indeed give a flow category. First, we restate the properties of real blowups for the particular spaces
B(\rho,\beta)=B(\psi_{\rho\beta})
. First,
B(\rho,\beta)
sits in a canonical commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{B(\rho,\beta)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S_{\rho}\times\beta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}(\rho,\beta)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rho\times\beta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi\times 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{-}\times e_{+}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi/\|\psi\|\times e_{+}}

When considered as a geometric chain downstairs in
\rho\times\beta
by following the projection
\pi\times 1
, Lemma
A.3
asserts that this space is collapse-equivalent to
\mathcal{C}(\rho,\beta)
, so define the same elements in the geometric chain complex of
\rho\times\beta
, and induce the same chain-level fiber product maps. Lastly, by the same lemma we have

\partial B(\rho,\beta)=B(\psi_{\rho\beta}|_{\partial\mathcal{C}(\rho,\beta)})-(-1)^{\dim\mathcal{C}(\rho,\beta)}Z(\rho,\beta)\times_{\rho}S(\mathcal{H}_{\rho}).

By commuting the odd-dimensional factor
S(\mathcal{H}_{\rho})
to the left, the last term simplifies to
-S_{\rho}\times_{\rho}Z(\rho,\beta)
. As for the first term: expanding out using the boundary formula for
\partial\mathcal{C}(\rho,\beta)
from Definition
4.2
, and using the fact that
\psi_{\rho\beta}=\psi_{\rho\alpha}\times 0
over
\mathcal{C}(\rho,\alpha)\times_{\alpha}\mathcal{C}(\alpha,\beta)
, the whole boundary relation simplifies to

\partial B(\rho,\beta)=-S_{\rho}\times_{\rho}Z(\rho,\beta)+\sum_{\alpha\in\mathfrak{C}\setminus\rho}(-1)^{\widetilde{i}_{\mathcal{C}}(\rho,\alpha)}B(\rho,\alpha)\times_{\alpha}\mathcal{C}(\alpha,\beta).

(Recall here that
\dim\mathcal{C}(\rho,\alpha)=\widetilde{i}_{\mathcal{C}(\rho,\alpha)}-1
.) This in hand, we are prepared to establish that
\mathcal{S}
is a flow category.

The reader should have on-hand Definition
4.2
while reading the argument. Below is a diagram depicting the various moduli spaces in the definition of this flow category, as well as where they run from and to; when reading the argument below, keep in mind that the goal is to show that the boundary components of
\mathcal{S}(\alpha,\beta)
are a sum over
(-1)^{\widetilde{i}(\alpha,\gamma)}\mathcal{S}(\alpha,\gamma)\times_{\gamma}\mathcal{S}(\gamma,\beta)
, and this sum can be understood as running over all two-step paths from
\alpha
to
\beta
in the diagram. The moduli space
\mathcal{S}(\alpha,\gamma)
decorates the arrow from
\alpha
to
\gamma
, while the moduli space
\mathcal{S}(\gamma,\beta)
decorates the arrow from
\gamma
to
\beta
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha/\beta}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S_{\rho}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{\rho}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{\rho}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}(\alpha,\rho)\times_{\rho}S_{\rho}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-B(\rho,\beta)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z(\rho,\beta)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}(\alpha,\beta)}

The term
\alpha/\beta
means that an arrow leaving this oribt correspond to moduli spaces domain
\alpha
, while arrows running to this orbit correspond to moduli spaces with codomain
\beta
.

Theorem 5.18
.

The objects, relative gradings, and morphism spaces constituting
\mathcal{S}_{\rho}(\mathcal{C})
, constructed as above, define a flow category as in Definition
4.2
.

Proof.

Because all of these spaces are
SO(3)
-spaces and all of the objects of
\mathcal{S}
are
SO(3)
-orbits, it follows that all endpoint maps are still submersions. We leave it to the reader to confirm that

\dim\mathcal{S}(\alpha,\beta)=\dim\alpha+\widetilde{i}_{\mathcal{S}}(\alpha,\beta)-1

for all
\alpha,\beta
. Now let us check the boundary relations in the definition of flow category, one-by-one. When proving the flow category relations below, we immediately replace every occurrence of
\mathcal{S}(\alpha,\beta)
with its definition in terms of
\mathcal{C}
from the matrix in Construction
5.3
.

•

If
\alpha,\beta\neq\overline{\rho},S_{\rho}
, then the flow category relations stipulate that

\displaystyle(-1)^{\dim\alpha}\partial\mathcal{C}(\alpha,\beta)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\gamma\neq\rho}(-1)^{\widetilde{i}_{\mathcal{S}}(\alpha,\gamma)}\mathcal{C}(\alpha,\gamma)\times_{\gamma}\mathcal{C}(\gamma,\beta)

\displaystyle+(-1)^{\widetilde{i}_{\mathcal{S}}(\alpha,S_{\rho})}\mathcal{C}(\alpha,\rho)\times_{\rho}S_{\rho}\times_{S_{\rho}}(-B(\rho,\beta)).

When
\gamma\neq\rho
, the expression
\widetilde{i}_{\mathcal{S}}(\alpha,\gamma)
is simply
\widetilde{i}_{\mathcal{C}}(\alpha,\gamma)
. Because
\widetilde{i}_{\mathcal{S}}(\alpha,S_{\rho})=\widetilde{i}_{\mathcal{C}}(\alpha,\rho)-1,
the terms in the last sum simplify to

(-1)^{\widetilde{i}_{\mathcal{C}}(\alpha,\rho)}\mathcal{C}(\alpha,\rho)\times_{\rho}B(\rho,\beta).

Because
B(\rho,\beta)
is collapse-equivalent to
\mathcal{C}(\rho,\beta)
as a chain in
\rho\times\beta
, this fiber product is equal to
\mathcal{C}(\alpha,\rho)\times_{\rho}\mathcal{C}(\rho,\beta)
in the geometric chain complex. Thus the flow category relation for
\mathcal{S}(\alpha,\beta)
asks that we have an equality of elements of
C_{*}(\alpha\times\beta)

(-1)^{\dim\alpha}\partial\mathcal{C}(\alpha,\beta)=\sum_{\gamma\in\mathsf{Ob}(\mathcal{C})}(-1)^{\widetilde{i}_{\mathcal{C}}(\alpha,\gamma)}\mathcal{C}(\alpha,\gamma)\times_{\gamma}\mathcal{C}(\gamma,\beta).

This is precisely what it means when we assume that
\mathcal{C}
is a flow category to begin with.

•

If
\alpha=S_{\rho}
and
\beta\neq\overline{\rho},S_{\rho}
, the desired boundary relation is

\displaystyle(-1)^{\dim S_{\rho}}(-\partial B(\rho,\beta))

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\widetilde{i}_{\mathcal{S}}(S_{\rho},\overline{\rho})}S_{\rho}\times_{\rho}Z(\rho,\beta)

\displaystyle+\sum_{\gamma\neq\rho}(-1)^{\widetilde{i}_{\mathcal{S}}(S_{\rho},\gamma)}-B(\rho,\gamma)\times_{\gamma}\mathcal{C}(\gamma,\beta).

After simplifying the signs and simplifying the last terms, we are trying to show that

\partial B(\rho,\beta)=-S_{\rho}\times_{\rho}Z(\rho,\beta)+\sum_{\gamma}(-1)^{\widetilde{i}_{\mathcal{C}}(\rho,\gamma)}B(\rho,\gamma)\times_{\gamma}\mathcal{C}(\gamma,\beta).

This is precisely the boundary relation for the real blowup recalled above.

•

The boundary relation for
\alpha=\overline{\rho}
and
\beta\neq\overline{\rho},S_{\rho}
follows immediately from the flow category relation for
\mathcal{C}
(take the zero locus of
L
on both sides of the flow category relation). The case
\alpha\neq S_{\rho},\overline{\rho}
and
\beta=S_{\rho}
is similarly tautological.

•

The boundary of
\mathcal{S}(\alpha,\overline{\rho})
is tautologically empty. On the right-hand side of the flow category relation, the only factorization
\mathcal{S}(\alpha,\beta)\times_{\beta}\mathcal{S}(\beta,\overline{\rho})
which is not tautologically empty is
\beta=S_{\rho}
. This means that the flow category relation in this case is

0=\mathcal{C}(\alpha,\rho)\times_{\rho}S_{\rho}\times_{S_{\rho}}S_{\rho}=\pm\mathcal{C}(\alpha,\rho)\times_{\rho}S_{\rho},

where the right-hand side is interpreted as a chain in
\alpha\times\overline{\rho}
. Because the map
\mathcal{C}(\alpha,\rho)\times_{\rho}S_{\rho}\to\overline{\rho}
factors through the circle bundle projection

\mathcal{C}(\alpha,\rho)\times_{\rho}S_{\rho}\to\mathcal{C}(\alpha,\rho),

it follows that every stratum of this chain has small rank. So it is a degenerate chain, hence zero on the chain level, and our relation holds.

The final handful of cases follow by nearly identical arguments, occasionally recalling that
B(\rho,\beta)
and
\mathcal{C}(\rho,\beta)
define the same geometric chains in
\rho\times\beta
, as they are collapse-equivalent.
∎

As mentioned above, there is a copy of the algebraic mapping cone

\mathsf{Cone}=\mathsf{Cone}\big(C_{*}(SO_{3})[-1]\to C_{*}(S^{2})[-2]\big)

as a summand of the flow complex
CM_{*}(\mathcal{S}_{\rho})
(though not a subcomplex!). We think of the flow category
\mathcal{S}_{\rho}
as being the flow-categorical analogue of this mapping cone construction.

In fact, we will build a comparison map between these two flow categories based on that idea. There is a degree-zero chain map
\Sigma:C_{*}(S^{2})\to\mathsf{Cone}
, given by taking a chain
\phi:P\to S^{2}
to the pullback
\phi\times_{S^{2}}SO(3)
in
SO(3)
. If
P
is a point, it is sent to a circle; if
\phi
is the fundamental class of
S^{2}
it is sent to the fundamental class of
SO(3)
. This is a chain map because pushing
\phi\times_{S^{2}}SO(3)
down to
S^{2}
sends it to a degenerate chain (hence zero), so the mapping-cone portion of the differential is zero on
\phi\times_{S^{2}}SO(3)
.

It is easy to see by hand that the map above induces an isomorphism on homology
H_{*}(S^{2})\to H_{*}(\mathsf{Cone})
.

Next, we use this observation to define a bimodule
\Sigma_{\rho}:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{S}_{\rho}
which induces an isomorphism on the homology of their flow complexes.

Construction 5.19
.

Define the
SO(3)
-equivariant bimodule
\Sigma_{\rho}
as follows. Let
\Sigma_{\rho}(\alpha,\alpha)=\alpha
for all
\alpha\neq\rho
, while
\Sigma_{\rho}(\rho,S_{\rho})=S_{\rho}
and
\Sigma_{\rho}(\alpha,\beta)=0
in all other cases. That is,

\Sigma_{\rho}=\begin{pmatrix}[c|cc]&\alpha\neq\rho&\rho\\
\hline\cr\beta\neq\rho&\delta_{\alpha=\beta}\alpha&0\\
S_{\rho}&0&S_{\rho}\\
\overline{\rho}&0&0\end{pmatrix}

with

\widetilde{i}_{\Sigma}(\alpha,\beta)=\widetilde{i}_{\mathcal{C}}(\alpha,\beta)-s(\beta),

where
s(\beta)=0
if
\beta\not\in I
while
s(S_{\rho})=-1
and
s(\overline{\rho})=-2
.

Here
\delta_{\alpha=\beta}\alpha
means this term is zero unless
\alpha=\beta
, in which case it is given by
\alpha
. It is a straightforward definition-push to verify that
\mathcal{S}
is a bimodule, and the nontrivial parts of the argument are similar in spirit to parts of Theorem
5.3
: the key point is that
B(\rho,\beta)
and
\mathcal{C}(\rho,\beta)
define the same geometric chain in
\rho\times\beta
.
∎

Proposition 5.20
.

For the
SO(3)
-equivariant bimodule
\Sigma_{\rho}:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{S}_{\rho}(\mathcal{C})
defined above, the induced chain map

CM_{*}(\Sigma_{\rho}):CM_{*}(\mathcal{C})\to CM_{*}(\mathcal{S}_{\rho})

is an isomorphism on homology.

Proof.

The induced map on chains sends each
C_{*}(\alpha)
identically onto
C_{*}(\alpha)
whenever
\alpha\neq\rho
, and on the remaining factor is the map
\Sigma:C_{*}(\rho)\to\mathsf{Cone}
discussed before the construction above.

To see that
CM_{*}(\Sigma_{\rho})
induces an isomorphism on homology, we will use a spectral sequence argument using ‘periodic filtrations’; see Remark
4.2
for more details on these and their associated spectral sequences. We give
CM_{*}(\mathcal{C})
and
CM_{*}(\mathcal{S})
periodic filtrations, with filtration on the unrolled complex given by

F_{k}\widetilde{CM}_{*}(\mathcal{C})=\bigoplus_{\widetilde{i}_{\mathcal{C}}(\alpha)\leq k}C_{*}(\alpha)[\widetilde{i}_{\mathcal{C}}(\alpha)]\subset\widetilde{CM}_{*}(\mathcal{C}),

F_{k}\widetilde{CM}_{*}(\mathcal{S})=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\mathsf{Ob}(\mathcal{S})\\
\widetilde{i}_{\mathcal{C}}(\alpha)\leq k\end{subarray}}C_{*}(\alpha)[\widetilde{i}_{\mathcal{S}}(\alpha)]\subset\widetilde{CM}_{*}(\mathcal{S}),

where in the second expression we filter by the
\mathcal{C}
-index of an object of
\mathcal{S}
; that is, we filter by

\widetilde{i}_{\mathcal{C}}(S_{\rho})=\widetilde{i}_{\mathcal{C}}(\overline{\rho})=\widetilde{i}_{\mathcal{C}}(\rho)

for
\rho\in I
and by the usual
\widetilde{i}_{\mathcal{C}}(\alpha)
for
\alpha\neq\rho
.

The associated graded complex in the first case is
\bigoplus_{\alpha\in\mathsf{Ob}(\mathcal{C})}C_{*}(\alpha)[\widetilde{i}_{\mathcal{C}}(\alpha)]
with differential the direct sum of the geometric chain complex differentials. The associated graded complex in the second case is

\bigoplus_{\alpha\neq\rho}C_{*}(\alpha)[\widetilde{i}_{\mathcal{C}}(\alpha)]\oplus\mathsf{Cone}[\widetilde{i}_{\mathcal{C}}(\rho)].

Because the induced map on associated graded complexes is the identity on
C_{*}(\alpha)
for
\alpha\neq\rho
, and on
C_{*}(\rho)
is given by the map
\Sigma
described above, we see that
\Sigma_{\rho}
induces an isomorphism on the homology of the associated graded complexes. Hence (because the spectral sequence associated to this periodic filtration is absolutely convergent) the map
CM_{*}(\Sigma_{\rho})
induces an isomorphism on
HM_{*}
.
∎

Therefore, for the purposes of equivariant cohomology groups — which are preserved under
SO(3)
-equivariant quasi-isomorphisms — we may freely pass between
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{S}_{\rho}
. The corresponding flow complexes are not, however, equivariantly homotopy equivalent. This will be discussed further in Section 7 below, where it will be used to provide a wall-crossing formula of sorts for Floer’s non-equivariant instanton homology groups.

Any bimodule
(\mathcal{S}_{\rho}\mathcal{C})\to\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
induces a bimodule
\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
by precomposition with
\Sigma_{\rho}
. This leads us to ask: when does an
SO(3)
-equivariant bimodule
\mathcal{W}:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
enjoy a lift to an
SO(3)
-equivariant bimodule
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}:\mathcal{S}_{\rho}\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
for which
\mathcal{W}=\widehat{\mathcal{W}}\circ\Sigma_{\rho}
?

This is not always the case (for instance, this is not possible for the identity bimodule). It turns out that this is true precisely when a certain index condition holds.

Construction 5.21
.

Suppose
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
are flow categories satisfying the assumptions at the beginning of this section, and suppose we have chosen an orbit
\rho
of type
S^{2}
in
\mathsf{Ob}(\mathcal{C})
, an
SO(3)
-equivariant complex line bundle
\mathcal{H}_{\rho}\to\rho
, as well a compatible family of equivariant transverse sections
\psi_{\rho\alpha}:\mathcal{C}(\rho,\alpha)\to\mathcal{H}_{\rho}
lifting the endpoint map to
\rho
.

Suppose
\mathcal{W}:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
is an
SO(3)
-equivariant bimodule, satisfying the following condition. For each
\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Ob}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime})
there is a stratified-smooth subspace
\mathcal{W}^{\text{red}}(\rho,\alpha^{\prime})
of those points with stabilizer conjugate to
SO(2)
.

We demand that at each
\Lambda\in\mathcal{W}^{\text{red}}(\rho,\alpha^{\prime})
, the normal space to this locus is positive-dimension. It follows that the normal space has dimension at least two, as this normal space is an
SO(2)
-space with weight one
SO(2)
-action.

For each
\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Ob}(\mathcal{C}^{\prime})
, choose an
SO(3)
-equivariant transverse section

\psi^{W}_{\rho\alpha^{\prime}}:\mathcal{W}(\rho,\alpha^{\prime})\to\mathcal{H}_{\rho}.

On the boundary face
\mathcal{C}(\rho,\alpha)\times_{\alpha}\mathcal{W}(\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})
we demand this is given by the section
\psi_{\rho\alpha^{\prime}}\times 0
chosen above, while on the boundary face
\mathcal{W}(\rho,\beta^{\prime})\times_{\beta^{\prime}}\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(\beta^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})
we demand this is given by
\psi^{W}_{\rho^{\prime}\beta}\times 0
.

If there were no reducibles in these moduli spaces, these would be straightforward to construct by induction; at a reducible of type
S^{2}
, the construction of such sections is possible by our assumption that each reducible has non-trivial normal space. This corresponds to the fact that there exists an
SO(2)
-equivariant map
f:\mathbb{C}^{n}\to\mathbb{C}
which is transverse to zero if and only if
n\geq 1
.

We write
B(\mathcal{W};\rho,\alpha^{\prime})
and
Z(\mathcal{W};\rho,\alpha^{\prime})
for the blowup and zero locus, respectively, of
\psi^{W}_{\rho\alpha^{\prime}}
over
\mathcal{W}(\rho,\alpha^{\prime})
.

Given this, one may define a bimodule
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}:\mathcal{S}_{\rho}\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
by the formula

\widehat{\mathcal{W}}(\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})=\begin{pmatrix}[c|ccc]&\alpha\neq S_{\rho},\overline{\rho}&\alpha=S_{\rho}&\alpha=\overline{\rho}\\
\hline\cr\alpha^{\prime}&\mathcal{W}(\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})&B(\mathcal{W};\rho,\alpha^{\prime})&Z(\mathcal{W};\rho,\alpha^{\prime})\end{pmatrix}

Here

\widetilde{i}_{\widehat{\mathcal{W}}}(\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})=\widetilde{i}_{\mathcal{W}}(\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})-s(\alpha),

where the shift
s
is defined by the same formula as above.
∎

This defines a bimodule lifting
\mathcal{W}
.

Again, here is a diagram which may be helpful when verifying the bimodule relations. The left column depicts the flow category
