\mathcal{S}_{\rho}\mathcal{C}
, while the right column depicts the flow category
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
, and the arrows running left to right depict the bimodule
\mathcal{W}
. As before, the bimodule relation for
\partial\mathcal{W}(\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})
amounts to saying that the boundary of any arrow running left to right is the signed sum over all two-step paths from
\alpha
to
\alpha^{\prime}
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\alpha/\beta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S_{\rho}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\alpha^{\prime}/\beta^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{\rho}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{W}(\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}(\alpha,\rho)\times_{\rho}S_{\rho}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{\rho}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z(\rho,\beta)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B(\mathcal{W};\rho,\alpha^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z(\mathcal{W};\rho,\alpha^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-B(\rho,\beta)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}(\alpha,\beta)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}^{\prime}(\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})}

Theorem 5.22
.

The formulas above define a bimodule
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}:\mathcal{S}_{\rho}\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{I}
. This bimodule satisfies
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}\circ\Sigma_{\rho}=\mathcal{W}
.

Proof.

This follows by an analysis much like Theorem
5.3
, though one needs to verify
fewer
relations. Once again, one repeatedly uses the fact that
S_{\rho}\times_{S_{\rho}}B(\mathcal{W};\rho,\alpha^{\prime})
is collapse-equivalent to
\mathcal{W}(\rho,\alpha^{\prime})
so defines the same geometric chain, as well as the boundary formula for
B(\mathcal{W};\rho,\alpha^{\prime})
given by Lemma
A.3
and the boundary formula for
\mathcal{W}(\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})
given by the definition of bimodule.

The statement about the composite bimodule follows nearly immediately from the relevant definitions. The only term in this composite where this isn’t tautological is

(\widehat{\mathcal{W}}\circ\Sigma_{\rho})(\rho,\alpha^{\prime})=S_{\rho}\times_{S_{\rho}}B(\mathcal{W};\rho,\alpha^{\prime})=B(\mathcal{W};\rho,\alpha^{\prime})\textup{ as a chain in }\rho\times\alpha^{\prime},

which we know coincides with
\mathcal{W}(\rho,\alpha^{\prime})
at the level of geometric chains, as desired.
∎

5.4
The bimodule of an obstructed cobordism

We will now show that the modified moduli spaces of Section 5.2 give rise to a bimodule
to
the suspended flow category, opposite to the situation of Theorem
5.3
.

Let
(W,c):(Y,w,\pi)\to(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime},\pi^{\prime})
be a nearly unobstructed cobordism whose obstructed reducible we denote
\Lambda:\rho\to\rho^{\prime}
. In Section 5.2, we constructed ‘modified moduli spaces’
N^{+}_{z}(W,c;\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})
. Our first goal in this section will be to use these to construct a bimodule

\mathcal{W}_{-}:\mathcal{I}(Y,w,\pi)\to\mathcal{S}_{\rho}\mathcal{I}(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime},\pi^{\prime}).

The minus sign indicates that
\widetilde{i}(\Lambda)=-2
, so that index
decreases
across this obstructed reducible.

To make the notation more parseable, let us make some abbreviations. We write
M(\alpha,\beta)=\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta}(Y,w;\alpha,\beta)
, while
M^{\prime}(\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})
is the same for
(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime})
.

There are canonical equivariant diffeomorphisms
\rho\xleftarrow{e_{-}^{\Lambda}}\mathcal{O}_{\Lambda}\xrightarrow{e_{+}^{\Lambda}}\rho^{\prime}
. We use these diffeomorphisms to pull back
\mathcal{H}_{\Lambda}
and
S_{\Lambda}
to bundles over
\rho^{\prime}
, and we refer to the map
e_{+}^{\rho}:\rho\to\rho^{\prime}
for the map
e_{+}^{\rho}=e_{+}^{\Lambda}\circ(e_{-}^{\Lambda})^{-1}
obtained by inverting the first arrow in the above zig-zag. Every appearance of
M\times_{\rho}\mathcal{O}_{\Lambda}
we replace with
M
in a canonical way, and similarly we replace
\mathcal{O}_{\Lambda}\times_{\rho^{\prime}}M^{\prime}
with
M^{\prime}
.

As in section 5.2 and the previous section, we choose a compatible family of transverse equivariant sections

\psi_{\alpha\rho}:M(\alpha,\rho)\to\mathcal{H}_{\rho^{\prime}},\quad\psi^{\prime}_{\rho^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}}:M^{\prime}(\rho^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})\to\mathcal{H}_{\rho^{\prime}}.

We write
B(\alpha,\rho)
and
B^{\prime}(\rho^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})
for the blowups along these sections, and
Z(\alpha,\rho)
and
Z^{\prime}(\rho^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})
for the zero loci of these sections; the family of sections described here plays the role of
\psi^{\prime}_{\rho^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}}
in Section 5.3. Both
B(\alpha,\rho)
and
B^{\prime}(\rho^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})
come with canonical maps to
S_{\rho^{\prime}}
.

In Section 5.2, these were the sections
\psi^{\text{priv}}_{\zeta\Lambda}
, where
\zeta
is the unique path with
-1\leq i(\zeta)\leq 1
.

When defining the suspended flow category, we will use
\psi^{\prime}_{\rho^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}}=-\psi^{\text{priv}}_{\zeta\Lambda}
. This minus sign makes no difference at the level of zero sets, but does change the map
B(\psi)\to S_{\rho^{\prime}}
; it will be clear momentarily why this minus sign is important.

Finally, for the modified moduli spaces
N^{+}_{\zeta}(W,c;\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})
, we abbreviate this expression to
N(\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}).
In indices below, we suppress the symbols
w
in
(Y,w)
and
c
in
(W,c)
for ease of expression.

Construction 5.23
.

We define the bimodule
\mathcal{W}_{-}:\mathcal{I}\to\mathcal{S}_{\rho^{\prime}}\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
by the following formula.

\mathcal{W}_{-}(\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})=\begin{pmatrix}[c|cc]&\alpha\neq\rho&\alpha=\rho\\
\hline\cr&&\\
\alpha^{\prime}\neq\rho^{\prime}&N(\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})&N(\rho,\alpha^{\prime})\\
&&\\
\alpha^{\prime}=S_{\rho^{\prime}}&N(\alpha,\rho^{\prime})\times_{\rho^{\prime}}S_{\rho^{\prime}}-(-1)^{\widetilde{i}(Y;\alpha,\rho)}B(\alpha,\rho)&0\\
&&\\
\alpha^{\prime}=\overline{\rho}^{\prime}&0&\rho\end{pmatrix}

Here we have

\widetilde{i}_{\mathcal{W}_{-}}(\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})=\widetilde{i}(W;\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})+s(\alpha^{\prime}),

where
s(\overline{\rho}^{\prime})=2
and
s(S_{\rho^{\prime}})=1
, while
s(\alpha^{\prime})=0
otherwise. The bottom-right entry is the correspondence
\rho\xleftarrow{1_{\rho}}\rho\xrightarrow{e_{+}^{\rho}}\rho^{\prime}
induced by the endpoint maps of
\mathcal{O}_{\Lambda}
which was described above.
∎

Remark 5.24
.

Entry
(2,1)
in this bimodule may be unexpected. What we will see in the calculation below is that this particular form is forced upon us by demanding that
\mathcal{W}_{-}(\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})=N(\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})
whenever possible.

One could place this expression in its proper context by proving a more general statement: if
\mathcal{N}(\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})
is a collection of moduli spaces satisfying the boundary relations obtained in Proposition
5.2
, then it assembles to give a bimodule
\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{S}_{\rho^{\prime}}\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
by formulas along the lines of the above, and conversely every bimodule
\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{S}_{\rho^{\prime}}\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
arises in this way. We will not bother formulating or proving such a statement.
∎

Before writing out the proof, let’s recall some of the important boundary relations here. Proposition
5.2
can be interpreted as giving the following boundary formula, at the level of geometric chains; the passage to geometric chains allows us to cancel out faces with opposite orientation, and equate things which are collapse-equivalent.

When
\alpha\neq\rho
and
\alpha^{\prime}\neq\rho^{\prime}
, we have the boundary relation

\displaystyle(-1)^{\dim\alpha}\partial N(\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})=

\displaystyle\sum_{\beta}M(\alpha,\beta)\times_{\beta}N(\beta,\alpha^{\prime})

\displaystyle+\sum_{\beta^{\prime}}(-1)^{\widetilde{i}(W;\beta,\alpha^{\prime})+1}N(\alpha,\beta^{\prime})\times_{\beta^{\prime}}M^{\prime}(\beta^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})

\displaystyle+B(\alpha,\rho)\times_{S_{\rho^{\prime}}}B^{\prime}(\rho^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime}).

The final term deserves some comment. It appears in Proposition
5.2
as a negatively-oriented copy of the zero set of the family
t\psi_{\alpha\rho}-(1-t)\psi^{\prime}_{\rho^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}}
on the pullback of
\mathcal{H}_{\Lambda}
over

M(\alpha,\rho)\times[0,1]\times_{\rho^{\prime}}M^{\prime}(\rho^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime}).

Applying the orientation-reversing isomorphism
t\mapsto 1-t
(which flips the negative orientation) on the unit interval and negating the section (which does not change the orientation on the zero set), this section is sent to
(1-t)\psi_{\alpha\rho}-t\psi^{\prime}_{\rho^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}}
. We show in Lemma
A.3
in the appendix that the zero set of this section is identical as a geometric chain to the fiber product
B(\alpha,\rho)\times_{S_{\rho^{\prime}}}B^{\prime}(\rho^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime}),

giving the statement written above. (This is where it is important to use
\psi^{\prime}_{\rho^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}}=-\psi^{\text{priv}}_{\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}
; with the opposite sign, Lemma
A.3
would not be applicable.)

If
\alpha\neq\rho
and
\alpha^{\prime}=\rho
, the last term is instead
Z(\rho,\alpha)
. If
\alpha=\rho
and
\alpha^{\prime}\neq\rho^{\prime}
, the final term is instead
-Z^{\prime}(\rho^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})
.

In the diagram below, the left column corresponds to the flow category
\mathcal{C}
, while the right column corresponds to the flow category
\mathcal{S}_{\rho^{\prime}}\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
. The bimodule relations for
\mathcal{W}_{-}(\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})
assert that the boundary of theh arrow running from (say)
\alpha
to
\alpha^{\prime}
is the signed sum of the fiber product over all ways to go from
\alpha
to
\alpha^{\prime}
in two steps, one along
\mathcal{W}_{-}
and one inside either the initial or ending flow category.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\alpha^{\prime}/\beta^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\alpha/\beta}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S_{\rho^{\prime}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{\rho}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{\rho^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\breve{M}^{\prime}(\alpha^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})\times_{\rho^{\prime}}S_{\rho^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z^{\prime}(\rho^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-B^{\prime}(\rho^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N(\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}_{-}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\breve{M}(\alpha,\rho)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\breve{M}(\rho,\beta)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N(\alpha,\rho^{\prime})\times_{\rho^{\prime}}S_{\rho^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-(-1)^{\widetilde{\zeta}}B(\beta,\rho)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N(\rho,\alpha^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\breve{M}(\alpha,\beta)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\breve{M}^{\prime}(\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})}

Theorem 5.25
.

For
(W,c)
a cobordism as in the beginning of this section, the moduli spaces
\mathcal{W}_{-}
defined above give a bimodule
\mathcal{W}_{-}:\mathcal{I}\to\mathcal{S}_{\rho^{\prime}}\mathcal{I}^{\prime}.

Proof.

The statement that
\mathcal{W}_{-}
gives a bimodule amounts to six boundary formulas. We will only verify two of these; the rest follow by nearly identical arguments, and are furthermore similar in spirit to the verifications in Theorem
5.3
.

•

First (filling in the values of
\mathcal{W}_{-}
), the bimodule relations for
\mathcal{W}_{-}(\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})
for
\alpha\neq\rho
and
\alpha^{\prime}\neq\overline{\rho}^{\prime},S_{\rho^{\prime}}
stipulate that

\displaystyle(-1)^{\dim\alpha}\partial N(\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\beta\in\mathfrak{C}(Y,w)}M(\alpha,\beta)\times_{\beta}N(\beta,\alpha^{\prime})

\displaystyle+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{C}(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime})\\
\beta^{\prime}\neq\rho^{\prime}\end{subarray}}(-1)^{\widetilde{i}_{\mathcal{W}_{-}}(\alpha,\beta^{\prime})+1}N(\alpha,\beta^{\prime})\times_{\beta^{\prime}}M^{\prime}(\beta^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})

\displaystyle+(-1)^{\widetilde{i}_{\mathcal{W}_{-}}(\alpha,S_{\rho^{\prime}})+1}N(\alpha,\rho^{\prime})\times_{\rho^{\prime}}S_{\rho^{\prime}}\times_{S_{\rho^{\prime}}}\big(-B^{\prime}(\rho^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})\big)

\displaystyle+(-1)^{\widetilde{i}_{\mathcal{W}_{-}}(\alpha,S_{\rho^{\prime}})+1}(-1)^{\widetilde{i}(Y;\alpha,\rho)+1}B(\alpha,\rho)\times_{S_{\rho^{\prime}}}\big(-B^{\prime}(\rho^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})\big).

The third and last line arise from
\mathcal{W}_{-}(\alpha,S_{\rho^{\prime}})\times_{S_{\rho^{\prime}}}\mathcal{S}_{\rho^{\prime}}\mathcal{I}^{\prime}(S_{\rho^{\prime}},\alpha^{\prime})
. Our first order of business is to clean up these last few lines. First,
\widetilde{i}_{\mathcal{W}_{-}}(\rho,\overline{\rho}^{\prime})=0
. Next,
\widetilde{i}_{\mathcal{W}_{-}}(\alpha,S_{\rho^{\prime}})=\widetilde{i}(W;\alpha,\rho^{\prime})+1
while

\displaystyle N(W;\alpha,\rho^{\prime})\times_{\rho^{\prime}}S_{\rho^{\prime}}\times_{S_{\rho^{\prime}}}\big(-B^{\prime}(\rho^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})\big)

\displaystyle=-N(W;\alpha,\rho^{\prime})\times_{\rho^{\prime}}B^{\prime}(\rho^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})

\displaystyle=-N(W;\alpha,\rho^{\prime})\times_{\rho^{\prime}}M^{\prime}(\rho^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime}),

because by Lemma
A.3
the chains
B^{\prime}(\rho^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})
and
M^{\prime}(\rho^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})
are identical when considered as geometric chains in
\rho^{\prime}\times\alpha^{\prime}
. Lastly, the final line may be simplified with the observation that

\widetilde{i}_{\mathcal{W}_{-}}(\alpha,S_{\rho^{\prime}})+1-\widetilde{i}(Y;\alpha,\rho)-1=\widetilde{i}(W;\alpha,\rho^{\prime})+1-\widetilde{i}(Y;\alpha,\rho)=\widetilde{i}(W;\rho,\rho^{\prime})=-2

so that the sign out front is
-1
; pulling the factor of
-1
from
-B^{\prime}(\rho^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})
out front as well, this reads

\displaystyle(-1)^{\dim\alpha}\partial N(\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\beta\in\mathfrak{C}(Y,w)}M(\alpha,\beta)\times_{\beta}N(\beta,\alpha^{\prime})

\displaystyle+\sum_{\beta^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{C}(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime})}(-1)^{\widetilde{i}(W;\alpha,\beta^{\prime})+1}N(\alpha,\beta^{\prime})\times_{\beta^{\prime}}M^{\prime}(\beta^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})

\displaystyle+B(\alpha,\rho)\times_{S_{\rho^{\prime}}}B^{\prime}(\rho^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime}).

But this is precisely the relation written above, coming from Proposition
5.2
(iii).

•

Let’s verify the bimodule relation for the other interesting case,
\mathcal{W}_{-}(\alpha,S_{\rho^{\prime}})
where
\alpha\neq\rho
. To briefly describe what happens:
N(\alpha,\rho^{\prime})\times_{\rho^{\prime}}S_{\rho^{\prime}}
carries an extra boundary term
Z(\alpha,\rho^{\prime})\times_{\rho^{\prime}}S_{\rho^{\prime}}
; the boundary relation from the moduli space
B(\alpha,\rho)
which appears in
\mathcal{W}_{-}(\alpha,S_{\rho^{\prime}})
will ‘cancel it out’. To be more precise, the bimodule relations stipulate that

\displaystyle(-1)^{\dim\alpha}\partial\mathcal{W}_{-}(\alpha,S_{\rho^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta\in\mathfrak{C}(Y,w)\\
\alpha\neq\beta\neq\rho\end{subarray}}\mathcal{I}(\alpha,\beta)\times_{\beta}\mathcal{W}_{-}(\beta,S_{\rho^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{C}(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime})\\
\alpha^{\prime}\neq\rho^{\prime}\end{subarray}}(-1)^{\widetilde{i}_{\mathcal{W}_{-}}(\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})+1}\mathcal{W}_{-}(\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})\times_{\alpha^{\prime}}\mathcal{S}_{\rho^{\prime}}\mathcal{I}^{\prime}(\alpha^{\prime},S_{\rho^{\prime}}).

(The last sum does not include a factorization through
\mathcal{S}_{\rho^{\prime}}\mathcal{I}(\overline{\rho}^{\prime},S_{\rho^{\prime}})
because this moduli space is empty.) The term
\mathcal{I}(\alpha,\beta)
is
M(\alpha,\beta)
; the term
\mathcal{S}_{\rho^{\prime}}\mathcal{I}^{\prime}(\alpha^{\prime},S_{\rho^{\prime}})
is
M^{\prime}(\alpha^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})\times_{\rho^{\prime}}S_{\rho^{\prime}}.
Filling in the values of
\mathcal{W}_{-}
, the right-hand-side simplifies to

\displaystyle\sum_{\beta\neq\rho}M(\alpha,\beta)\times_{\beta}N(\beta,\rho^{\prime})\times_{\rho^{\prime}}S_{\rho^{\prime}}-(-1)^{\widetilde{i}(Y;\beta,\rho)}M(\alpha,\beta)\times_{\beta}B(\beta,\rho)

\displaystyle+\sum_{\alpha^{\prime}\neq\rho^{\prime}}(-1)^{\widetilde{i}(W;\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})+1}N(\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})\times_{\alpha^{\prime}}M^{\prime}(\alpha^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})\times_{\rho^{\prime}}S_{\rho^{\prime}}.

The left-hand side of the bimodule relation reads

(-1)^{\dim\alpha}\partial\left(N(\alpha,\rho^{\prime})\times_{\rho^{\prime}}S_{\rho^{\prime}}-(-1)^{\widetilde{i}(Y;\alpha,\rho)}B(\alpha,\rho)\right).

Now use the boundary relation for
(-1)^{\dim\alpha}\partial N(\alpha,\rho^{\prime})
stated before this Proposition; most every term in that boundary relation is represented in the sum above, except for the final term corresponding to the boundary face
Z(\alpha,\rho)
.

Subtracting
(-1)^{\dim\alpha}\partial N(\alpha,\rho^{\prime})\times_{\rho^{\prime}}S_{\rho^{\prime}}
from both sides, what we’re left with trying to show is that

(-1)^{\dim\alpha+\widetilde{i}(Y;\alpha,\rho)-1}\partial B(\alpha,\rho)=-Z(\alpha,\rho)\times_{\rho}S_{\rho}+\sum_{\beta}(-1)^{\widetilde{i}(Y;\beta,\rho)}M(\alpha,\beta)\times_{\beta}B(\beta,\rho)\times_{\rho}S_{\rho^{\prime}}.

Multiplying through by
(-1)^{\dim\alpha+\widetilde{i}(Y;\alpha,\rho)}-1
, what we assert is that

\partial B(\alpha,\rho)=(-1)^{\dim\alpha+\widetilde{i}(Y;\alpha,\rho)}Z(\alpha,\rho)\times_{\rho}S_{\rho}+\sum_{\beta}(-1)^{\dim\alpha+\widetilde{i}(Y;\alpha,\beta)}M(\alpha,\beta)\times_{\beta}B(\beta,\rho).

Finally, this relation is given in Lemma
A.3
: the final term is
B(\psi|_{\partial M(\alpha,\rho)})
, while the first term is
(-1)^{\dim Z(\alpha,\rho)+1}Z(\alpha,\rho)\times_{\rho}S_{\rho}
, as
\dim Z(\alpha,\rho)=\widetilde{i}(Y;\alpha,\rho)+\dim\alpha-1.

Carrying out a similar discussion in all the other cases is similar but strictly simpler.
∎

5.5
Invariance of instanton homology

Recall from Section
3.1
that associated to each weakly admissible pair
(Y,w)
and each regular perturbation
\pi
is a function
\sigma_{\pi}:\mathfrak{A}(Y,w)\to 2\mathbb{Z}
, called its
signature data
.

Recall also from Section
4.3
that associated to each
(Y,w,\pi)
with basepoint
b
is an
SO(3)
-equivariant flow category
\mathcal{I}(Y,w,\pi)
, whose associated flow complex we call
\widetilde{C}(Y,w,\pi)
, and that for each unobstructed cobordism
(W,c):(Y,w,\pi)\to(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime},\pi^{\prime})
with regular perturbation
\pi_{W}
, homology orientation of
W
, and path between the basepoints, we associate a bimodule
\mathcal{I}(W,c,\pi_{W},\gamma)
and thus an
SO(3)
-equivariant chain map

\widetilde{C}(W,c,\pi_{W},\gamma):\widetilde{C}(Y,w,\pi)\to\widetilde{C}(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime},\pi^{\prime}),

independent of
\pi_{W}
up to equivariant chain-homotopy. Furthermore, these compose functorially (up to equivariant chain homotopy).

Taking
(W,c)=\mathbb{R}\times(Y,w)
(with trivial homology orientation and path
\gamma=\mathbb{R}\times b
) and applying the index computation Proposition
3.5
, this cobordism is unobstructed if and only if we have the inequality on signature data functions
\sigma_{\pi}\leq\sigma_{\pi^{\prime}}
. In that case, we obtain an
SO(3)
-equivariant continuation map

\varphi_{\pi\to\pi^{\prime}}:\widetilde{C}(Y,w,\pi)\to\widetilde{C}(Y,w,\pi^{\prime}).

Our goal for the remainder of this section is to prove the following.

Theorem 5.26
.

Whenever
\pi_{0},\pi_{1}
are two regular perturbations on
(Y,w)
with
\sigma_{\pi_{0}}\leq\sigma_{\pi_{1}}
, the continuation map

\varphi_{\pi_{0}\to\pi_{1}}:\widetilde{I}(Y,w,\pi_{0})\to\widetilde{I}(Y,w,\pi_{1})

is an isomorphism.

Proof of Theorem
5.5
.

When
\sigma_{\pi_{0}}=\sigma_{\pi_{1}}
this follows from the machinery of Section
4.3
: there are continuation maps
\varphi_{\pi_{0}\to\pi_{1}}
and
\varphi_{\pi_{0}\to\pi_{1}}
which are equivariantly homotopy inverse, and hence each is an equivariant homotopy equivalence. The theorem above is primary interesting when
\sigma_{\pi_{0}}<\sigma_{\pi_{1}}
.

We will only need to prove this in a special case. We say that
\pi_{0}
and
\pi_{1}
have
adjacent signature data
, meaning that there exists a reducible
\rho
with
\sigma_{\pi_{1}}=\sigma_{\pi_{0}}+4\delta_{\rho}
; that is,
\sigma_{\pi_{0}}
is equal to
\sigma_{\pi_{1}}
for all reducibles except
\rho
, where
\sigma_{\pi_{1}}
is larger by the smallest possible amount (
4
).

To see why this is sufficient, recall that Proposition
3.1
shows that any signature data function is realized as the signature data of some regular perturbation on
(Y,w)
. It follows that whenever
\sigma_{\pi_{0}}<\sigma_{\pi_{1}}
, we may find a sequence of intermediate perturbations
\pi_{0},\pi_{1/n},\cdots,\pi_{(n-1)/n},\pi_{1}
so that

\sigma_{\pi_{0}}<\cdots<\sigma_{\pi_{1}},

where each
(\pi_{i/n},\pi_{(i+1)/n})
have adjacent signature data. Because we can write the continuation map
\varphi_{\pi_{0}\to\pi_{1}}
as a composite of the individual continuation maps
\varphi_{\pi_{i/n}\to\pi_{(i+1)/n}}
, if the continuation map is an isomorphism for perturbations with adjacent signature data, it is an isomorphism whenever
\sigma_{\pi_{0}}\leq\sigma_{\pi_{1}}
.

So suppose that
\pi_{0}
and
\pi_{1}
have adjacent signature data. Write
W_{+}
for the cylinder
\mathbb{R}\times(Y,c)
, considered as a cobordism
(Y,w,\pi_{0})\to(Y,w,\pi_{1})
equipped with a regular perturbation
\pi_{+}
interpolating between these, and
W_{-}
for the same cylinder considered as a cobordism in the opposite direction
(Y,w,\pi_{1})\to(Y,w,\pi_{0})
equipped with an obstructed-regular perturbation
\pi_{-}
interpolating between these.

The cobordism
W_{-}
is a nearly unobstructed cobordism (its reducibles correspond to the constant trajectories on the cylinder, so their normal index is given by
\frac{1}{2}(\sigma_{\pi_{0}}(\rho)-\sigma_{\pi_{1}}(\rho)
), and the one obstruced reducible
\Lambda:\rho\to\rho
has normal index
-2
), and thus by the machinery of the previous section we obtain a bimodule

\mathcal{W}_{-}:\mathcal{I}(Y,w,\pi_{1})\to\mathcal{S}_{\rho}\mathcal{I}(Y,w,\pi_{0}).

We write
\varphi_{-}
for the induced map on flow complexes

\varphi_{+}:\widetilde{C}(Y,w,\pi_{1})\to S_{\rho}\widetilde{C}(Y,w,\pi_{0}).

On the other hand, the cobordism
W_{+}
satisfies the hypotheses of Construction
5.3
: the only reducible running
\rho\to\rho
has normal index
2
. We thus obtain a bimodule
\mathcal{W}_{+}
running in the opposite direction, and we write
\varphi_{+}
for the induced map on flow complexes.

Abbreviating
\mathcal{I}_{0}=\mathcal{I}(Y,w,\pi_{0})
while
\mathcal{I}_{1}=\mathcal{I}(Y,w,\pi_{1})
, we obtain the following diagram of flow categories and bimodules:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{I}_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{I}_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{S}_{\rho}\mathcal{I}_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{W}_{\pi_{0}\to\pi_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma_{\rho}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{W}_{+}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{W}_{-}}

Here
\mathcal{W}_{\pi_{0}\to\pi_{1}}
is the bimodule obtained from the usual (unmodified) moduli spaces of instantons on the unobstructed cobordism
\mathbb{R}\times Y:(Y,w,\pi_{0})\to(Y,w,\pi_{1})
; its induced map on the complex
\widetilde{C}
is the continuation map we named
\varphi_{\pi_{0}\to\pi_{1}}
above. We established in Theorem
5.3
that we have an equality of composites

\mathcal{W}_{+}\circ\Sigma_{\rho}=\mathcal{W}_{\pi_{0}\to\pi_{1}},

so that on flow complexes we have
\varphi_{+}\circ\Sigma_{\rho}=\varphi_{\pi_{0}\to\pi_{1}}
. We established in Proposition
5.3
that
\Sigma_{\rho}
induces an isomorphism on homology, so we are reduced to showing that
\varphi_{+}
is an isomorphism on homology. Indeed, we will prove that
\varphi_{+}
is an equivariant chain homotopy equivalence.

To see this, we assert that there exist
SO(3)
-equivariant bimodule homotopies

\mathcal{H}_{1}:\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{I}_{1}}\implies\mathcal{W}_{+}\circ\mathcal{W}_{-},\quad\mathcal{H}_{0}:\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{S}_{\rho}\mathcal{I}_{0}}\implies\mathcal{W}_{-}\circ\mathcal{W}_{+}.

It follows that the induced maps
\varphi_{+}
and
\varphi_{-}
are equivariantly homotopy inverse, as desired.

The construction of these bimodule homotopies is carried out in Section 5.5.2 below, based on the analytic setup of Section 5.5.1. The fact that they are indeed bimodule homotopies are verified in Propositions
5.5.2
and
5.5.2
in Section 5.5.2.
∎

Now fix a PID
R
. Associated to the
C_{*}(SO(3);R)
-module
\widetilde{C}
(together with its periodic filtration by index
\widetilde{i}
) are three equivariant homology groups
H^{+},H^{-},H^{\infty}
, which fit into an exact triangle; each of these are modules over
H^{-*}(BSO(3);R)
. A (periodic filtration-preserving) chain map
\widetilde{C}\to\widetilde{C}^{\prime}
induces a map on all equivariant homology groups, which commutes with the structure maps in the exact triangle. If the equivariant map
\widetilde{C}\to\widetilde{C}^{\prime}
is a quasi-isomorphism, it induces an isomorphism on all equivariant homology groups.

These constructions for periodically-filtered and periodically-graded dg-modules were first described in
[
25
, Appendix A.8]
; essentially, one unwraps
\widetilde{C}
into a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded complex
\bar{C}
, uses this to filter certain complexes
C^{+}(\bar{C}),C^{-}(\bar{C}),C^{\infty}(\bar{C})
arising from
\mathbb{Z}
-graded equivariant homological algebra, and performs a completion with this filtration to extract well-behaved equivariant homology groups (well-behaved meaning they satisfy the properties above).

However, the filtration used there to define
H^{\infty}
fails to be exhaustive, and so the completion procedure used in
[
25
, Appendix A.8]
does not produce a well-behaved homology group
H^{\infty}
(in particular, one fitting into an exact triangle and preserved by equivariant quasi-isomorphisms). One should instead use the completion procedure of
[
28
, Section 5.4]
, called
full completion
; in doing so, one recovers all the properties claimed in
[
25
, Theorem A.29]
, and this is explicitly stated as
[
28
, Proposition 5.40]
.

We write
I^{\bullet}(Y,w,\pi;R)
to mean any the various equivariant instanton homology groups
\widetilde{I},I^{+},I^{-},I^{\infty}
associated to the complex
\widetilde{C}(Y,w,\pi;R)
.

Corollary 5.27

(Instanton homology is well-defined)
.

The equivariant instanton homology groups
I^{\bullet}(Y,w,\pi;R)
are independent of regular perturbation
\pi
, as well as their module structures and the exact triangle relating them.

Proof.

Given any two regular perturbations
\pi,\pi^{\prime}
with
\sigma_{\pi}\leq\sigma_{\pi^{\prime}}
, we showed above that the natural map
\varphi_{\pi\to\pi^{\prime}}:\widetilde{C}(Y,w,\pi;R)\to\widetilde{C}(Y,w,\pi^{\prime};R)
induces an isomorphism on homology. By the discussion before this corollary,
\varphi_{\pi\to\pi^{\prime}}
also induces an isomorphism on all groups
I^{\bullet}(Y,w,\pi;R)
, which is a module map that commutes with the structure maps in the exact triangle.

Because any signature-data function is realized as the signature data of some regular perturbation, and the maximum of two signature data functions is again a signature data function, given any two regular perturbations
\pi_{0},\pi_{1}
, there is a regular perturbation
\pi_{2}
with
\sigma_{\pi_{2}}=\text{max}(\sigma_{\pi_{0}},\sigma_{\pi_{1}})
. Because the continuation maps
\varphi_{02}=\varphi_{\pi_{0}\to\pi_{2}}
and
\varphi_{12}=\varphi_{\pi_{1}\to\pi_{2}}
induce isomorphisms on all equivariant homology groups, this provides an isomorphism between

\varphi_{12}^{-1}\varphi_{02}:I^{\bullet}(Y,w,\pi_{0})\to I^{\bullet}(Y,w,\pi_{1}).

This isomorphism is canonical (independent of the choice of
\pi_{2}
). If one picks a
\pi_{2}^{\prime}
with
\sigma_{\pi_{2}}\leq\sigma_{\pi_{2}^{\prime}}
, then by functoriality of induced maps of cobordisms, we have

\varphi_{22^{\prime}}\varphi_{02}=\varphi_{02^{\prime}},\quad\quad\varphi_{22^{\prime}}\varphi_{12}=\varphi_{12^{\prime}},

so that

\varphi_{12^{\prime}}^{-1}\varphi_{02^{\prime}}=\varphi_{12}^{-1}\varphi_{22^{\prime}}^{-1}\varphi_{22^{\prime}}\varphi_{02}=\varphi_{12}^{-1}\varphi_{02}.

For any two
\pi_{2}
and
\pi_{2}^{\prime}
exceeding
\pi_{0}
and
\pi_{1}
, there is another
\pi_{2}^{\prime\prime}
exceeding all of these. Because
\varphi_{12}^{-1}\varphi_{02}=\varphi_{12^{\prime\prime}}^{-1}\varphi_{02^{\prime\prime}}=\varphi_{12^{\prime}}^{-1}\varphi_{02^{\prime}}
, we see that the isomorphism between
I^{\bullet}(Y,w,\pi_{0})
and
I^{\bullet}(Y,w,\pi_{1})
is independent of any choices in its construction.
∎

We use the previous corollary to give a definition of the instanton homology groups which is transparently independent of
\pi
.

Definition 5.28
.

Let
(Y,w)
be a weakly admissible pair. We set

I^{\bullet}(Y,w;R)=\bigoplus_{\pi\textup{ regular}}I^{\bullet}(Y,w,\pi;R)\bigg/\varphi_{\pi\to\pi^{\prime}}.

Here we quotient by the additive relation generated by setting

(\varphi_{\pi\to\pi^{\prime}}(x),\pi^{\prime})\sim(x,\pi)

for any
x\in I^{\bullet}(Y,w,\pi;R)
and any
\pi^{\prime}
with
\sigma_{\pi}\leq\sigma_{\pi^{\prime}}
.
∎

Notice the continuation map
\varphi_{\pi\to\pi}
is the identity, all continuation maps are isomorphisms, we have
\varphi_{\pi^{\prime}\to\pi^{\prime\prime}}\varphi_{\pi\to\pi^{\prime}}=\varphi_{\pi\to\pi^{\prime\prime}}
, and for any
\pi,\pi^{\prime}
there is a perturbation
\pi^{\prime\prime}
so that
\sigma_{\pi^{\prime\prime}}\leq\sigma_{\pi},\sigma_{\pi^{\prime}}
. It is a straightforward exercise in group theory to see that the natural map
I^{\bullet}(Y,w,\pi)\to I^{\bullet}(Y,w)
is an isomorphism for all regular perturbations
\pi
. This is the essential content of the fact proven in the Corollary above: not only are all
I^{\bullet}(Y,w,\pi)
isomorphic, they are
canonically
so.

5.5.1
Modified moduli spaces for homotopies

For the weakly admissible pair
(Y,w)
, fix two regular perturbations
\pi_{0}
and
\pi_{1}
with adjacent signature data. A typical element of
{\mathfrak{C}}_{\pi_{0}}(Y,w)
is denoted by
\alpha
and a typical element of
{\mathfrak{C}}_{\pi_{1}}(Y,w)
is denoted by
\alpha^{\prime}
. Any reducible element
\sigma
of
{\mathfrak{C}}_{\pi_{0}}(Y,w)
corresponds to a unique reducible element
\sigma^{\prime}
of
{\mathfrak{C}}_{\pi_{1}}(Y,w)
. There is a unique such reducible
\rho\in{\mathfrak{C}}_{\pi_{0}}(Y,w)
such that

\sigma_{\pi_{1}}(\rho^{\prime})-\sigma_{\pi_{0}}(\rho)=4,

and otherwise
\sigma_{\pi_{0}}
and
\sigma_{\pi_{1}}
are equal.

Let
(W_{+},c_{+},\pi_{+})
be the product cobordism from
(Y,w)
to
(Y,w)
together with a perturbation
\pi_{+}
from
\pi_{0}
to
\pi_{1}
; the subscript
+
is meant to indicate that the signature data increases across the cylinder. Using our assumption on
\sigma_{\pi_{0}}
and
\sigma_{\pi_{1}}
, we may assume that
\pi_{+}
is regular and for any reducible
\sigma\in{\mathfrak{C}}_{\pi_{0}}(Y,w)
, there is a unique (unobstructed) reducible instanton associated to
(W_{+},c_{+},\pi_{+})
which is asymptotic to
\sigma
and
\sigma^{\prime}
on the two ends. In particular, there is a unique reducible instanton
\Lambda_{+}
on
W_{+}
from
\rho
to
\rho^{\prime}
with
\widetilde{i}(\Lambda_{+})=+2
. The remaining reducible instantons on
(W_{+},c_{+})
have
\widetilde{i}(\Lambda)=0
and are isolated.

Similarly, let
(W_{-},c_{-},\pi_{-})
be the product cobordism from
(Y,w)
to
(Y,w)
together with a perturbation
\pi_{-}
from
\pi_{1}
to
\pi_{0}
, the subscript indicating that signature data decreases along the cylinder. In this case, we may only assume that
\pi_{-}
is obstructed-regular as in Theorem
3.5
(ii). To be more precise, we may arrange for
\pi_{-}
such that for any reducible
\sigma\in{\mathfrak{C}}_{\pi_{0}}(Y,w)
, there is a unique reducible instanton associated to
(W_{+},c_{+},\pi_{+})
, which is asymptotic to
\sigma
and
\sigma^{\prime}
on the two ends. This reducible instanton is unobstructed, with
\widetilde{i}(\Lambda)=0
, unless
\sigma=\rho
. We will write
\Lambda_{-}
for the obstructed reducible instanton that also satisfies
\widetilde{i}(\Lambda_{-})=-2
, and
\lambda_{-}:\rho^{\prime}\to\rho
for the corresponding path of connections.

Composition of
(W_{+},c_{+},\pi_{+})
and
(W_{-},c_{-},\pi_{-})
produces the product cobordism
(W_{-}^{+},c^{+}_{-})
together with a family of perturbations
\pi_{-}^{+}(t)
, where
t
denotes the length of the neck, taking values in
[T_{0},\infty)
. In particular, we may regard
(W_{-}^{+},c^{+}_{-},\pi^{+}_{-})
as a cobordism from
(Y,w,\pi_{0})
to
(Y,w,\pi_{0})
. We extend the parametrizing set to
I=[T_{0},\infty]
, by including the broken perturbation given by
\pi_{+}
and
\pi_{-}
. For any
\alpha,\beta\in{\mathfrak{C}}_{\pi_{0}}(Y,w)
and a path
z
from
\alpha
to
\beta
with
i(z)\leq 5
, consider the moduli space

M_{z}^{+}(W_{-}^{+},I;\alpha,\beta):=\bigsqcup_{t\in I}M^{+}_{z}(W_{-}^{+},\pi_{-}^{+}(t);\alpha,\beta).

(5.29)

Here the disjoint union is meant in the set-theoretic sense, not the topological sense; topologically, if
\mathbf{A}_{n}
is a sequence of
\pi_{-}^{+}(t_{n})
-instantons, and
t_{n}\to t
while
\mathbf{A}_{n}
chain-converges to
\mathbf{A}
, then this space is topologized so that
(t_{n},\mathbf{A}_{n})\to(t,\mathbf{A})
. This is completely analogous to the discussion in Section
3.6
, and the definition of
\pi_{-}^{+}(\infty)
-instantons is identical to the definition given there.

The following lemma can be established by the same arguments which give Theorem
3.5
.

Lemma 5.30
.

The family
\pi_{-}^{+}(t)
can be chosen such that any element in the moduli space

M_{z}^{+}(W_{-}^{+},I;\alpha,\beta)\;\setminus\;M_{z}^{+}(W_{-}^{+},\pi_{-}^{+}(\infty);\alpha,\beta)

is regular.

Similar in spirit to Section
5.1
, we will divide non-regular solutions of
M_{z}^{+}(W_{-}^{+},I;\alpha,\beta)
into two types, all of which belong to the stratum
M^{+}_{z}(W_{-}^{+},\pi_{-}^{+}(\infty);\alpha,\beta)
.

For any
\alpha
and
\beta=\rho
, we say that an element of the subspace

M_{z_{+}}^{+}(W_{+},\pi_{+};\alpha,\rho^{\prime})\times_{\rho^{\prime}}\mathcal{O}_{-}

(5.31)

of
M_{z}^{+}(W_{-}^{+},I;\alpha,\beta)
is an obstructed solution of type I, where
\mathcal{O}_{-}
denotes the orbit of
\Lambda_{-}
in the framed moduli space. Here
z_{+}
is the unique path with
z=z_{+}*\lambda_{-}
.

For any
\alpha
and
\beta\neq\rho
, we say that an obstructed solution of type II is an element of the subspace

X_{z_{+}\Lambda_{-}\zeta}:=M_{z_{+}}^{+}(W_{+},\pi_{+};\rho,\rho^{\prime})\times_{\rho^{\prime}}\mathcal{O}_{-}\times_{\rho}\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta}(\pi_{0};\rho,\beta).

(5.32)

of
M_{z}^{+}(W_{-}^{+},I;\alpha,\beta)
. Here we have
z=z_{+}*\lambda_{-}*\zeta
.

We have analogues of Proposition
5.1
for the obstructed solutions of any of the above four types that describe the behavior of the moduli space
M_{z}^{+}(W_{-}^{+},I;\alpha,\beta)
in a neighborhood of obstructed solutions. We start by analyzing the case of reducible solutions of type I.

First note that the complex line bundle
\mathcal{H}^{+}_{{\Lambda}_{-}}
over
\mathcal{O}_{-}
pulls back to a line bundle
\mathcal{H}_{z_{+}}
over
X_{z_{+}\Lambda_{-}}
. There is an
SO(3)
-equivariant section
\psi^{\textup{an}}_{z_{+}\Lambda_{-}}
of the pullback of
\mathcal{H}_{\alpha,\rho^{\prime}}
to the space

(0,\infty]\times M_{z_{+}}^{+}(W_{+},\pi_{+};\alpha,\rho^{\prime})\times_{\rho^{\prime}}\mathcal{O}_{-}.

This section vanishes over

\{\infty\}\times M_{z_{+}}^{+}(W_{+},\pi_{+};\alpha,\rho^{\prime})\times_{\rho^{\prime}}\times\mathcal{O}_{-}

(5.33)

and
\psi^{\textup{an}}_{z_{+}\Lambda_{-}}
is transverse to the zero section in the complement of this subspace.

As in part (ii) of Proposition
5.1
, there is also an injective open continuous map

\Phi_{z_{+}\Lambda_{-}}:\left(\psi^{\textup{an}}_{z_{+}\Lambda_{-}}\right)^{-1}(0)\to M_{z}(W_{-}^{+},I;\alpha,\rho)

whose image is an open neighborhood of the subspace of obstructed solutions of type I, where
z=z_{+}*\lambda_{-}
.

For any
[x,y]\in M_{z_{+}^{\prime}}(W_{+},\pi_{+};\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})\times_{\alpha^{\prime}}\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta^{\prime}}(\pi_{1};\alpha^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})
with
z_{+}=z_{+}^{\prime}*\zeta^{\prime}
, we have

\psi^{\textup{an}}_{z_{+}\Lambda_{-}}(t,[x,y])=\psi^{\textup{an}}_{\zeta^{\prime}\Lambda_{-}}(t,y)

where
\psi^{\textup{an}}_{\zeta^{\prime}\Lambda_{-}}
is the map to
\mathcal{H}_{\Lambda}^{+}
defined in Section
5.1
. Similarly for any
[x,y]\in\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta}(\pi_{0};\alpha,\gamma)\times_{\gamma}M_{z_{+}^{\prime}}(W_{+},\pi_{+};\gamma,\rho^{\prime})
with
z_{+}=\zeta*z_{+}^{\prime}
, we have

\psi^{\textup{an}}_{z_{+}\Lambda_{-}}(t,[x,y])=\psi^{\textup{an}}_{z_{+}^{\prime}\Lambda_{-}}(t,y).

Just as in Section
5.1
, there are further compatibility maps on the open embeddings
\Phi
.

For the obstructed solutions of type II, first we define the complex line bundle
\mathcal{H}_{z_{+}}
over
X_{z_{+}\Lambda_{-}\zeta}
as the pullback of
\mathcal{H}_{\Lambda}^{+}
as usual. Then we have the obstruction section
\psi^{\textup{an}}_{z_{+}\Lambda_{-}\zeta}
defined over

(0,\infty]\times M_{z_{+}}^{+}(W_{+},\pi_{+};\alpha,\rho^{\prime})\times_{\rho^{\prime}}\mathcal{O}_{-}\times_{\rho}[-\infty,0)\times\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta}(\pi_{0};\rho,\beta).

This section vanishes on the strata lying over over
\{(\infty,-\infty)\}\in(0,\infty]\times[-\infty,0)
, and is transverse to the zero section in the complement of these strata. Again, there is a map
\Phi_{z_{+}\Lambda_{-}\zeta}
from the zero set of
\psi^{\textup{an}}
into an open neighborhood of the obstructed solutions of type II.

The restriction of this obstruction section to the remaining strata is determined by the obstructions sections defined on smaller moduli spaces. For any
[x,\Lambda_{-},y]\in X_{z_{+}\Lambda_{-}\zeta}
, we have

\psi^{\textup{an}}_{z_{+}\Lambda_{-}\zeta}(t,[x,\Lambda_{-}],-\infty,y)=\psi^{\textup{an}}_{z_{+}\Lambda_{-}}(t,[x,\Lambda_{-}]),\hskip 28.45274pt\psi^{\textup{an}}_{z_{+}\Lambda_{-}\zeta}(\infty,[x,\Lambda_{-}],t^{\prime},y)=\psi^{\textup{an}}_{\Lambda_{-}\zeta}(\Lambda_{-},t^{\prime},y).

For any
[x,y]\in M_{z_{+}^{\prime}}(W_{+},\pi_{+};\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})\times_{\alpha^{\prime}}\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta^{\prime}}(\pi_{1};\alpha^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})
with
z_{+}=z_{+}^{\prime}*\zeta^{\prime}
and
u\in\breve{M}_{\zeta}(\pi_{0};\rho,\beta)
, we have

\psi^{\textup{an}}_{z_{+}\Lambda_{-}\zeta}(t,[x,y,\Lambda_{-}],t^{\prime},u)=\psi^{\textup{an}}_{\zeta^{\prime}\Lambda_{-}\zeta}(t,[y,\Lambda_{-},u],t^{\prime}),

and for any
[x,y]\in\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta}(\pi_{0};\alpha,\gamma)\times_{\gamma}M_{z_{+}^{\prime}}(W_{+},\pi_{+};\gamma,\rho^{\prime})
with
z_{+}=\zeta*z_{+}^{\prime}
and
u\in\breve{M}_{\zeta}(\pi_{0};\rho,\beta)
, we have

\psi^{\textup{an}}_{z_{+}\Lambda_{-}\zeta}(t,[x,y,\Lambda_{-}],t^{\prime},u)=\psi^{\textup{an}}_{z_{+}^{\prime}\Lambda_{-}\zeta}(t,[y,\Lambda_{-}],t^{\prime},u).

Finally if
x\in M_{z_{+}}^{+}(W_{+},\pi_{+};\alpha,\rho^{\prime})
and
[y,u]\in\breve{M}_{\zeta_{0}}(\pi_{0};\rho,\gamma)\times_{\gamma}M_{\zeta_{1}}(\pi_{0};\gamma,\beta)
with
\zeta=\zeta_{0}*\zeta_{1}
, then

\psi^{\textup{an}}_{z_{+}\Lambda_{-}\zeta}(t,[x,\Lambda_{-}],t^{\prime},[y,u])=\psi^{\textup{an}}_{z_{+}\Lambda_{-}\zeta_{0}}(t,[x,\Lambda_{-}],t^{\prime},y).

For all of these obstructed solutions, there is a map
\Phi
with appropriate decoration that identifies the zero locus of the obstruction with a neighborhood of obstructed solutions in the moduli space
M_{z}^{+}(W_{-}^{+},I;\alpha,\beta)
.

As in the case of the modified moduli spaces, we first remove a neighborhood of obstructed solutions in the moduli spaces
M_{z}^{+}(W_{-}^{+},I;\alpha,\beta)
and then add a new part, which is defined in terms of a family of privileged sections of the obstruction bundles and homotopies from the analytical obstruction sections to these sections.

To initiate this construction, we fix
T
, and let
\psi^{T}_{z_{+}\Lambda_{-}}
denote the restriction of
\psi^{\textup{an}}_{z_{+}\Lambda_{-}}
to the space
\{T\}\times M_{z_{+}}^{+}(W_{+},\pi_{+};\alpha,\rho^{\prime})\times_{\rho^{\prime}}\mathcal{O}_{-}
. Similarly, let
\psi^{T}_{z_{+}\Lambda_{-}}\zeta
be the restriction of
\psi^{\textup{an}}_{z_{+}\Lambda_{-}\zeta}
to the subspace of
X_{z_{+}\Lambda_{-}\zeta}
lying over
A_{T}\subset(0,\infty]\times[-\infty,0)
; recall that this subspace
A_{T}
was defined in Section
5.2
.

We then defined privileged sections
\psi^{\text{priv}}_{z_{+}\Lambda_{-}}
and
\psi^{\text{priv}}_{z_{+}\Lambda_{-}\zeta}
over these subspaces, which satisfy the same compatibilty conditions that the analytic gluing sections do. We demand that the latter takes the form

\psi^{\text{priv}}_{z_{+}\Lambda_{-}\zeta}(t,[x,y],t^{\prime})=\frac{T}{t}\cdot\psi^{\text{priv}}_{z_{+}\Lambda_{-}}(x)-\frac{T}{t^{\prime}}\cdot\psi^{\text{priv}}_{\Lambda_{-}\zeta}(y);

we assume that the two previously-chosen sections
\psi^{\text{priv}}
(one chosen here, the other chosen in Section
5.2
) are chosen so that this is a transverse section.

We then choose homotopies of sections
\psi^{\text{htpy}}_{z_{+}\Lambda_{-}}
and
\psi^{\text{htpy}}_{z_{+}\Lambda_{-}\zeta}
once again satisfying the same compatibility properties, and interpolating between the analytic gluing sections
\psi^{T}
and the priveleged sections
\psi^{\text{priv}}
.

Finally, the modified moduli spaces
N^{+}_{z}(W_{-}^{+},I;\alpha,\beta)
are defined by exactly the same procedure given in Section
5.2
, with the same orientation conventions: they are oriented as zero sets of sections of complex line bundles, where the domains of these sections are oriented as products given the product structures written above. In the case of
\psi^{\text{priv}}_{z_{+}\Lambda_{-}\zeta}
, we orient the zero set as the zero set of

s\psi^{\text{priv}}_{z_{+}\Lambda_{-}}(x)+(1-s)\cdot\psi^{\text{priv}}_{\Lambda_{-}\zeta}(y)

over the space

M_{z_{+}}(W_{+},\pi_{+};\alpha,\rho^{\prime})\times_{\rho^{\prime}}\mathcal{O}_{-}\times_{\rho}\mathopen{}\left([0,1]\times\breve{M}_{\zeta}(Y;\rho,\beta)\right)\mathclose{}.

That these are stratified-smooth spaces follows by the same argument as in Section
5.2
. Below, we record the boundary of these stratified-smooth spaces, together with their orientations; unlike Section
5.2
, we use the language of geometric chains to avoid talking about faces which appear with opposite orientations.

Suppose
z=z_{+}*\lambda_{-}
, where
z_{+}:\alpha\to\rho^{\prime}
is a path of connections on
W_{+}
.

Proposition 5.34
.

The boundary of the stratified smooth space
N_{z}^{+}(W_{-}^{+},I;\alpha,\beta)
, considered as a geometric chain in
\alpha\times\beta
, is given
as follows.

(i)

If
\beta=\rho
, then
\partial N_{z}^{+}(W_{-}^{+},I;\alpha,\rho)
is given by

\displaystyle(-1)^{\dim\alpha}\partial N_{z}^{+}(W_{-}^{+},I;\alpha,\rho)=\;

\displaystyle\sum_{z=z_{+}*z_{-}}M^{+}_{z_{+}}(W_{+},\pi_{+};\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})\times_{\alpha^{\prime}}N^{+}_{z_{-}}(W_{-},\pi_{-};\alpha^{\prime},\rho)

\displaystyle-N^{+}_{z}(W_{-}^{+},\pi_{-}^{+}(T_{0});\alpha,\rho)

\displaystyle+\sum_{z=\zeta*z^{\prime}}(-1)^{\widetilde{i}(\zeta)}\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta}(Y,\pi_{0};\alpha,\gamma)\times_{\gamma}N^{+}_{z^{\prime}}(W_{-}^{+},I;\gamma,\rho)

\displaystyle+\sum_{z=z^{\prime}*\zeta^{\prime}}(-1)^{\widetilde{i}(z^{\prime})}N^{+}_{z^{\prime}}(W_{-}^{+},I;\alpha,\gamma)\times_{\gamma}\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta^{\prime}}(Y,\pi_{0};\gamma,\rho)

\displaystyle+\mathopen{}\left(\psi_{z_{+}\Lambda_{-}}^{\textup{priv}}\right)\mathclose{}^{-1}(0).

In the final term, we have
z=z_{+}*\lambda_{-}
, where
z_{+}:\alpha\to\rho^{\prime}
is a path of connections along
W_{+}
.

(ii)

If
\beta\neq\rho
, then
\partial N_{z}^{+}(W_{-}^{+},I;\alpha,\beta)
is given by

\displaystyle(-1)^{\dim\alpha}\partial N_{z}^{+}(W_{-}^{+},I;\alpha,\beta)=\;

\displaystyle\sum_{z=z_{+}*z_{-}}M^{+}_{z_{+}}(W_{+},\pi_{+};\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})\times_{\alpha^{\prime}}N^{+}_{z_{-}}(W_{-},\pi_{-};\alpha^{\prime},\beta)

\displaystyle-N^{+}_{z}(W_{-}^{+},\pi_{-}^{+}(T_{0});\alpha,\beta)

\displaystyle+\sum_{z=\zeta*z^{\prime}}(-1)^{\widetilde{i}(\zeta)}\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta}(Y,\pi_{0};\alpha,\gamma)\times_{\gamma}N^{+}_{z^{\prime}}(W_{-}^{+},I;\gamma,\beta)

\displaystyle+\sum_{z=z^{\prime}*\zeta^{\prime}}(-1)^{\widetilde{i}(z^{\prime})}N^{+}_{z^{\prime}}(W_{-}^{+},I;\alpha,\gamma)\times_{\gamma}\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta^{\prime}}(Y,\pi_{0};\gamma,\beta)

\displaystyle-\mathopen{}\left(\psi_{z_{+}\Lambda_{-}\zeta}^{\textup{priv}}\right)\mathclose{}^{-1}(0).

where in the final term we have
z=z_{+}*\lambda_{-}*\zeta
.

Remark 5.35
.

The computation of boundary faces is analogous to the work concluding Section
5.2
, including the discussion of signs. For the final term, the domain of this section is oriented as

\alpha\times(T_{0},\infty)\times M^{\text{Fiber}}_{z_{+}}(W_{+};\alpha,\rho^{\prime})\times(-\infty,0)\times\breve{M}^{\text{Fiber}}_{\zeta}(Y,\pi_{0};\rho,\beta);

here
t\in(T_{0},\infty)
corresponds to the parameter in
\pi_{-}^{+}(t)
, while
(-\infty,0)
corresponds to how far we translate the instanton on
Y
to the left before gluing.

To compute the orientation on the zero set, one shuffles
(T_{0},\infty)\times(-\infty,0)
to the front incurring a sign of
(-1)^{\widetilde{i}(z_{+})}
, passes to the locus
A_{T}\cong[0,1]
where
T>T_{0}
is large (which incurs a sign of
-1
because the outward-normal-first convention is here negative the orientation of the regular zero set convention), and shuffles that back to

(-1)^{\dim\alpha-1}\alpha\times M^{\text{Fiber}}_{z_{+}}(W_{+};\alpha,\rho^{\prime})\times[0,1]\times\breve{M}^{\text{Fiber}}_{\zeta}(Y,\pi_{0};\rho,\beta).\qed

Moving on to the other composite, one may construct a family of perturbations
\pi_{+}^{-}(t)
on the cylinder which are equal to
\pi_{1}
on the ends, so that
\pi_{+}^{-}(\infty)
is the broken perturbation
\pi_{-}\sqcup\pi_{+}:(Y,\pi_{1})\to(Y,\pi_{0})\to(Y,\pi_{1})
. Running through the same construction, we obtain modified moduli spaces
N_{z}^{+}(W_{+}^{-},I)
, and we enumerate the boundary strata (with signs) below. Here when doing gluing on both sides of
\Lambda_{-}
our open set is indexed by
(T_{0},\infty)\times(0,\infty)
(The first term corresponding to the perturbation parameter, the second corresponding to how far left we translate the instanton on
(\mathbb{R}\times Y,\pi_{1})
before gluing), and we instead use the function
L(t,t^{\prime})=-1/t-1/t^{\prime}
to truncate, with locus
A^{\prime}_{T}=\{(t,t^{\prime})\mid 1/t+1/t^{\prime}=1/T\}
oriented as the regular preimage
L^{-1}(-1/T)
. The isomorphism
A^{\prime}_{T}\cong[0,1]
given by
t\mapsto 1-T/t=T/t^{\prime}
is orientation-preserving, and sends the section
\psi_{\zeta^{\prime}\Lambda_{-}z_{+}}^{\text{priv}}
to

s\psi^{\text{priv}}_{\zeta^{\prime}\Lambda_{-}}(x)+(1-s)\cdot\psi^{\text{priv}}_{\Lambda_{-}z_{+}}(y).

Proposition 5.36
.

The boundary of the stratified smooth space
N_{z}^{+}(W_{+}^{-},I;\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})
as a geometric chain is given
as follows.

(i)

If
\alpha^{\prime}=\rho^{\prime}
, then
\partial N_{z}^{+}(W_{+}^{-},I;\rho^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})
is given by

\displaystyle(-1)^{\dim\rho^{\prime}}\partial N_{z}^{+}(W_{+}^{-},I;\rho^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})=\;

\displaystyle\sum_{z=z_{-}*z_{+}}N^{+}_{z_{-}}(W_{-},\pi_{-};\rho^{\prime},\alpha)\times_{\alpha}M^{+}_{z_{+}}(W_{+},\pi_{+};\alpha,\beta^{\prime})

\displaystyle-N^{+}_{z}(W_{+}^{-},\pi_{+}^{-}(T_{0});\rho^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})

\displaystyle+\sum_{z=\zeta^{\prime}*z^{\prime}}(-1)^{\widetilde{i}(\zeta^{\prime})}\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta^{\prime}}(Y,\pi_{1};\rho^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})\times_{\gamma^{\prime}}N^{+}_{z^{\prime}}(W_{+}^{-},I;\gamma^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})

\displaystyle+\sum_{z=z^{\prime}*\zeta}(-1)^{\widetilde{i}(z^{\prime})}N^{+}_{z^{\prime}}(W_{+}^{-},I;\rho^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})\times_{\gamma^{\prime}}\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta}(Y,\pi_{1};\gamma^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})

\displaystyle+\mathopen{}\left(\psi_{\Lambda_{-}z_{+}}^{\textup{priv}}\right)\mathclose{}^{-1}(0).

In the final term, we have
z=\lambda_{-}*z_{+}
, where
z_{+}:\rho\to\beta^{\prime}
is a path of connections along
W_{-}
. Of course, the sign
(-1)^{\dim\rho^{\prime}}
is simply
1
.

(ii)

If
\alpha^{\prime}\neq\rho^{\prime}
, then
\partial N_{z}^{+}(W_{+}^{,}I;\alpha,\beta)
is given by

\displaystyle(-1)^{\dim\alpha^{\prime}}\partial N_{z}^{+}(W_{+}^{-},I;\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})=\;

\displaystyle\sum_{z=z_{-}*z_{+}}N^{+}_{z_{-}}(W_{-},\pi_{-};\alpha^{\prime},\alpha)\times_{\alpha}N^{+}_{z_{+}}(W_{+},\pi_{+};\alpha,\beta^{\prime})

\displaystyle-N^{+}_{z}(W_{+}^{-},\pi_{+}^{-}(T_{0});\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})

\displaystyle+\sum_{z=\zeta^{\prime}*z^{\prime}}(-1)^{\widetilde{i}(\zeta^{\prime})}\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta^{\prime}}(Y,\pi_{1};\alpha^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})\times_{\gamma^{\prime}}N^{+}_{z^{\prime}}(W_{+}^{-},I;\gamma^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})

\displaystyle+\sum_{z=z^{\prime}*\zeta}(-1)^{\widetilde{i}(z^{\prime})}N^{+}_{z^{\prime}}(W_{+}^{-},I;\alpha^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})\times_{\gamma^{\prime}}\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta}(Y,\pi_{1};\gamma^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})

\displaystyle-(-1)^{\widetilde{i}(\zeta^{\prime})}\mathopen{}\left(\psi_{\zeta^{\prime}\Lambda_{-}z_{+}}^{\textup{priv}}\right)\mathclose{}^{-1}(0),

where in the final term we have
z=\zeta^{\prime}*\lambda_{-}*z_{+}
.

Remark 5.37
.

For the final term, the domain of this section is oriented as

\alpha^{\prime}\times(T_{0},\infty)\times(0,\infty)\times\breve{M}^{\text{Fiber}}_{\zeta^{\prime}}(Y,\pi_{1};\alpha^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})\times M^{\text{Fiber}}_{z_{+}}(W_{+};\rho,\beta^{\prime});

one shuffles
(T_{0},\infty)\times(0,\infty)
to the front incurring no additional sign, passes to the locus
A^{\prime}_{T}\cong[0,1]
(which incurs a sign of
-1
because the outward-normal-first convention is here negative the orientation of the regular zero set convention), and shuffles that back to

(-1)^{\dim\alpha^{\prime}+\widetilde{i}(\zeta^{\prime})-1}\alpha^{\prime}\times M^{\text{Fiber}}_{\zeta^{\prime}}(Y,\pi_{1};\alpha^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})\times[0,1]\times\breve{M}^{\text{Fiber}}_{z_{+}}(W_{+};\rho,\beta^{\prime}).\qed

5.5.2
Homotopies of bimodules from modified moduli spaces

We can now use the constructions of the preceding section to define the desired bimodule homotopies. To check these easily, have on-hand the definitions of
\mathcal{W}_{+}
from Construction
5.3
and
\mathcal{W}_{-}
from Construction
5.4
, as well as the definition of bimodule homotopy from Definition
4.2
. We begin with the easier of the two cases.

For compactness of notation, write
\mathcal{H}_{+}^{-}(\alpha,\beta)
for the modified moduli spaces
N^{+}(W,\pi_{+}^{-}(t);\alpha,\beta)
from the previous section. Let
\pi_{+}^{-}(T_{0})
be the constant perturbation at
\pi_{1}
, so that
M^{+}(W,\pi_{+}^{-}(T_{0}))
defines the identity bimodule on
\mathcal{I}_{1}=\mathcal{I}(Y,\pi_{1})
.

Proposition 5.38
.

The modified moduli spaces above define a bimodule homotopy

\mathcal{H}_{+}^{-}:\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{I}_{1}}\implies\mathcal{W}_{+}\circ\mathcal{W}_{-}.

Proof.

We hope that the following diagram indicating the relevant bimodules is helpful when parsing the argument; the composite bimodule is given by summing over the fiber products of all two-step paths running from
\alpha^{\prime}
to
\beta^{\prime}
, and we want to find a homotopy from the identity bimodule to this composite bimodule.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\alpha}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S_{\rho}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{\rho}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B(W_{+};\rho,\beta^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(W_{-};\alpha^{\prime},\rho)\times_{\rho}S_{\rho}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(W_{-};\alpha^{\prime},\alpha)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(W_{+};\alpha,\beta^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{\alpha^{\prime}=\rho^{\prime}}\mathcal{O}_{-}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z(W_{+};\rho,\beta^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-(-1)^{\widetilde{i}(\zeta^{\prime})}B(\pi_{1};\alpha^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})}

The boundary relations enumerated in Proposition
5.5.1
almost immediately show that this gives a bimodule homotopy from the identity bimodule. The only interesting stratum is the last one, arising from obstructed gluing theory; we will focus on the case
\beta^{\prime}\neq\rho^{\prime}
, as the case
\beta=\rho
is simpler. In the homotopy relation, this boundary stratum comes from the composite term, as

\mathcal{W}_{-}(\alpha^{\prime},\rho)\times_{\rho}\mathcal{W}_{+}(\rho,\beta^{\prime})

has the extra term

-(-1)^{\widetilde{i}(\zeta)}B_{\zeta}(\alpha^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})\times_{S_{\rho}}B(W;\rho,\beta^{\prime});

because we use
-\psi^{\text{priv}}_{\zeta^{\prime}\Lambda_{-}}
in defining the blowups, Lemma
A.3
identifies this with
-(-1)^{\widetilde{i}(\zeta)}
times the zero set of
(1-s)\psi^{\text{priv}}_{\Lambda_{-}z_{+}}-s\psi^{\text{priv}}_{\zeta^{\prime}\Lambda_{-}}
, which is identified with the zero set of the section
\psi_{\zeta^{\prime}\Lambda_{-}z_{+}}^{\text{priv}}.
So this additional term in the homotopy relation is precisely the same as the additional term in the boundary relation of Proposition
5.5.1
, as desired.
∎

Though the obstructed-gluing analysis of the two cases is nearly identical (except for one sign), the definition of the bimodule homotopy running in the other direction, and the verification that it is a bimodule homotopy, are significantly more intricate.

Construction 5.39
.

For compactness of notation, write
H_{-}^{+}(\alpha,\beta)
for the modified moduli spaces
N^{+}(W,\pi_{-}^{+}(t);\alpha,\beta)
from the previous section. Here we assume that
\pi_{-}^{+}(T_{0})
is the constant perturbation at
\pi_{0}
, so that
N_{z}^{+}(W_{-}^{+},\pi_{-}^{+}(T_{0});\alpha,\beta)
is either diffeomorphic to
[0,1]\times\breve{M}(\pi_{0};\alpha,\beta)
, with endpoint maps determined by the latter factor), or when
\alpha=\beta
equal to
\alpha
itself. In the former case, this is a trivial chain (it supports the orientation-reversing isomorphism given by
t\mapsto 1-t
in the first factor), hence zero at the chain level, as in Remark
4.2
.

Before moving on, observe that
H_{-}^{+}(\rho,\rho)^{\text{red}}
is diffeomorphic to
[0,1]\times\rho
, and (because the endpoint maps determine an equivariant map to
\rho\times\rho
, which has two isolated reducible orbits) the endpoint maps factor through the projection to
\rho
, so this is also a trivial chain.

Over each moduli space
H_{-}^{+}(\rho,\beta)
, we will define certain equivariant sections of the complex line bundle
\mathcal{H}_{\rho}
, written
\psi^{H}_{\rho\beta}
. We prescribe the boundary values on the various faces enumerated in Proposition
5.5.1
:
\psi^{H}_{\rho\beta}
should be equal to the priveleged sections
\psi^{\text{priv}}_{z_{+}\Lambda_{-}}
on the first listed face; should agree with the priveleged sections
\psi_{\zeta\Lambda_{-}}^{\text{priv}}
on the third face; should be pulled back from
\psi^{H}_{\rho\gamma}
on the fourth enumerated face; and on the final face — diffeomorphic to
B(W_{+};\rho,\rho^{\prime})\times_{S_{\rho^{\prime}}}B(\pi_{0};\rho,\rho)
— it should be pulled back from a section
\psi^{+}_{\rho\rho}
over the first factor. On the part of the fourth face given by
H_{-}^{+}(\rho,\rho)^{\text{red}}\times_{\rho}\breve{M}(\pi_{0};\rho,\beta)
, we demand that the section
\psi^{H}_{\rho\beta}
factor through the projection to
\breve{M}(\pi_{0};\rho,\beta)
. This guarantees that even after blowing up, this boundary component is a trivial chain.

Finally, in the case
\beta=\rho
, we only attempt to define a transverse section over the irreducible locus of
H_{-}^{+}(\rho,\rho)
; there is no transverse section over the reducible locus, but we will (mostly) be able to ignore it, as this is a trivial chain.

Define
B_{-}^{+}(\rho,\beta)
to be the blowup of
\psi^{H}_{\rho\beta}
, so a geometric chain in
S_{\rho}\times\beta
; let
Z_{-}^{+}(\rho,\beta)
be the zero set of
\psi^{H}_{\rho\beta}
, so still a geometric chain in
\rho\times\beta
.

In the special case
\beta=\rho
, we set
\widehat{B}_{-}^{+}(\rho,\rho)
to be the blowup of the section
\psi^{H}_{\rho\rho}\sqcup\psi^{+}_{\rho\rho}
over

H_{-}^{+}(\rho,\rho)^{\text{irr}}\times_{\rho}S_{\rho}-B(W_{+};\rho,\rho^{\prime});

transversality dictates that the zero set
\widehat{Z}_{-}^{+}(\rho,\rho)
is empty.

Then the bimodule homotopy

\mathcal{H}_{-}^{+}:\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{S}_{\rho}\mathcal{I}_{0}}\implies\mathcal{W}_{-}\circ\mathcal{W}_{+}

is defined by the formula

\mathcal{H}_{-}^{+}(\alpha,\beta)=\begin{pmatrix}[c|ccc]&\alpha&S_{\rho}&\overline{\rho}\\
\hline\cr\beta&H_{-}^{+}(\alpha,\beta)&-B_{-}^{+}(\rho,\beta)&Z_{-}^{+}(\rho,\beta)\\
S_{\rho}&H_{-}^{+}(\alpha,\rho)\times_{\rho}S_{\rho}-(-1)^{\widetilde{i}(z_{+})}B_{z_{+}}(W_{+};\alpha,\rho)&-\widehat{B}_{H}(\rho,\rho)&0\\
\overline{\rho}&0&0&0\end{pmatrix}.\qed

Proposition 5.40
.

The formula above defines a bimodule homotopy.

To aid with the verification, we include a diagram indicating both of the bimodules we are composing.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S_{\rho}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S_{\rho}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{\rho}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{\rho}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{\alpha^{\prime}\neq\rho^{\prime}}N(W_{-};\alpha^{\prime},\rho)\times_{\rho}S_{\rho}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N(W_{-};\alpha^{\prime},\beta)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{\alpha^{\prime}=\rho^{\prime}}\mathcal{O}_{-}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B(W_{+};\rho,\alpha^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z(W_{+};\rho,\alpha^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(W_{+};\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-(-1)^{\widetilde{i}(\zeta^{\prime})}B(\pi_{1};\alpha^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})}

Next, we depict the relevant flow categories and the bimodule homotopies.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha/\gamma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma/\beta}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S_{\rho}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S_{\rho}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{\rho}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{\rho}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{-}^{+}(\alpha,\beta)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\widehat{B}_{H}(\rho,\rho)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{-}^{+}(\alpha,\rho)\times_{\rho}S_{\rho}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-(-1)^{\widetilde{i}(z_{+})}B(W_{+};\alpha,\rho^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-B_{-}^{+}(\rho,\beta)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z^{+}_{-}(\rho,\beta)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{\rho}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{\rho}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-B(\pi_{0};\rho,\alpha)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z(\pi_{0};\rho,\alpha)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-B(\pi_{0};\rho,\beta)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z(\pi_{0};\rho,\beta)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\breve{M}(\pi_{0};\alpha,\gamma)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\breve{M}(\pi_{0};\gamma,\beta)}

Then the relation we are attempting to prove asserts that for every left-to-right arrow in the second diagram (from, say,
\alpha
to
\beta
), its boundary is a sum over all two-step paths in the second diagram which start at
\alpha
and end at
\beta
, plus the corresponding fiber product in the first diagram, minus
\delta_{\alpha=\beta}\alpha
.

Sketch of proof.

Because this computation is intricate and delicate, we will avoid discussing the signs (which would only serve to obfuscate the discussion); the careful reader should not have difficulty inserting the signs into the arguments below. That
\mathcal{H}_{-}^{+}(\alpha,\beta)
satisfies the homotopy relation for
\alpha,\beta\neq\rho
follows exactly as in the computation that
\mathcal{W}_{-}
is a bimodule.

The novel parts are those involving moduli spaces of the form
H_{-}^{+}(\rho,\rho)
. We will only discuss those parts of the bimodule relation which involve this term below, as all other parts of the discussion follow the same strategy as previous arguments; the reader should understand this as a guide to clarifying potentially confusing details when running through the verification.

•

In entry
(1,2)
, the term
\mathcal{H}_{-}^{+}(S_{\rho},S_{\rho})\times_{S_{\rho}}\mathcal{S}_{\rho}\mathcal{C}(S_{\rho},\beta)
is given by

B\mathopen{}\left(H_{-}^{+}(\rho,\rho)^{\text{irr}}\times_{\rho}S_{\rho}+B(W_{+};\rho,\rho^{\prime})\right)\mathclose{}\times_{S_{\rho}}B_{\zeta}(\rho,\beta);

the left-hand blowup is determined entirely by the first factor, so we focus on this last. The latter term simplifies to give the
\mathopen{}\left(\psi^{\text{priv}}_{z_{+}\Lambda_{-}\zeta}\right)\mathclose{}^{-1}(0)
term, blown up on the left-hand side (this is where we use the assumption that
\psi_{\rho\beta}^{H}
is pulled back from a section
\psi^{H}_{\rho\rho}
on the first factor here). The former term simplifies to give

B\mathopen{}\left(H_{-}^{+}(\rho,\rho)^{\text{irr}}\times_{\rho}\breve{M}_{\zeta}(\rho,\beta)\right)\mathclose{};

by the assumption that the section on this term is pulled back from the first factor, this simplifies to
B_{-}^{+}(\rho,\rho)^{\text{irr}}\times_{\rho}\breve{M}_{\zeta}(\rho,\beta)
, which is one of the terms promised by the boundary relation Proposition
5.5.1
. Finally, this leaves one term in the known boundary relation unaccounted for: the blowup of
H_{-}^{+}(\rho,\rho)^{\text{red}}\times_{\rho}\breve{M}_{\zeta}(\rho,\beta).
However, we assumed that the section used to define the blowup factors through a projection to the latter factor (and every stratum of the first factor has positive dimension), so that this chain has small rank on each positive-dimensional stratum. In particular, the corresponding blown-up chain is degenerate, so that this term is zero on the chain level. This squares away the unexpected factors in both the bimodule relation and the boundary relation Proposition
5.5.1
.

Entry
(1,3)
is similar but strictly simpler because
\widehat{Z}_{-}^{+}(\rho,\rho)=\varnothing
.

•

In entry
(2,1)
, the novel terms arise from
\mathcal{S}_{\rho}\mathcal{C}(\alpha,S_{\rho})\times_{S_{\rho}}\mathcal{H}_{-}^{+}(S_{\rho},S_{\rho}),
which may be expanded as

\breve{M}(\alpha,\rho)\times_{\rho}S_{\rho}\times_{S_{\rho}}B\left(H_{-}^{+}(\rho,\rho)^{\text{irr}}\times_{\rho}S_{\rho}+B(W_{+};\rho,\rho^{\prime})\right);

cancelling out
S_{\rho}\times_{S_{\rho}}
and replacing the blowup with the original chain, this simplfies to

\breve{M}(\alpha,\rho)\times_{\rho}H_{-}^{+}(\rho,\rho)^{\text{irr}}\times_{\rho}S_{\rho}+\breve{M}(\alpha,\rho)\times_{\rho}M(W_{+};\rho,\rho^{\prime}),

which contribute the terms corresponding to factorization through
\rho
to the boundary relations of both
H_{-}^{+}(\alpha,\rho)\times_{\rho}S_{\rho}
and
B_{z_{+}}(W_{+};\alpha,\rho)
. In the former case, this still leaves an absent term corresponding to
H_{-}^{+}(\rho,\rho)^{\text{red}}
; but this is a degenerate geometric chain, so zero at the chain level, so irrelevant in checking the boundary relation.

•

In entry
(3,1)
, there are only two terms in the bimodule homotopy relation for
\partial\mathcal{H}_{-}^{+}(\alpha,\overline{\rho})
which are not tautologically empty: the first is

\mathcal{M}_{1}(\alpha,\overline{\rho})=\mathcal{W}_{+}(\alpha,\rho^{\prime})\times_{\rho^{\prime}}\mathcal{W}_{-}(\rho^{\prime},\overline{\rho})=M(W_{+};\alpha,\rho^{\prime})\times_{\rho^{\prime}}\mathcal{O}_{-}\cong M(W_{+};\alpha,\rho^{\prime}),

and the second is

\mathcal{H}_{-}^{+}(\alpha,S_{\rho})\times_{S_{\rho}}\mathcal{S}_{\rho}\mathcal{C}(S_{\rho},\overline{\rho})=\mathopen{}\left(H_{-}^{+}(\alpha,\rho)\times_{\rho}S_{\rho}+B_{z_{+}}(W_{+};\alpha,\rho)\right)\mathclose{}\times_{S_{\rho}}S_{\rho},

which simplifies to

H_{-}^{+}(\alpha,\rho)\times_{\rho}S_{\rho}+B_{z_{+}}(W_{+};\alpha,\rho)

as a chain in
\alpha\times\rho
.

Now the first term is degenerate — it factors through the projection to
H_{-}^{+}(\alpha,\rho)
, so has small rank — and considering the second as a chain in
\alpha\times\rho
instead of
\alpha\times S_{\rho}
leaves it equivalent to the chain
M(W_{+};\alpha,\rho)
. Thus this term cancels out with the previous.

•

Entry
(2,2)
is by far the most intricate; there is no avoiding talking about every component. First, let us point out that in

\widehat{B}_{H}(\rho,\rho)=B\mathopen{}\left(H_{-}^{+}(\rho,\rho)^{\text{irr}}\times_{\rho}S_{\rho}+B(W_{+};\rho,\rho^{\prime})\right)\mathclose{},

the first term has no boundary: this moduli space has fiber-dimension
1
over
S_{\rho}
, so that
H_{-}^{+}(\rho,\rho)^{\text{irr}}
is 3-dimensional, so its quotient by
SO(3)
is hence a 0-manifold, which has no boundary. Because the section over this space is non-vanishing, taking the left blowup is equivalent to choosing a lift of the map to
\rho
to a map to
S_{\rho}
; no new boundary terms appear.

So we are engaged in a computation of the boundary of the second term. Let us first write the boundary predicted by the bimodule homotopy. This consists of four terms:

\displaystyle\sum_{\gamma}\mathcal{S}(S_{\rho},\gamma)\times_{\gamma}\mathcal{H}(\gamma,S_{\rho})\quad\quad\sum_{\gamma}\mathcal{H}(S_{\rho},\gamma)\times_{\gamma}\mathcal{S}(\gamma,S_{\rho})

\displaystyle\mathcal{\sum}_{\gamma^{\prime}}\mathcal{W}_{+}(S_{\rho},\gamma^{\prime})\times_{\gamma^{\prime}}\mathcal{W}_{-}(\gamma^{\prime},S_{\rho})\quad\quad\mathcal{M}_{0}(S_{\rho},S_{\rho})=S_{\rho}.

Spelling these out, this amounts to
S_{\rho}
and six additional boundary terms:

\displaystyle B(\pi_{0};\rho,\gamma)\times_{\gamma}H^{+}_{-}(\gamma,\rho)\times_{\rho}S_{\rho}

(5.41)

\displaystyle B(\pi_{0};\rho,\gamma)\times_{\gamma}B(W_{+};\gamma,\rho^{\prime})

(5.42)

\displaystyle B^{+}_{-}(\rho,\gamma)\times_{\gamma}\breve{M}(\pi_{0};\gamma,\rho)\times_{\rho}S_{\rho}

(5.43)

\displaystyle B(W_{+};\rho,\gamma^{\prime})\times_{\gamma^{\prime}}M(W_{-};\gamma^{\prime},\rho)\times_{\rho}S_{\rho}

(5.44)

\displaystyle B(W_{+};\rho,\gamma^{\prime})\times_{\gamma^{\prime}}B(\pi_{1};\gamma^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})

(5.45)

The boundary has fiber-dimension zero, so we may discard all terms which necessarily have positive fiber-dimension: the first, third, and fourth do not appear. What we are left with is precisely the boundary relation for
B(W_{+};\rho,\rho^{\prime})
; the final term
S_{\rho}
corresponds to
Z(W_{+};\rho,\rho^{\prime})\times_{\rho}S_{\rho}
, as this is identifiable with
\mathcal{O}_{+}\times_{\rho}S_{\rho}=S_{\rho}
, cancelling out
\mathcal{M}_{0}(S_{\rho},S_{\rho})=S_{\rho}
. So this is indeed the boundary of
\widehat{B}_{H}(\rho,\rho)
, as desired.

•

In entry
(3,3)
, the only terms in the bimodule homotopy relation which aren’t tautologically zero (or of fiber-dimension
\geq 8
) are
\mathcal{M}_{1}(\overline{\rho},\overline{\rho})-\mathcal{M}_{0}(\overline{\rho},\overline{\rho})
. The latter is a copy of
\overline{\rho}
itself, with the identity map as its endpoint maps. The first is a sum over fiber products, but the only term which isn’t tautologically zero is
Z(W_{+};\overline{\rho},S_{\rho})\times_{\overline{\rho}}\mathcal{O}_{-}
. Both factors consist of a single reducible orbit, so that the fiber product is also a single reducible orbit; the reducible locus in
H_{-}^{+}(\rho,\rho)^{\text{red}}
, diffeomorphic to
[0,1]\times\rho
, gives a homotopy between their endpoint maps. Because the reducible orbits in
\rho\times\rho
are isolated, this means the endpoint maps can be identified, so that
\mathcal{M}_{1}(\overline{\rho},\overline{\rho})-\mathcal{M}_{-}(\overline{\rho},\overline{\rho})=\overline{\rho}-\overline{\rho}=0
. So this term in the bimodule homotopy relation is satisfied.

We leave boundary relations
(2,3)
and
(3,2)
to the interested reader; the arguments are no more difficult.
∎

This completes the proof of Theorem
5.5
.

6
Functoriality of equivariant instanton homology

6.1
Cobordism maps in the pseudo-unobstructed case

The results we have thusfar are enough to prove that equivariant instanton homology is a well-defined invariant of rational homology sphere. We described in Definition
5.5
how to define a group which is transparently independent of perturbation, which we called
I^{\bullet}(Y,w)
: we identify the various
I^{\bullet}(Y,w,\pi)
using the continuation maps
\varphi_{\pi\to\pi^{\prime}}
whenever
\sigma_{\pi}\leq\sigma_{\pi^{\prime}}
, which we have established are isomorphisms on equivariant homology. We would like to upgrade
I^{\bullet}(Y,w)
to a functorial invariant. The goal of this section is to establish the following result, whose proof is given at the end of the section.

Theorem 6.1
.

If
(W,c):(Y,w)\to(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime})
is a cobordism for which either
b^{+}(W)=0
or
(W,c)
supports no central connections, there is a well-defined induced map
I^{\bullet}(W,c):I^{\bullet}(Y,w)\to I^{\bullet}(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime})
on equivariant insanton homology, which is a module map in the appropriate sense and respects the exact triangles.

The statement that
(W,c)
supports no central connections is equivalent to the claim that
PD(c)\in H^{2}(W;\mathbb{Z})
is not twice some other class, or equivalently, there exists a closed and oriented surface
\Sigma\subset W
with
\#(\Sigma\cap c)
odd.

We would like to argue as follows. Because
I^{\bullet}(Y,w)
is independent of the choice of perturbation, we might hope to choose perturbations
\pi,\pi^{\prime}
so that
(W,c):(Y,w,\pi)\to(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime},\pi^{\prime})
is an unobstructed cobordism. Then we could define the induced map on equivariant instanton homology
I^{\bullet}(Y,w,\pi)\to I^{\bullet}(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime},\pi^{\prime})
by applying the construction of Theorem
4.3
and then composing with the equivariant homology functors. This induced map
(W,c)_{\pi\to\pi^{\prime}}
descends to a well-defined map on
I^{\bullet}(Y,w)
; to see this, suppose
(\pi_{0},\pi_{0}^{\prime})
is another pair of perturbations for which
(W,c)
is unobstructed and
\sigma_{\pi_{0}}\leq\sigma_{\pi}
while
\sigma_{\pi^{\prime}}\leq\sigma_{\pi_{0}^{\prime}}
. Then the fact that unobstructed cobordism maps compose functorially implies that

\varphi_{\pi^{\prime}\to\pi_{0}^{\prime}}(W,c)_{\pi\to\pi^{\prime}}\varphi_{\pi_{0}\to\pi^{\prime}}=(W,c)_{\pi_{0}\to\pi^{\prime}_{0}}.

We will discuss the structure of this argument in more detail towards the end of this section.

Unfortunately, we usually cannot find perturbations
\pi,\pi^{\prime}
so that
(W,c)
is unobstructed: the impish pseudocentral reducibles are unimpressed by our ability to change perturbations on the ends. Their normal index
N(\Lambda;\pi,\pi^{\prime})
is independent of
\pi
and
\pi^{\prime}
, and this quantity may well be negative. By Lemma
3.5
, the best we can do is find perturbations
(\pi,\pi^{\prime})
so that
(W,c):(Y,w,\pi)\to(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime},\pi^{\prime})
is ‘pseudo-unobstructed’: all its reducibles are either unobstructed or pseudocentral.

To prove Theorem
6.1
, we must construct cobordism maps
\widetilde{C}(Y,w,\pi)\to\widetilde{C}(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime},\pi^{\prime})
associated to pseudo-unobstructed cobordisms, which are well-defined up to equivariant homotopy and compose correctly with the continuation maps.

Now recall the pentachotomy of Proposition
3.5
. In cases (i) and (v), the relevant moduli spaces are cut out transversely (even in families), so that we have already constructed the relevant cobordism maps and verified that they are well-defined up to equivariant homotopy, by Theorem
4.3
. In cases (ii) and (iv), we have an induced map associated to a regular perturbation
\pi_{W}
, but one has to argue carefully to show that the induced map is well-defined up to equivariant homotopy. In case (iii) one has to argue carefully to show that the induced map exists at all.

First we will handle case (iii) of pseudo-unobstructed cobordisms with
b_{1}(W)-b^{+}(W)=0
, explaining both how to guarantee that an induced map exists and why it is well-defined.

Let
(W,c):(Y,w,\pi)\to(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime},\pi^{\prime})
be a pseudo-unobstructed cobordism with
b_{1}(W)-b^{+}(W)=0
; by Proposition
3.5
(iii), we may choose a perturbation
\pi_{W}
on
(W,c)
so that all instantons are cut out transversely except these pseudocentral reducibles, each of which corresponds to a 2-dimensional orbit
\mathcal{O}_{\Lambda}
of framed instantons on
W
whose linearized ASD operator has trivial kernel and cokernel isomorphic to
\mathbb{C}
.

Given
\theta\in\mathfrak{Z}(Y,w)
and
\theta^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{Z}(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime})
, we will write
\mathcal{P}(W,c;\theta,\theta^{\prime})
for the space of pseudocentral instantons on
(W,c)
running from
\theta
to
\theta^{\prime}
. The
SO(3)
-space
\mathcal{P}
is a disjoint union of orbits
\mathcal{O}_{\Lambda}
, each of which is diffeomorphic to
S^{2}
. There is an obstruction bundle
\mathcal{H}_{P}\to\mathcal{P}
, an
SO(3)
-equivariant complex line bundle, assigning to each instanton
\Lambda\in\mathcal{P}
the cokernel of the associated linearized ASD operator.

We will write
\mathfrak{P}(W,c;\theta,\theta^{\prime})=\pi_{0}\mathcal{P}(W,c;\theta,\theta^{\prime})
for a corresponding discrete space of components. When
(W,c)
or
(\theta,\theta^{\prime})
are clear from context, we will suppress them from notation.

Let us briefly discuss orientations; the naturally oriented object is the
0
-dimensional space
\mathfrak{P}(W,c;\theta,\theta^{\prime})
. To see this, recall the conventions of Section
3.7
. So long as one chooses an element of
\Lambda(\theta)
and
\Lambda(\theta^{\prime})
and a homology orientation on the cobordism, the moduli space
\mathfrak{P}(W,c;\theta,\theta^{\prime})
is canonically oriented. More precisely, these choices induce an orientation on the determinant line of the complex
T_{\Lambda}\mathcal{P}\xrightarrow{0}\mathcal{H}_{\Lambda}
; perturbing this so that the map is a nonzero
\Gamma_{\Lambda}
-equivariant map, the determinant line corresponds to the orientation of a generic zero set of a
\Gamma_{\Lambda}
-equivariant section of
\mathcal{H}_{\Lambda}
.

Such a generic section has two zeroes, which are canonically oriented with the same orientation. We thus consider this an orientation on the set
\pi_{0}\mathcal{P}=\mathfrak{P}
.

Remark 6.2
.

Each element of
\mathfrak{P}(W,c;\theta,\theta^{\prime})
is oriented with the same sign, because the corresponding deformation complexes are homotopic. This sign is the same as the sign on the set of central connections
\mathfrak{Z}(W,c;\theta,\theta^{\prime})
, for the same reason. This is mostly interesting when
b_{1}(W)=b^{+}(W)=0
; when
b^{+}(W)>0
our standing assumption is that
(W,c)
supports no central connections, and one can perturb away the abelian connections.
∎

Fix an orbit
\mathcal{O}_{\Lambda}\subset\mathcal{P}(W,c;\theta,\theta^{\prime})
, and write
\lambda:\theta\to\theta^{\prime}
for the corresponding path of connections along
(W,c)
. When carrying out the obstructed gluing theory of Section 5.1, one finds that the ends of the moduli spaces
M^{+}_{z}(W,c;\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})
corresponding to gluing along
\mathcal{O}_{\Lambda}
are modeled on the zero set of an appropriate section:

(l)

If
\alpha^{\prime}=\theta^{\prime}
and
z=\zeta*\lambda
, the corresponding end is modeled on the zero set of an
SO(3)
-equivariant section of the pullback bundle over
(0,\infty)\times\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta}(Y,w;\alpha,\theta)\times\mathcal{O}_{\Lambda}
.

(r)

If
\alpha=\theta
and
z=\lambda*\zeta^{\prime}
, the corresponding end is modeled on the zero set of an
SO(3)
-equivariant section of the pullback bundle over
\mathcal{O}_{\Lambda}\times(-\infty,0)\times\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta^{\prime}}(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime};\theta^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})
.

(c)

If
\alpha\neq\theta
and
\alpha^{\prime}\neq\theta^{\prime}
and
z=\zeta*\lambda*\zeta^{\prime}
, the corresponding end is modeled on the zero set of an
SO(3)
-equivariant section of the pullback bundle over

(0,\infty)\times\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta}(Y,w;\alpha,\theta)\times\mathcal{O}_{\Lambda}\times(-\infty,0)\times\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta^{\prime}}(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime};\theta^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime}).

Applying the modified moduli space ideas of Section 5.2, we interpolate between these sections and a pre-chosen section. In choosing these pre-chosen sections, we will want them to satisfy some special properties.

For each pseudocentral reducible
\Lambda
, we choose priveleged
SO(3)
-equivariant sections

\displaystyle\psi^{\text{priv}}_{\zeta\Lambda}:\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta}(Y,w;\alpha,\theta)\times\mathcal{O}_{\Lambda}\to\mathcal{H}_{\Lambda}

\displaystyle\psi^{\text{priv}}_{\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}:\mathcal{O}_{\Lambda}\times\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta^{\prime}}(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime};\theta^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})\to\mathcal{H}_{\Lambda}

\displaystyle\psi^{\text{priv}}_{\zeta\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}:\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta}(Y,w;\alpha,\theta)\times\mathcal{O}_{\Lambda}\times[0,1]\times\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta^{\prime}}(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime};\theta^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})\to\mathcal{H}_{\Lambda}

which satisfy conditions precisely analagous to those enumerated in Section 5.2. In small degrees, we can understand the zero sets of these sections explicitly.

•

When
\widetilde{i}(\zeta)=1
, the first map has domain equivariantly diffeomorphic to a disjoint union of copies of
SO(3)\times S^{2}
; an
SO(3)
-equivariant section of this bundle may be identified with a section of the complex line bundle over
S^{2}
of Euler class
2
. Equivariance implies that the projection map
\pi:\psi^{-1}(0)\to SO(3)
is a local diffeomorphism, and
\psi^{-1}(0)
consists of a number of copies of
SO(3)
. The Euler class computation shows that the signed count of these is two, so that
\pi:\psi^{-1}(0)\to SO(3)
identifies
\psi^{-1}(0)
as twice the fundamental class in
C_{3}^{gm}SO(3)=\mathbb{Z}
.

•

When
\widetilde{i}(\zeta^{\prime})=\dim\alpha+1
, the second map has domain equivariantly diffeomorphic to a disjoint union of copies of
S^{2}\times SO(3)
, and a similar discussion applies:
\psi^{-1}(0)
can be identified with twice the fundamental class of
SO(3)
.

Now in all of these cases,
\mathopen{}\left(\psi^{\text{priv}}\right)\mathclose{}^{-1}(0)
takes a particularly simple form. In what follows,
\{\Lambda\}
is oriented as the corresponding element of
\mathfrak{P}(W,c;\theta,\theta^{\prime})
.

•

In the first case,
\mathopen{}\left(\psi^{\text{priv}}_{\zeta\Lambda}\right)\mathclose{}^{-1}(0)
has dimension
\widetilde{i}(\zeta)+\dim\alpha-1
. As a geometric chain in
C_{*}(\alpha\times\theta^{\prime})
, this is zero unless
\widetilde{i}(\zeta)=1
, in which case this geometric chain coincides with
2\breve{M}_{\zeta}\times\{\Lambda\}
, as discussed above.

•

In the second case,
\mathopen{}\left(\psi^{\text{priv}}_{\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}\right)\mathclose{}^{-1}(0)
has dimension
\widetilde{i}(\zeta^{\prime})-1
; this is always at least
\dim\alpha^{\prime}
, and this chain is zero as an element of
C_{*}(\theta\times\alpha^{\prime})
unless
\widetilde{i}(\zeta^{\prime})=1+\dim\alpha
, in which case it coincides with
-2\{\Lambda\}\times\breve{M}_{\zeta^{\prime}}
as above. The sign arises because
\{-\infty\}
is a negatively-oriented boundary point in
[-\infty,0)
.

•

In the third case,
\mathopen{}\left(\psi^{\text{priv}}_{\zeta\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}\right)\mathclose{}^{-1}(0)
has dimension
\widetilde{i}(\zeta)+\widetilde{i}(\zeta^{\prime})+\dim\alpha-1
. The term
\widetilde{i}(\zeta)+\dim\alpha-1
is the dimension of
\breve{M}_{\zeta}(\alpha,\theta)
