, which is always at least
\dim\alpha
; the term
\widetilde{i}(\zeta^{\prime})-1
is the dimension of
\breve{M}_{\zeta^{\prime}}(\theta^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})
, which is always at least
\dim\alpha^{\prime}
. It follows that

\widetilde{i}(\zeta)+\widetilde{i}(\zeta^{\prime})+\dim\alpha-1=(\widetilde{i}(\zeta)+\dim\alpha-1)+(\widetilde{i}(\zeta^{\prime})-1)+1\geq\dim\alpha+\dim\alpha^{\prime}+1,

and the chain complex
C_{*}(\alpha\times\alpha^{\prime})
is supported in degrees
*\leq\dim\alpha+\dim\alpha^{\prime}.
So this chain is zero on the chain level.

All this being said, one may follow the recipe of Section 5.2 to produce
modified moduli spaces
associated to
(W,c,\pi_{W})
: these will be moduli spaces
N^{+}_{z}(W,c;\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})
with the usual boundary terms together with, for each
\Lambda
, two oppositely-oriented boundary terms corresponding to the section of
\mathcal{H}_{\Lambda}
arising from obstructed gluing theory, and additional boundary terms corresponding to the zero set of
\psi^{\text{priv}}_{\zeta\Lambda}
or
\psi^{\text{priv}}_{\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}
or
\psi^{\text{priv}}_{\zeta\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}
as appropriate.

Proposition 6.3
.

Let
N^{+}_{z}(W,c;\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})
be the modified moduli spaces constructed above. As geometric chains in
\alpha\times\alpha^{\prime}
, we have the following equalities.

•

If
\alpha^{\prime}=\theta^{\prime}
is central, then

\displaystyle(-1)^{\dim\alpha}\partial N^{+}(W,c;\alpha,\theta^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\beta}\breve{M}^{+}(Y,w;\alpha,\beta)\times_{\beta}N^{+}(W,c;\beta,\theta^{\prime})

\displaystyle+\sum_{\beta^{\prime}}(-1)^{\widetilde{i}(W;\alpha,\beta^{\prime})}N^{+}(W,c;\alpha,\beta^{\prime})\times_{\beta^{\prime}}\breve{M}^{+}(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime};\beta^{\prime},\theta^{\prime})

\displaystyle+\sum_{\theta}2\breve{M}^{+}(Y,w;\alpha,\theta)\times\mathfrak{P}(W,c;\theta,\theta^{\prime}).

•

If
\alpha=\theta
is central, then

\displaystyle\partial N^{+}(W,c;\theta,\alpha^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\beta}\breve{M}^{+}(Y,w;\theta,\beta)\times_{\beta}N^{+}(W,c;\beta,\alpha^{\prime})

\displaystyle+\sum_{\beta^{\prime}}(-1)^{\widetilde{i}(W;\theta,\beta^{\prime})}N^{+}(W,c;\theta,\beta^{\prime})\times_{\beta^{\prime}}\breve{M}^{+}(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime};\beta^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})

\displaystyle+\sum_{\theta^{\prime}}-2\mathfrak{P}(W,c;\theta,\theta^{\prime})\times\breve{M}^{+}(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime};\theta^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime}).

•

If neither
\alpha
nor
\alpha^{\prime}
is central, then

\displaystyle(-1)^{\dim\alpha}\partial N^{+}(W,c;\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\beta}\breve{M}^{+}(Y,w;\alpha,\beta)\times_{\beta}N^{+}(W,c;\beta,\alpha^{\prime})

\displaystyle+\sum_{\beta^{\prime}}(-1)^{\widetilde{i}(W;\alpha,\beta^{\prime})}N^{+}(W,c;\alpha,\beta^{\prime})\times_{\beta^{\prime}}\breve{M}^{+}(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime};\beta^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime}).

Given this, we may define a bimodule
\mathcal{I}(W,c)
associated to the cobordism
(W,c)
, together with the necessary additional data of a homology orientation and a path between the basepoints on the boundary components. We define this by the formula

\mathcal{I}(W,c)(\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})=N^{+}(W,c;\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})+2\mathfrak{P}(W,c;\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}).

(6.4)

Here we interpret the final term
\mathfrak{P}(W,c;\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})
as a discrete set of points which is empty unless
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}
are both central, in which case it is the set of pseudocentral reducibles running from
\alpha
to
\alpha^{\prime}
, with the orientation discussed at the start of the section. Notice that if
\Lambda:\alpha\to\alpha^{\prime}
is pseudocentral, we have
\widetilde{i}(W;\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})=0
, so this has the correct expected dimension.

This gives an induced map between instanton chain complexes for a chosen pseudo-regular perturbation
\pi_{W}
on
(W,c)
. When arguing that the induced map is well-defined, one may carry out essentially the same construction for the moduli spaces
M^{+}_{z}(W,c,I;\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})
for a 1-parameter family of metrics and perturbations: one needs to delete a neighborhood of the stratum
[0,1]\times\mathcal{P}(W,c)
from all the compactified moduli spaces for 1-parameter families of metric and perturbation.

Thus the boundary relations above (and the corresponding version in the case of 1-parameter families) establishes the following.

Corollary 6.5
.

Whenever
(W,c):(Y,w,\pi)\to(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime},\pi^{\prime})
is a pseudo-unobstructed cobordism with
b_{1}(W)-b^{+}(W)=0
, there is a natural map
\widetilde{C}(Y,w,\pi)\to\widetilde{C}(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime},\pi^{\prime})
, independent of the choice of perturbation on
(W,c)
up to equivariant chain homotopy.

Before moving on to the remaining cases, we take this opportunity to point out that the components of
\widetilde{C}(W,c)
are determined by explicit cohomological information. Write
r_{W}:H^{2}(W;\mathbb{Z})_{\text{tors}}\to H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z})\oplus H^{2}(Y^{\prime};\mathbb{Z})
. Recall here from Lemma
2
that a central connection
\theta
on
(Y,w)
corresponds to a cohomology class
x\in H^{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z})
with
2x=PD(w)
, and similarly on
(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime})
. The statement of the following proposition uses the set of pairs

\tilde{Z}(W,c;\theta,\theta^{\prime})=\big\{(y,y^{\prime})\in H^{2}(W;\mathbb{Z})\times H^{2}(W;\mathbb{Z})\mid r_{W}(y)=r_{W}(y^{\prime})=(x,x^{\prime})\text{ and }y-y^{\prime}\text{ is torsion}\big\}.

Proposition 6.6
.

Suppose
(W,c):(Y,w,\pi)\to(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime},\pi^{\prime})
is a pseudo-unobstructed cobordism with
b_{1}(W)=b^{+}(W)=0
. If
\theta\in\mathfrak{Z}(Y,w)
and
\theta^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{Z}(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime})
and
x,x^{\prime}
are the corresponding cohomology classes, the component of the map
\widetilde{C}(W,c)
running from
C_{*}(\theta)=\mathbb{Z}
to
C_{*}(\theta^{\prime})=\mathbb{Z}
is multiplication by
\pm\big|\tilde{Z}(W,c;\theta,\theta^{\prime})|
.

Proof.

The only component of
N^{+}(W,c;\theta,\theta^{\prime})
which is 0-dimensional is the component of central connections. By the definition of
\mathcal{I}(W,c)(\theta,\theta^{\prime})
in Equation
6.4
, the induced map
\theta\to\theta^{\prime}
is given by the signed count of
\mathfrak{Z}(W,c;\theta,\theta^{\prime})+2\mathfrak{P}(W,c;\theta,\theta^{\prime})
. Finally, the sign on every point is the same by Remark
6.1
, so we just need to determine the number of points in this count.

Recall from Proposition
3.5
that a central connection on
(W,c)
extending
\theta
and
\theta^{\prime}
corresponds to a cohomology class
y\in H^{2}(W;\mathbb{Z})
with
2y=PD(c)
and
r_{W}(y)=(x,x^{\prime})
, where
x
and
x^{\prime}
are the cohomology classes corresponding to
\theta
and
\theta^{\prime}
. An abelian connection running between these is identified with a pair
\{y,y^{\prime}\}
with
y+y^{\prime}=PD(c)
and
y\neq y^{\prime}
for which
r_{W}(y)=r_{W}(y^{\prime})=(x,x^{\prime})
. Finally, Definition
3.5
says that such an abelian connection is called pseudocentral precisely when
(y^{\prime}-y)^{2}=0
. Because
b^{+}(W)=0
, this is true if and only if
y^{\prime}-y
is torsion.

There is a natural involution
\iota(y,y^{\prime})=(y^{\prime},y)
; the pseudocentral reducibles correspond to free orbits of
\iota
, while the central reducibles correspond to fixed points of
\iota
. It follows that

\big|\tilde{Z}(W,c;\theta,\theta^{\prime})\big|=\big|\mathfrak{Z}(W,c;\theta,\theta^{\prime})\big|+2\big|\mathfrak{P}(W,c;\theta,\theta^{\prime})\big|,

as desired.
∎

Remark 6.7
.

Consider the special case that
PD(c)
is the sum of a torsion class and an even class which vanishes on the boundary:
PD(c)=2a+t
where
r_{W}(a)=0
. Then the set
\tilde{Z}(W,c;\theta,\theta^{\prime})
is in bijection with
r_{W}^{-1}(x,x^{\prime})
, via the map
z\mapsto(a+z,a+t-z)
. When
w,w^{\prime},
and
c
are furthermore empty (so that the corresponding
U(2)
-bundle is trivial) and
\theta,\theta^{\prime}
are taken to be the trivial connection, this set is in bijection with
\text{ker}(r_{W})
, and in particular guaranteed to be nonempty.
∎

We move on to verifying that the induced map is well-defined in the remaining cases.

Proposition 6.8
.

Suppose
(W,c):(Y,w,\pi)\to(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime},\pi^{\prime})
is a pseudo-unobstructed cobordism with
b_{1}(W)-b^{+}(W)=-1
. For a pseudo-regular path of perturbations
\pi^{t}_{W}
, the modified moduli spaces
N_{z}^{+}(W,c,I;\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})
define an
SO(3)
-equivariant bimodule homotopy.

Proof.

Recall that being pseudo-regular means here that the ASD operator associated to
\Lambda
has trivial kernel and cokernel isomorphic to
\mathbb{C}^{2}
, which assembles into an
SO(3)
-equivariant rank 2 complex vector bundle
\mathcal{H}_{\Lambda}
over
\mathcal{O}_{\Lambda}
.

Given a pseudocentral
\Lambda:\theta\to\theta^{\prime}
in the family moduli space, consider a neighborhood of
\breve{M}_{\zeta}(\alpha,\theta)\times\mathcal{O}_{\Lambda}
in
M_{z}^{+}(W,c,I;\theta,\alpha^{\prime})
; here
\widetilde{i}(z)=\widetilde{i}(\zeta)+\widetilde{i}(\lambda)=\widetilde{i}(\zeta)-3
. Truncating and modifying as usual, we see that the modified moduli spaces will have an additional boundary component corresponding to the zero set of a priveleged section

\breve{M}_{\zeta}(\alpha,\theta)\times\mathcal{O}_{\Lambda}\to\mathcal{H}_{\Lambda}.

The zero set is a free
SO(3)
-space with dimension
\dim\alpha+\widetilde{i}(\zeta)-3
, so can only be nonempty of this quantity exceeds
3
; it is also only non-trivial in
C_{*}(\alpha\times\theta^{\prime})
if this quantity is at most
\dim\alpha
. These are only simultaneously possible when
\alpha
is irreducible and
\widetilde{i}(\zeta)=3
.

In this case, one may restrict this
SO(3)
-equivariant section to a non-equivariant map
\breve{M}_{\zeta}^{\text{Fiber}}(\alpha,\theta)\times\mathcal{O}_{\Lambda}\to\mathcal{H}_{\Lambda}
, and conversely induce an
SO(3)
-equivariant section on the total space from a non-equivariant one on this subspace. Because this rank 2 complex vector bundle is pulled back from the second factor, there exists a nowhere-vanishing section (for instance, the composite of projection to the second factor and a nowhere-vanishing section
\mathcal{O}_{\Lambda}\to\mathcal{H}_{\Lambda}
, which exists for dimension reasons). Choose such a non-vanishing section for
\psi_{\zeta\Lambda}^{\text{priv}}
. Running the same argument on the other side, we may do the same for
\psi_{\Lambda\zeta^{\prime}}
.

Then the boundary relations analogous to Proposition
6.1
have no factors corresponding to factorization through
\Lambda
in the dimension range where such factors could be nonzero, so the modified moduli spaces define a bimodule homotopy.
∎

This establishes that the induced map is well-defined up to equivariant homotopy in the case
b_{1}(W)-b^{+}(W)=-1
. The only remaining case in the pentachotomy is
b_{1}(W)-b^{+}(W)=1
. This case is in some sense more irritating than the others, because in 1-parameter families there may exist pseudocentral reducibles which have both non-trivial kernel and cokernel. The preceding gluing analysis does not apply here. Fortunately, this also doesn’t matter: the obstructed reducibles only appear in moduli spaces of large dimension.

Lemma 6.9
.

If
(W,c):(Y,w,\pi)\to(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime},\pi^{\prime})
is a pseudo-unobstructed cobordism with
b_{1}(W)-b^{+}(W)=1
, and
M^{+}_{z}(W,c,I;\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})
is a family moduli space which contains a broken trajectory factoring through a pseudocentral reducible
\Lambda:\theta\to\theta^{\prime}
, we have
\widetilde{i}(z)+1\geq\dim\alpha^{\prime}+4
, so that this moduli space has dimension at least
\dim\alpha+\dim\alpha^{\prime}+4
.

Proof.

Supposing this moduli space contains the broken instanton
(\mathbf{A},\Lambda,\mathbf{A}^{\prime})
, write
z=\zeta*\lambda*\zeta^{\prime}
, where
\zeta:\alpha\to\theta
and
\zeta^{\prime}:\theta^{\prime}\to\alpha^{\prime}
are the paths of connections along
Y
(resp
Y^{\prime}
) given by
\mathbf{A}
(resp
\mathbf{A}^{\prime}
); here
\zeta
and
\zeta^{\prime}
may be constant if, for instance, this moduli space runs from
\theta
to
\alpha^{\prime}
or
\alpha
to
\theta^{\prime}
. For any transversely cut out trajectory on a cylinder
\zeta:\alpha\to\theta
we have
\widetilde{i}(\zeta)\geq 0
(with
\widetilde{i}(\zeta)\geq 1
if this is a nonconstant trajectory) and for
\zeta^{\prime}:\theta\to\alpha^{\prime}
we have
\widetilde{i}(\zeta^{\prime})\geq\dim\alpha^{\prime}
because the fiber over
\theta^{\prime}
is the moduli space itself, which maps submersively onto
\alpha^{\prime}
(with this framed index being larger than
\dim\alpha^{\prime}+1
if the trajectory is nonconstant).

Now in general for a reducible
\Lambda:\theta\to\theta^{\prime}
we have

\widetilde{i}(\lambda)=(b_{1}(W)-b^{+}(W)-\dim\Gamma_{\Lambda})+N(\Lambda)+3;

the first term is the index of the deformation complex internal to the
SO(2)
-reducible locus, the second the index of the deformation complex normal to that locus, and the last term
\dim\Gamma_{\theta}
, as in Definition
3.2
. If
\Lambda
is pseudocentral,
N(\Lambda)=2(b_{1}(W)-b^{+}(W)-1)
and
\dim\Gamma_{\Lambda}=1
. so that this reduces to
\widetilde{i}(\lambda)=3(b_{1}(W)-b^{+}(W))=3
in our case of interest.

Thus in this case
\widetilde{i}(\lambda)=3
. By additivity, we have
\widetilde{i}(z)=\widetilde{i}(\zeta)+\widetilde{i}(\lambda)+\widetilde{i}(\zeta^{\prime})\geq 3+\dim\alpha^{\prime}
, which gives the inequality stated in the Lemma.
∎

Now a variant on Lemma
4.2
stated for bimodule homotopies instead of flow categories shows that to define the relevant bimodule homotopy, we only need those moduli spaces
M^{+}_{z}(W,c,I;\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})
of dimension
\leq\dim\alpha+\dim\alpha^{\prime}+1
. In this dimension range, all of our moduli spaces are smooth and satisfy the expected boundary relations. It follows that even in this case the induced map
\widetilde{C}(W,c,\pi_{W})
is independent of
\pi_{W}
up to equivariant homotopy, which we have now established in all cases.

Proof of Theorem
6.1
.

Observe that when
(W_{1},c_{1})
and
(W_{2},c_{2})
are composable pseudo-unobstructed cobordisms, the composite is also pseudo-unobstructed, and the bimodules described above compose functorially up to equivariant homotopy (by a small variation on the standard argument).

For each regular perturbation
\pi
we have a well-defined equivariant instanton homology group
I^{\bullet}(Y,w;\pi)
. We write
\pi\leq\pi^{\prime}
to mean that their signature data satisfy
\sigma_{\pi}\leq\sigma_{\pi^{\prime}}
. For each
\pi\leq\sigma^{\prime}
we have a continuation map
\varphi_{\pi\to\pi^{\prime}}
between these, which is an isomorphism by Theorem
5.5
, and these compose as you expect. The homology group
I^{\bullet}(Y,w)
is defined by identifying all of these groups for varying
I^{\bullet}(Y,w;\sigma)
using the continuation maps.

To rephrase this, we have a preordered set
\Pi(Y,w)
, the set of regular perturbations
\pi
on
(Y,w)
. When we have
\pi\leq\pi^{\prime}
, we can identify the associated instanton homology groups via the continuation maps. Finally, for any two perturbations, there is always a larger one, so we can identify them both by passing to that larger one. We will use this same recipe for cobordism maps.

For a cobordism, there is a set
\Pi(W,c)\subset\Pi(Y,w)\times\Pi(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime})
of pairs
(\pi,\pi^{\prime})
so that
(W,c):(Y,w,\pi)\to(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime},\pi^{\prime})
is pseudo-unobstructed. This set is nonempty by Lemma
3.5
. For each such, we have defined a module map
I^{\bullet}(W,c)_{\pi\to\pi^{\prime}}:I^{\bullet}(Y,w,\pi)\to I^{\bullet}(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime},\pi^{\prime})
respecting the exact triangles by applying the equivariant instanton homology functors to the map
\widetilde{C}(W,c)
described above, which we have now verified are well-defined up to equivariant homotopy. We abbreviate this to
(W,c)_{\pi\to\pi^{\prime}}
for convenience.

To see that this gives a well-defined map
I^{\bullet}(Y,w)\to I^{\bullet}(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime})
, we need to show that for any two pairs
(\pi,\pi^{\prime})\in\Pi(W,c)
, the maps
(W,c)_{\pi\to\pi^{\prime}}
are related by the relevant continuation maps.

By the index formula, if
(\pi_{1},\pi^{\prime}_{1})\in\Pi(W,c)
and we have
\pi_{0}\leq\pi_{1}
and
\pi^{\prime}_{1}\leq\pi^{\prime}_{2}
, then
(\pi_{0},\pi_{2}^{\prime})\in\Pi(W,c)
as well. By the functoriality mentioned at the beginning of this proof, we have

\varphi_{\pi_{1}^{\prime}\to\pi_{2}^{\prime}}(W,c)_{\pi_{1}\to\pi_{1}^{\prime}}\varphi_{\pi_{0}\to\pi_{1}}=(W,c)_{\pi_{0}\to\pi_{2}^{\prime}}.

Given any
(\pi_{0},\pi_{0}^{\prime})
and
(\pi_{1},\pi_{1}^{\prime})
in
\Pi(W,c)
, pick a perturbation
\pi_{2}
with
\sigma_{\pi_{2}}=\text{min}(\sigma_{\pi_{0}},\sigma_{\pi_{1}})
and a perturbation
\pi_{2}^{\prime}
with
\sigma_{\pi_{2}^{\prime}}=\text{max}(\sigma_{\pi_{0}}^{\prime},\sigma_{\pi_{1}}^{\prime})
. We have
(\sigma_{2},\sigma^{\prime})\in\Pi(W,c)
; the procedure above identifies both maps
(W,c)_{\pi_{i}\to\pi_{i}^{\prime}}
with
(W,c)_{\pi_{2}\to\pi_{2}^{\prime}}
, and hence with each other, using the various continuation maps. It is not much more work to show that the identification induced by any other pair
(\pi_{3},\pi_{3}^{\prime})
with
\pi_{3}\leq\pi_{2}
and
\pi_{2}^{\prime}\leq\pi_{3}^{\prime}
agrees with the identification induced by
(\pi_{2},\pi_{2}^{\prime})
.

Altogether, this shows that the cobordism maps given above assemble into a well-defined map between
I^{\bullet}(Y,w)\to I^{\bullet}(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime})
.
∎

Remark 6.10
.

In practice, there are two ways to describe the induced map. If one feels free to choose the perturbations
\pi,\pi^{\prime}
, then one can say the induced map
I^{\bullet}(W,c)
is defined by choosing perturbations
(\pi,\pi^{\prime})
so that
(W,c)
is pseudo-unobstructed, and defining this to be the map
I^{\bullet}(Y,w,\pi)\to I^{\bullet}(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime},\pi^{\prime})
given by applying the equivariant homology functor to the construction described earlier in this section.

On the other hand, if one wishes to define the induced map
I^{\bullet}(W,c)_{\pi_{0}\to\pi_{0}^{\prime}}:I^{\bullet}(Y,w,\pi_{0})\to I^{\bullet}(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime},\pi_{0}^{\prime})
between perturbations where
(W,c)
is not necessarily pseudo-unobstructed, instead one should choose new perturbations
\pi_{1},\pi_{1}^{\prime}
with
(W,c):(Y,w,\pi_{1})\to(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime},\pi_{1}^{\prime})
pseudo-unobstructed, and with
\sigma_{\pi_{1}}\leq\sigma_{\pi_{0}}
and
\sigma_{\pi_{0}^{\prime}}\leq\sigma_{\pi_{1}^{\prime}}
; then the construction above defines

I^{\bullet}(W,c)_{\pi_{0}\to\pi_{0}^{\prime}}=\varphi_{\pi_{0}^{\prime}\to\pi_{1}^{\prime}}^{-1}I^{\bullet}(W,c)_{\pi_{1}\to\pi_{1}^{\prime}}\varphi_{\pi_{1}\to\pi_{0}}^{-1},

using that the continuation maps are known to be isomorphisms on equivariant instanton homology by Theorem
5.5
.
∎

6.2
Composites

Theorem
6.1
establishes that each cobordism
(W,c):(Y,w)\to(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime})
between admissible pairs induces a well-defined cobordism map. What remains is to establish that these compose functorially. If
(W_{1},c_{1}):(Y_{0},w_{0})\to(Y_{1},w_{1})
and
(W_{2},c_{2}):(Y_{1},w_{1})\to(Y_{2},w_{2})
are cobordisms, write
(W_{12},c_{12}):(Y_{0},w_{0})\to(Y_{2},w_{2})
for their composite, obtained by pasting the two cobordisms along
(Y_{1},w_{1})
.

As mentioned at the beginning of the proof of Theorem
6.1
, if
\pi_{i}
are regular perturbations on the respective admissible pairs
(Y_{i},w_{i})
for which
(W_{i},c_{i})
are pseudo-unobstructed, the composite bimodule
\mathcal{I}(W_{2},c_{2})\circ\mathcal{I}(W_{1},c_{1})
is homotopic to
\mathcal{I}(W_{12},c_{12})
. Unfortunately, it is not always possible to choose
\pi_{i}
so that all three of these cobordisms are pseudo-unobstructed, even in favorable-seeming situations. For instance, suppose
b_{1}(W_{i})=b^{+}(W_{i})=0
for
i=1,2,12
, and that
W_{12}
supports a pseudocentral reducible
\Lambda:\theta_{0}\to\theta_{2}
which restricts to
(Y_{1},w_{1})
as an
abelian
connection
\rho_{1}
.

In this situation, the normal index is additive; writing
\Lambda_{i}
for the restriction of
\Lambda
to
W_{i}
, we see that
N(\Lambda_{1})+N(\Lambda_{2})=N(\Lambda)=-2
. It follows that
no matter what perturbations we choose on the
Y_{i}
, one of
W_{1}
or
W_{2}
must be obstructed:
neither
\Lambda_{i}
is a pseudocentral reducible, as it is abelian on one end, but one of them must have negative normal index.

The best we can prove is the following.

Lemma 6.11
.

Suppose
(W_{i},c_{i})
are composable cobordisms with
b^{+}(W_{i})-b_{1}(W_{i})\geq 0
, and for which either
b^{+}(W_{i})=0
or
(W_{i},c)
supports no central connections. Then there exist perturbations
\pi_{0},\pi_{1}^{\pm},\pi_{2}
on
(Y_{i},w_{i})
so that

(W_{1},c_{1}):(Y_{0},w_{0},\pi_{0})\to(Y_{1},w_{1},\pi_{1}^{+}),

(W_{2},c_{2}):(Y_{1},w_{1},\pi_{1}^{-})\to(Y_{2},w_{2},\pi_{2}),

(W_{12},c_{12}):(Y_{0},w_{0},\pi_{0})\to(Y_{2},w_{2},\pi_{2})

are all pseudo-unobstructed, and
0\leq\sigma_{\pi_{1}^{+}}-\sigma_{\pi_{1}^{-}}\leq 4
.

Proof.

This is really a lemma about signature data functions, so we speak in terms of signature data functions
\sigma_{i}
instead of perturbations
\pi_{i}
; choose one on each
(Y_{i},w_{i})
to start with. Write
F_{Y,w}=\text{Map}(\mathfrak{A}(Y,w),2\mathbb{Z})
. Just as in the proof of Lemma
3.5
, we will consider the normal index of a reducible as a function

N_{i}:\mathfrak{A}(W_{i},c_{i})\times F_{Y_{i-1},w_{i-1}}\times F_{Y_{i},w_{i}}\to 2\mathbb{Z}

with

N_{i}(\Lambda,f_{i-1},f_{i})=N_{i}(\Lambda,\sigma_{i-1},\sigma_{i})+\frac{f_{i}(r_{i}\Lambda)-f_{i-1}(r_{i-1}\Lambda)}{2}

for
i=1,2
, and similarly for
i=12
. Here
r_{i}
is restriction to
Y_{i}
. For convenience below,
\rho
refers to an abelian flat connection on a 3-manifold, while
\theta
to a central flat connection.

If
b_{1}(Y_{1})>0
then
Y_{1}
is admissible and the cobordisms
(W_{i},c_{i})
support no reducible instantons; the relevant unobstructedness conditions hold tautologically for both
W_{1}
and
W_{2}
. The composite
W_{12}
need not be unobstructed, but Lemma
3.5
shows that one may choose the signature data on the ends appropriately so that it is. A similar discussion applies for
Y_{0}
and
Y_{2}
; for the remainder of the proof we assume all
Y_{i}
are rational homology spheres.

If
N_{12}
is the corresponding normal index function for the composite cobordism, we have an additivity property (thanks, in part, to the assumption that
b_{1}(Y_{1})=0
):

N_{12}(\Lambda,f_{0},f_{2})=N_{1}(\Lambda|_{W_{1}},f_{0},f_{1})+N_{2}(\Lambda|_{W_{2}},f_{1},f_{2})+c(\Lambda|_{Y_{1}}),

where
c(\alpha)=0
if
\alpha
is abelian and
c(\alpha)=2
if
\alpha
is central. Our goal is to choose
f_{0},f_{1},f_{2}
so that
N_{1}\geq b_{1}(W_{1})-b^{+}(W_{1})
and
N_{12}\geq b_{1}(W_{12})-b^{+}(W_{12})
while
N_{2}\geq b_{1}(W_{2})-b^{+}(W_{2})-2
; then the desired signature data will be given by
\sigma_{0}+f_{0}
and
\sigma_{2}+f_{2}
, while
\sigma_{1}^{+}=\sigma_{1}+f_{1}
and
\sigma_{1}^{-}=\sigma_{1}+f_{1}-2
.

To ensure that thorese inequalities hold for every reducible running
\Lambda_{1}:\theta_{0}\to\rho_{1}
along the first cobordism and
\Lambda_{2}:\rho_{1}\to\theta_{2}
along the second, we are obligated to choose
f_{1}
first. In this case, we have

N_{1}(\Lambda_{1},f_{0},f_{1})=N_{1}(\Lambda_{1};\sigma_{0},\sigma_{1})+\frac{f_{1}(\rho_{1})}{2}\quad\text{and}\quad N_{2}(\Lambda_{2},f_{1},f_{2})=N_{2}(\Lambda_{2};\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})-\frac{f_{1}(\rho_{1})}{2}.

The desired inequalities on
N_{1}
and
N_{2}
are equivalent to

2N_{2}(\Lambda_{2};\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})-2\big(b_{1}(W_{2})-b^{+}(W_{2})\big)+4\geq f_{1}(\rho_{1})\geq 2\big(b_{1}(W_{1})-b^{+}(W_{1})\big)-2N_{1}(\Lambda_{1};\sigma_{0},\sigma_{1})

for all abelian flat connections
\rho_{1}
and all reducible instantons
\Lambda_{i}
which are asymptotic to
\rho_{1}
at the appropriate end and a central connection on the other end.

Such a function exists if and only if

2N_{1}(\Lambda_{1};\sigma_{0},\sigma_{1})+2N_{2}(\Lambda_{2};\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})-2\big(b_{1}(W_{1})+b_{1}(W_{2})-b^{+}(W_{1})-b^{+}(W_{2})\big)+4\geq 0

for all
\Lambda_{1}:\theta_{0}\to\rho_{1}
and
\Lambda_{2}:\rho_{1}\to\theta_{2}
.

By additivity of index and the fact that
Y_{1}
is a rational homology sphere, this is equivalent to the inequality

N_{12}(\Lambda_{12};\sigma_{0},\sigma_{2})\geq b_{1}(W_{12})-b^{+}(W_{12})-2.

Finally, the index formula Proposition
3.5
implies
N_{12}\geq 2b_{1}-2b^{+}-2
, and the assumption that
b_{1}-b^{+}\geq 0
implies that this is at least
b_{1}-b^{+}-2
. Therefore one may choose
f_{1}
so that the given inequality holds. (If
b_{1}-b^{+}\geq 2
, it follows that in fact choose
\pi_{1},\pi_{2},\pi_{3}
so that
N_{i}(\Lambda_{i})\geq 0
for all
i
.)

This establishes the desired inequality for all abelian instantons
\Lambda_{1}:\theta_{0}\to\rho_{1}
and
\Lambda_{2}:\rho_{1}\to\theta_{2}
with this choice of
f_{1}
; this choice is irrelevant to
W_{12}
.

This is the only difficult point; the rest of the argument follows as in Lemma
3.5
. In fact, with these choices of signature data, the only obstructed non-pseudocentral reducibles
\Lambda_{2}
on
(W_{2},c_{2})
run from an abelian connection to a central connection.
∎

Because the situation in the preceding Lemma is quite subtle, so too is proving functoriality of the induced maps. As we will see in the proof of the following Theorem, establishing this requires slightly more general versions of our previous constructions. Instead of carrying out the necessary mild generalizations of previous constructions in detail we content ourselves to a sketch of the proof of functoriality.

Theorem 6.12
.

Suppose
(W_{1},c_{1}):(Y_{0},w_{0})\to(Y_{1},w_{1})
and
(W_{2},c_{2}):(Y_{1},w_{1})\to(Y_{2},c_{2})
are cobordisms between admissible pairs which have either
b^{+}(W_{i})=0
or that
(W_{i},c_{i})
supports no central connections. Then the same is true for the composite
(W_{12},c_{12})
, and the cobordism maps defined in Theorem
6.1
satisfy

I^{\bullet}(W_{2},c_{2})\circ I^{\bullet}(W_{1},c_{1})=I^{\bullet}(W_{12},c_{12}).

Sketch of proof.

If
(W_{12},c_{12})
supports central connections, then by restriction so too do both
(W_{i},c_{i})
(and neither end is admissible, so all boundary components are rational homology spheres); in this case, we must have
b^{+}(W_{1})=b^{+}(W_{2})=0
by assumption, but because
Y_{1}
is a rational homology sphere this implies
b^{+}(W_{12})=0
as well, proving the first claim about the composite.

As for the cobordism maps, to take advantage of Lemma
6.2
we want to reduce to the case
b_{1}(W_{i})-b^{+}(W_{i})\geq 0
for both
i=1,2
. First we handle the case where at least one of these is negative. By an argument along the lines of Lemma
6.2
, we may choose perturbations
\pi_{0},\pi_{1},\pi_{2}
so that one of
(W_{i},c_{i})
is a pseudo-unobstructed cobordism and the other — while not pseudo-unobstructed — has
b_{1}(W_{i})-b^{+}(W_{i})<0
. Because
b^{+}(W_{i})<0
, this implies that there exists a regular perturbation
\pi^{W}_{i}
on this cobordism with no reducible connections whatsoever; the same is necessarily true for
\pi^{W}_{1}\cup_{L}\pi^{W}_{2}
when the length of the neck
L
is sufficiently large. The stated result follows in this case by a standard neck-stretching argument which implies that the induced map on the composite is the same as the map induced by
\pi^{W}_{1}\cup_{L}\pi^{W}_{2}
for sufficiently large
L
.

So we assume
b_{1}(W_{i})-b^{+}(W_{i})\geq 0
for both
i=1,2
, and suppose that we have chosen perturbations
\pi_{0},\pi_{1}^{\pm},\pi_{2}
as in the statement of Lemma
6.2
.

Now the situation is much more complicated. If we can establish the existence of the diagram below, as well as show that it is homotopy-commutative, we will have proved the desired theorem. In this diagram, we write for instance
\widetilde{C}(\pi_{0})
in place of the full notation
\widetilde{C}(Y_{0},w_{0},\pi_{0};R)
for the sake of space, and similarly we suppress the geometric representatives
c
. On the other hand, we include the perturbations on the ends as part of the notation for clarity.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{C}(\pi_{1}^{+})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{C}(\pi_{0})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S\widetilde{C}(\pi_{1}^{-})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S\widetilde{C}(\pi_{2})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{C}(\pi_{1}^{-})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{C}(\pi_{2})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\text{Cyl}}^{1^{+}\to 1^{-}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W_{1}^{0\to 1^{+}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{W_{1}}^{0\to 1^{-}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Sigma W_{2})^{1^{-}\to 2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W_{2}^{1^{-}\to 2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W_{12}^{0\to 2}}

A handful of notational points need to be explained here. The existence of a handful of these
SO(3)
-equivariant chain complexes and chain maps are slight generalizations of work carried out earlier in this article.

•

Here
S\widetilde{C}(\pi_{1}^{-})
and
S\widetilde{C}(\pi_{2})
are defined by a variation on the blowup construction of Section
5.3
, where we blow up simultaneously at
every
abelian reducible. This variation can be constructed by hand or by iteratively applying the version of the construction where we blow up at a single abelian reducible.

•

The maps
\Sigma_{1},\Sigma_{2}
are defined by a variation on the map defined in Construction
5.3
, with the difference being that
\Sigma_{i}(\rho,S_{\rho})=S_{\rho}
for
every
abelian reducible
\rho
. Again, these induce an isomorphism on homology, and therefore equivariant homology.

•

The maps
\widetilde{\text{Cyl}}^{1^{+}\to 1^{-}}
and
\widetilde{W}_{1}^{0\to 1^{-}}
are defined by a variation on the construction of Section
5.4
of the bimodule of an obstructed cobordism, applied to the cylinder
\mathbb{R}\times Y
and the cobordism
W_{1}
; this variation allows for more than one obstructed abelian connection
\Lambda
, which may run from a central connection to an abelian connection. A careful reading shows that the construction and proofs of facts regarding these maps go through with minimal change; the only crucial points are that all obstructed abelian connections run
\alpha\to\rho
where
\rho
is abelian (but
\alpha
may be abelian or central), and each has normal index
-2
.

•

The bimodule inducing the map
(\Sigma W_{2})^{1^{-}\to 2}
is defined by applying Construction
5.3
to the bimodule
\Sigma_{2}\circ W_{2}^{1^{-}\to 2}
; it can also be constructed by hand with little difficulty.

Now we assert that this diagram commutes up to homotopy. In particular, we assert the existence of equivariant homotopies between the following pairs of maps:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\text{Cyl}}^{1^{+}\to 1^{-}}\circ\text{Cyl}^{1^{-}\to 1^{+}}

\displaystyle\sim\Sigma_{1}

\displaystyle\widetilde{\text{Cyl}}^{1^{+}\to 1^{-}}\circ W_{1}^{0\to 1^{+}}

\displaystyle\sim\widetilde{W_{1}}^{0\to 1^{-}}

\displaystyle(\Sigma W_{2})^{1^{-}\to 2}\circ\Sigma_{1}

\displaystyle\sim\Sigma_{2}\circ W_{2}^{1^{-}\to 2}

\displaystyle(\Sigma W_{2})^{1^{-}\to 2}\circ\widetilde{W}_{1}^{0\to 1^{-}}

\displaystyle\sim\Sigma_{2}\circ W_{12}^{0\to 2}.

The first statement was established in the proof of Theorem
5.5
; here
\text{Cyl}^{1^{-}\to 1^{+}}:\widetilde{C}(\pi_{1}^{-})\to\widetilde{C}(\pi_{1}^{+})
is the continuation map induced by the unobstructed cylinder.

The second statement follows by yet more obstructed gluing theory in the spirit of Section
5
, now applied to the obstructed cobordism given by the composite of
W_{1}
and the obstructed cylinder
\mathbb{R}\times Y_{1}:(Y_{1},\pi_{1}^{+})\to(Y_{1},\pi_{1}^{-})
; the modified moduli space ideas apply with minimal change.

The third statement is tautological: these maps are in fact identically equal.

The final statement is more difficult, but is rather similar to the arguments carried out in Section
5.5
. Again, this amounts to using obstructed gluing theory to construct the appropriate modified moduli spaces, and recognizing that their boundary relations establish a bimodule homotopy between the two composite bimodules represented by this relation.

Now recall our goal. Abusing notation, we write
W
also for the induced map on equivariant homology
I^{\bullet}
; for consistency with previous sections we write
\varphi_{1^{-}\to 1^{+}}
as the map induced by the unobstructed continuation map and
\widetilde{\varphi}_{1^{+}\to 1^{-}}
for the map induced by the obstructed continuation map. As discussed in Remark
6.1
, the composite

I^{\bullet}(W_{2},c_{2})\circ I^{\bullet}(W_{1},c_{1}):I^{\bullet}(\pi_{0})\to I^{\bullet}(\pi_{2})

is defined to be the composite

W_{2}^{1^{-}\to 2}\circ\varphi_{1^{-}\to 1^{+}}^{-1}\circ W_{1}^{0\to 1^{+}},

and we want to show that this is equal to the map
W_{12}^{0\to 2}
.

This follows purely formally using the identities above. Applying each of these in turn (occasionally rearranging using that
\Sigma_{i}
and
\varphi
are isomorphisms on equivariant homology), we find the desired equality:

\displaystyle W_{2}^{1^{-}\to 2}\varphi_{1^{-}\to 1^{+}}^{-1}W_{1}^{0\to 1^{+}}

\displaystyle=W_{2}^{1^{-}\to 2}\Sigma_{1}^{-1}\widetilde{\varphi}_{1^{+}\to 1^{-}}W_{1}^{0\to 1^{+}}

\displaystyle=W_{2}^{1^{-}\to 2}\Sigma_{1}^{-1}\widetilde{W_{1}}^{0\to 1^{-}}

\displaystyle=\Sigma_{2}^{-1}(\Sigma W_{2})^{1^{-}\to 2}\widetilde{W_{1}}^{0\to 1^{-}}

\displaystyle=\Sigma_{2}^{-1}\Sigma_{2}W_{12}^{0\to 2}=W_{12}^{0\to 2}.\qed

Altogether, we have proven Theorem
2
(i):
I^{\bullet}
is functorial on a category whose objects are admissible pairs
(Y,w)
with a basepoint on
Y
, and whose morphisms are cobordisms
(W,c)
equipped with a homology orientation and a path between the basepoints, where either
b^{+}(W)=0
or
(W,c)
admits no central connections.

6.3
The case
b_{2}^{+}>0
via the blowup trick

Thusfar we have established functoriality for negative-definite cobordisms and cobordisms which support no central connections. Our techniques do not apply to the case
b_{2}^{+}>0
without change; now we have an obstructed
central
connection on the cobordism, while we have explained only how to resolve (mildly) obstructed abelian connections.

It is likely that a modification of the previous techniques (a suspended flow category construction for central critical orbits instead) will allow us to extend functoriality to this setting, and this will be necessary for an investigation of the
\mathbb{F}_{2}
-theory. We leave this to future work, instead opting to use a trick which requires inverting
2
in the coefficient ring. This is founded on an idea appearing in
[
26
]
: by taking the connected sum with a bundle over
\overline{\mathbb{CP}}^{2}
with odd first Chern class, we can remove central connections and thus define induced maps associated to all cobordisms.

We
very
briefly recall the relevant definitions and results. See
[
8
, Section 5.2]
for a careful discussion of the setup.

Let
(W,c)
be a cobordism, and
\Sigma\subset W
a closed oriented surface embedded in
W
; arbitrarily choose a spin structure on this surface. The index bundle of the dual Dirac operator
\not{\partial}^{*}_{A,\Sigma}
(paired to the restriction of
A\in\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(W,c)
to
\Sigma
) induces an
SO(3)
-equivariant complex line bundle
\widetilde{L}_{\Sigma}
over the configuration space of framed connections on
W
. Given a reducible orbit
\mathcal{O}_{\Lambda}\subset\widetilde{\mathcal{B}}(W,c)
, the stabilizer
\Gamma_{\Lambda}
acts on the fiber of
\widetilde{L}_{\Sigma}
over
\Lambda
. If
\Lambda
is central, this action is necessarily trivial, as every homomorphism
SO(3)\to SO(2)
is trivial. If
\Lambda
is abelian, respecting a parallel splitting
E_{c}\cong L_{1}\oplus L_{2}
into complex line bundles with first Chern classes
\{x,y\}
, the weight of the
SO(2)\cong\Gamma_{\Lambda}\subset SO(3)
action is given by
|\langle x-y,[\Sigma]\rangle|
. The first Chern class of the associated complex line bundle over
\mathcal{O}_{\Lambda}
is
-2|\langle x-y,[\Sigma]\rangle|
.

In favorable situations (where there are few reducibles, or at least those reducibles that do exist appear with positive normal index) one may choose a generic
SO(3)
-equivariant section of
\widetilde{L}_{\Sigma}
which is transverse to zero on all moduli spaces
M^{+}_{z}(W,c;\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})
. In this situation, the cut-down moduli spaces again define a bimodule
\mathcal{I}(W,c,\Sigma)
of relative degree two smaller than
\mathcal{I}(W,c)
.

We will apply this construction in a special case.

Construction 6.13
.

Suppose
(W,c)
is a cobordism equipped with a base-path. Choose a point
p\in W
disjoint from this base-path and the surface
c
. Take the blowup at
p
, producing the connected-sum
(W_{\#},c_{\#})=(W\#\overline{\mathbb{CP}}^{2},c\cup\overline{\mathbb{CP}}^{1})
. If
z
is a component of connections on
W
, we write
z_{\#}
for the corresponding component on
W_{\#}
arising as connected sum with the component of connections on
(\overline{\mathbb{CP}}^{2},\overline{\mathbb{CP}}^{1})
of index
-1
(framed index
2
), so that
\widetilde{i}(z_{\#})=\widetilde{i}(z)+2
. We will be investigating the cut-down moduli spaces associated to the surface
\Sigma=\overline{\mathbb{CP}}^{1}
.

The reducibles on
(W_{\#},c_{\#})
are labeled by pairs
\{x^{\prime}=(x,i),y^{\prime}=(y,j)\}
, where
x,y\in H^{2}(W;\mathbb{Z})
and
i,j
are integers such that
x+y=PD(c)
and
i+j=1
. Because it is never possible that
x^{\prime}=y^{\prime}
(as
i+j
is odd), this cobordism supports no central reducibles; if
(W,c)
supports a central reducible, then the corresponding reducible of least index on
(W_{\#},c_{\#})
is pseudo-central. If
\Lambda
is an abelian reducible on
W
with cohomology classes
\{x,y\}
, the reducible
\Lambda_{i}
on
(W_{\#},c_{\#})
with cohomology classes
\{(x,i),(y,1-i)\}
has normal index
N(\Lambda_{i})=N(\Lambda)+2(2i-1)^{2}\geq N(\Lambda)+2
.

It follows that if
(W,c)
is pseudo-unobstructed, then
(W_{\#},c_{\#})
is unobstructed. When
b^{+}(W)>0
, it follows that for a generic choice of perturbation
(W_{\#},c_{\#})
supports no reducibles whatsoever. Choose such a perturbation, and then a section of
\widetilde{L}_{\Sigma}
so that the moduli spaces
M^{+}_{z_{\#}}(W_{\#},c_{\#};\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})
are always transverse to zero. We write
M^{+}_{z_{\#}}(W_{\#},c_{\#},\overline{\mathbb{CP}}^{1};\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})
for the moduli spaces cut-down by these sections. These define a bimodule
\mathcal{I}(W_{\#},c_{\#},\pi_{\#})
. This bimodule is transparently well-defined up to equivariant homotopy if
b^{+}(W)>1
.

Well-definedness in the case
b^{+}(W)=1
is somewhat more subtle, but entirely analogous to Proposition
6.1
; if
\Theta
is a central connection on
(W,c)
, then in 1-parameter families of perturbations on
(W_{\#},c_{\#})
, the abelian connection
\Theta_{0}
may appear as an isolated obstructed reducible of normal index
-2
on
(W_{\#},c_{\#})
. Over such a reducible there is no transverse section of
\widetilde{L}_{\Sigma}\to\mathcal{O}_{\Theta_{0}}
; these play the role that the obstructed reducibles of normal index
-4
played in Proposition
6.1
, though the sign on the zero locus is opposite, as the associated complex line bundle has first Chern class
-2
instead of
+2
.

The same modified moduli space construction from Proposition
6.1
implies that the resulting bimodule
\mathcal{I}(W_{\#},c_{\#},[\overline{\mathbb{CP}}^{1}],\pi_{\#})
is independent of
\pi_{\#}
up to equivariant homotopy.

We write
I^{\bullet}(W_{\#},c_{\#},[\overline{\mathbb{CP}}^{1}])
for the induced map on equivariant instanton homology.
∎

Before moving on to discussing functoriality, we should compare this cobordism map to the ones we’ve already defined.

Proposition 6.14
.

If
(W,c)
is a pseudo-unobstructed cobordism with
b^{+}(W)>0
, and
2
is invertible in
R
, then
-2I^{\bullet}(W,c)=I^{\bullet}(W_{\#},c_{\#},[\overline{\mathbb{CP}}^{1}])
.

Sketch of proof.

Choose a perturbation on
(W,c)
with no reducibes; because
(W,c)
is pseudo-unobstructed with
b^{+}(W)>0
, it supports no central connections. Write
(W_{L},c_{L})
for the connected sum
(W,c)\#(\overline{\mathbb{CP}}^{2},\overline{\mathbb{CP}}^{1})
above with a neck of length
L
. We may perform this connected sum in such a way that when
L=\infty
, the resulting space is conformally equivalent to
W\setminus p\sqcup\overline{\mathbb{CP}}^{2}\setminus\{q\}
, and therefore finite-energy on
(W_{\infty},c_{\infty})
instantons can be identified with pairs of instantons on
(W,c)
and instantons on
(\overline{\mathbb{CP}}^{2},\overline{\mathbb{CP}}^{1})
. The framed moduli space on
(\overline{\mathbb{CP}}^{2},\overline{\mathbb{CP}}^{1})
has dimension
2
modulo
8
, so the only relevant moduli space is the 2-dimensional one, which consists of a single abelian instanton.

It follows that

M^{+}_{z_{\#}}(W_{\infty},c_{\infty};\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})=M^{+}_{z}(W,c;\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})\times S^{2}.

Furthermore, the complex line bundle
\widetilde{L}_{\Sigma}
is determined entirely by the
\overline{\mathbb{CP}}^{2}
factor, so is pulled back from (a negatively oriented)
TS^{2}
over the latter factor.
If we ignore equivariance
, we could choose our section to be a section
S^{2}\to TS^{2}
with exactly two negatively-oriented zeroes; this would identify
M^{+}_{z_{\#}}(W_{\infty},c_{\infty},\overline{\mathbb{CP}}^{1};\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})
with
-2
copies of
M^{+}_{z}(W,c;\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})
. This observation, first appearing (with different orientation conventions) as
[
16
, Theorem 6.9]
and also appearing as
[
26
, Proposition 3.2]
, says that we have not produced anything new.

Unfortunately, this does not lead to an identification of the bimodules
-2\mathcal{I}(W,c)
and
\mathcal{I}(W_{\#},c_{\#},[\overline{\mathbb{CP}}^{1}])
. The issue is that it is not at all obvious that one may choose an equivariant section of
\widetilde{L}_{\Sigma}
whose zero set is oriented diffeomorphic to
-2M^{+}_{z}(W,c;\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})
and for which this diffeomorphism preserves (or at least nearly preserves) the endpoint maps. This is not an issue for the authors above; instead of ‘cutting down’ geometrically by taking a zero set of this complex line bundle, these authors take the cap product against a degree-2 cohomology class, where one does not have these equivariance concerns.

To solve this issue, we can pass to the finite-dimensional ‘Donaldson model’ of
[
25
, Section 7.2]
, which is defined under the hypothesis that
2\in R
is invertible. This finite-dimensional model is obtained by first replacing every irreducible orbit
\alpha
in
\widetilde{C}(Y,w)
with a copy
\tilde{\alpha}
of
S^{3}
, and replacing the moduli spaces running between them with appropriate double or four-fold covers
\tilde{M}
, weighted by
1/2
or
1/4
as appropriate. The chain maps
\widetilde{C}(W,c)
are defined by a matrix, whose entries count intersections of the endpoint maps
\tilde{M}^{+}_{z}(W,c;\tilde{\alpha},\tilde{\alpha}^{\prime})\to\tilde{\alpha}\times\tilde{\alpha}^{\prime}
with one of the four submanifolds
p_{\alpha}\times q_{\alpha^{\prime}}
or
\tilde{\alpha}\times q_{\alpha^{\prime}}
or
p_{\alpha}\times\tilde{\alpha}^{\prime}
or
\tilde{\alpha}\times\tilde{\alpha}^{\prime}
.

In this model, where the differential is defined by point-counts, the point-counts of the intersections of
\tilde{M}^{+}_{z_{\#}}(W_{\infty},c_{\infty},\overline{\mathbb{CP}}^{1};\tilde{\alpha},\tilde{\alpha}^{\prime})
with these submanifolds are precisely equal to
-2
times the corresponding point counts of
\tilde{M}^{+}_{z}(W,c;\tilde{\alpha},\tilde{\alpha}^{\prime})
. To be precise, if
\tilde{M}^{+}_{z}(W,c;\tilde{\alpha},\tilde{\alpha}^{\prime})\to\tilde{\alpha}\times\tilde{\alpha}^{\prime}
has intersection number
k
with
S\subset\tilde{\alpha}\times\tilde{\alpha}^{\prime}
, then
\tilde{M}^{+}_{z_{\#}}(W_{\#},c_{\#};\tilde{\alpha},\tilde{\alpha}^{\prime})\cap S
is identified with
k
copies of
S^{2}
(counting with sign). Taking the zero set of the transverse section of
\widetilde{L}_{\Sigma}
gives rise to
-2k
points.

It follows that
-2I^{\bullet}(W,c)=I^{\bullet}(W_{\#},c_{\#},[\overline{\mathbb{CP}}^{1}])
.
∎

Remark 6.15
.

The statement above extends to the case
b^{+}(W)=0
, but one needs to explain more clearly how to define the right-hand-side in the case that
b^{+}=0
(using a setup analogous to that of Section
6.1
); we do not see substantial benefit to doing so.

Further, this statement should hold with respect to all coefficient rings, not simply those in which
2
is invertible. (In particular, over
R=\mathbb{F}_{2}
, the induced map
I^{\bullet}(W_{\#},c_{\#},[\overline{\mathbb{CP}}^{1}])
. One proof would define a Donaldson model without passing to a double cover of
SO(3)
, using a more intricate CW decomposition of
SO(3)
. This is not difficult, but we will not carry it out here.

Instead of passing to the Donaldson model, another way to resolve the issue is to quotient by a larger equivalence relation in the definition of the geometric chain complex, so that the zero set of an equivariant transverse section over
M\times S^{2}
is identified with
2
copies of
M
.
∎

This in hand, we can prove Theorem
2
(ii) from the guide:

Theorem 6.16
.

When
R
is a PID with
1/2\in R
, then the functors
I^{\bullet}(Y,w;R)
extend to the category
\mathsf{Cob}_{3+1}^{w}
of weakly admissible pairs, allowing for indefinite cobordisms.

Proof.

Let
(W,c):(Y,w,\pi)\to(Y,w,\pi^{\prime})
be a cobordism (with the necessary additional data). We define

I^{\bullet}(W,c)=\frac{1}{2}I^{\bullet}_{\#}(W,c).

We have already verified in Proposition
6.3
that this agrees with the definition of
I^{\bullet}(W,c)
from Theorem
6.1
. That this map is functorial follows from the following chain of equalities. Write
W^{12}=W^{1}\cup_{Y}W^{2}
, where
W^{1}:Y_{1}\to Y
and
W^{2}:Y\to Y_{2}
are cobordisms (with suitable additional data, which is suppressed from notation for convenience). Then

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}I^{\bullet}(W^{12}_{\#},\mathbf{E}^{12}_{\#},\overline{\mathbb{CP}}^{1};R)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}I^{\bullet}(W^{12}_{\#},(\mathbf{E}^{12}_{\#})_{\#},\overline{\mathbb{CP}}_{1}^{1}\sqcup\overline{\mathbb{CP}}_{2}^{1};R)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}I^{\bullet}(W^{1}_{\#},\mathbf{E}^{1}_{\#},\overline{\mathbb{CP}}^{1}_{1})\circ I^{\bullet}(W^{2}_{\#},\mathbf{E}^{2}_{\#},\overline{\mathbb{CP}}^{1}_{2})

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{2}I^{\bullet}(W^{1}_{\#},\mathbf{E}^{1}_{\#},\overline{\mathbb{CP}}^{1}_{1})\right)\circ\left(\frac{1}{2}I^{\bullet}(W^{2}_{\#},\mathbf{E}^{2}_{\#},\overline{\mathbb{CP}}^{1}_{2})\right).

Here the subscripts on
\overline{\mathbb{CP}}^{1}
indicate which of the two blowups they correspond to. The second-to-last equality follows from the fact that instanton homology is functorial for unobstructed cobordisms by the usual neck-stretching argument.
∎

7
Irreducible Floer homology and the Casson-Walker invariant

In Floer’s original definition of the instanton homology of integer homology spheres, the invariant
I_{*}(Y)
was obtained by ignoring the central connection and constructing a chain complex generated by the irreducible connections.

One may define such a chain complex for rational homology spheres, but it is no longer independent of the choice of perturbation. Instead, we will see it depends on a choice of signature data function
\sigma
, and use the results of Section
5.5
to see how it changes as we pass between adjacent signature data.

7.1
Floer’s complex from algebra

Given an
SO(3)
-equivariant flow category
\mathcal{C}
as in Section
4.2
, we can extract a chain complex called the
irreducible flow complex
. By default, it is relatively graded; if
\mathcal{C}
is given an absolute grading, then the irreducible flow complex inherits an absolute grading as well.

We say an object
\alpha
of
\mathcal{C}
is
irreducible
if it is diffeomorphic to
SO(3)
. We define a complex

C_{*}^{\text{irr}}(\mathcal{C})=\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\mathsf{Ob}(\mathcal{C})\\
\alpha\text{ irreducible}\end{subarray}}\mathbb{Z}[\widetilde{i}(\alpha)]\times_{\mathbb{Z}/2}\Lambda(\alpha),

where as in Section
4.2
the two-element set
\Lambda(\alpha)
corresponds to orientation data, while
\widetilde{i}(\alpha)
is the absolute grading if one exists, but otherwise is meant to indicate that the relative grading between the
\alpha
and
\beta
summands is
\widetilde{i}(\alpha,\beta)
.

When
\widetilde{i}(\alpha,\beta)=1
, the moduli space
\mathcal{C}(\alpha,\beta)
has dimension
3=\dim\alpha
, and the map
\mathcal{C}(\alpha,\beta)\to\alpha
is an
SO(3)
-equivariant local diffeomorphism. The fiber above any point in
\alpha
is canonically identified with any other, and we write
\mathcal{C}^{\text{irr}}(\alpha,\beta)
for this fiber. When one chooses an element of
\Lambda(\alpha)
and an element of
\Lambda(\beta)
, this fiber is given an orientation, which reverses upon changing either of those choices. To suppress discussion of these orientation elements, let us suppose we have prechosen such elements for each irreducible orbit.

We may thus define a differential on
C_{*}^{\text{irr}}(\mathcal{C})
via

d^{\text{irr}}(\alpha)=\sum_{\beta}\#\mathcal{C}^{\text{irr}}(\alpha,\beta)\beta.

Instead of verifying that this squares to zero (which is straightforward), it is useful to identify the construction above with a purely algebraic avatar. Write
u=[SO(3)]\in C_{3}SO(3)
for the representative of the fundamental class. Consider the image of
u:CM_{*}(\mathcal{C})\to CM_{*+3}(\mathcal{C})
, which we write as
uCM_{*}(\mathcal{C})
. This is a chain complex with the differential
\tilde{d}
, because the Leibniz rule gives is
\tilde{d}(ux)=(du)x-u\tilde{d}(x)=u(-\tilde{d}(x))
as
d(u)=0
. Next, we will show that this complex is closely related to
C_{*}^{\text{irr}}(\mathcal{C})
.

Proposition 7.1
.

The complex
\big(C_{*}^{\textup{irr}}(\mathcal{C}),d^{\textup{irr}}\big)
is isomorphic to the complex
\big(uCM_{*}(\mathcal{C})[-3],-\tilde{d}\;\big)
.

Proof.

First, let us recall some structural properties of the complex
CM_{*}(\mathcal{C})
, and in particular of geometric chains. This complex has underlying relatively graded abelian group given by

CM_{*}(\mathcal{C})=\bigoplus_{\alpha\in\mathsf{Ob}(\mathcal{C})}C_{*}(\alpha)[\widetilde{i}(\alpha)]\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}/2}\mathbb{Z}[\Lambda(\alpha)].

Because we use the truncated geometric chain complex defined in Appendix
A.5
, and this complex has
C_{*}(\alpha)
supported in degrees
[0,\dim\alpha]
, we see that if
\alpha
is diffeomorphic to a point or
S^{2}
, we must have
uC_{*}(\alpha)=0
, while if
\alpha\cong SO(3)
, we have a noncanonical isomorphism
uC_{*}(\alpha)\cong\mathbb{Z}[3],
with the fundamental class with respect to some orientation
[\alpha]
being a cyclic generator.

By choosing the class
u
at the start we fixed an orientation on
SO(3)
; orient each irreducible
\alpha
so that the map
SO(3)\to\alpha
given by acting on a point is an orientation-preserving diffeomorphism. This makes the above isomorphism canonical, taking the cyclic generator to be the fundamental class with respect to the orientation described above; equivalently, by sending
u\cdot\text{pt}
to
1\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Thus, at the level of graded abelian groups, we have an identification

C_{*}^{\text{irr}}(\mathcal{C})=uCM_{*}(\mathcal{C})[-3]

sending
\alpha
to
[\alpha]
We will see this in fact their differentials coincide, so long as one interprets the differential on the right-hand-side as
-\tilde{d}
. To see this, observe that

\tilde{d}[\alpha]=\sum_{\beta}(-1)^{\dim\alpha}[\alpha]\times_{\alpha}\mathcal{C}(\alpha,\beta)=\sum_{\beta\in\mathsf{Ob}^{\textup{irr}}(\mathcal{C})}(-1)^{\dim\alpha}\mathcal{C}(\alpha,\beta);

if
\widetilde{i}(\alpha,\beta)>1
, the space
\mathcal{C}(\alpha,\beta)
has dimension at least 4, so is zero in
C_{*}(\beta)
. Using also that
\dim\alpha=3
, we may thus write this sum as

\tilde{d}[\alpha]=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta\in\mathsf{Ob}^{\textup{irr}}(\mathcal{C})\\
\widetilde{i}(\alpha,\beta)=1\end{subarray}}-\mathcal{C}(\alpha,\beta).

Now each
\mathcal{C}(\alpha,\beta)
here is three-dimensional, so each component projects diffeomorphically onto
\beta
; these components are in one-to-one bijection with
\mathcal{C}^{\text{irr}}(\alpha,\beta)
, the points in a fiber above some
x\in\alpha
.

To compare orientations, recall that
\mathcal{C}^{\text{irr}}(\alpha,\beta)
is oriented as a fiber over
x\in\alpha
, so that

\mathcal{C}(\alpha,\beta)\cong SO(3)\times\mathcal{C}^{\text{irr}}(\alpha,\beta)

is an oriented diffeomorphism, and the endpoint map
SO(3)\to\beta
is equivariant, hence an oriented diffeomorphism by our initial choice of orientations. It follows that

\tilde{d}[\alpha]=-\sum_{\beta}\#\mathcal{C}^{\text{irr}}(\alpha,\beta)[\beta],

as desired.
∎

Now observe that if
\tilde{C}
is a dg
C_{*}SO(3)
-module, then
u\tilde{C}
is again a chain complex; if
f:\tilde{C}\to\tilde{C}^{\prime}
is a
C_{*}SO(3)
-equivariant map, then because
f(ux)=uf(x)
we see that
f
restricts to a chain map
u\tilde{C}\to u\tilde{C}^{\prime}
. Finally, if
H:\tilde{C}\to\tilde{C}^{\prime}[1]
is a
C_{*}SO(3)
-equivariant chain homotopy, this inducues a chain homotopy
u\tilde{C}\to u\tilde{C}^{\prime}[1]
.

Corollary 7.2
.

Given a weakly admissible pair with choice of signature data
(Y,w,\sigma)
, there is a well-defined ‘irreducible instanton homology group’
I_{*}(Y,w,\sigma)
. If
(W,c):(Y,w,\sigma)\to(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime})
is nearly good, then there is a well-defined induced map

I(W,c):I_{*}(Y,w,\sigma)\to I_{*}(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}),

which composes functorially.

Proof.

As a corollary of Theorem
4.3
, whenever
\pi_{0}
and
\pi_{1}
are two regular perturbations on
(Y,w)
with the same signature data, there is a
C_{*}SO(3)
-equivariant continuation map
\tilde{C}(Y,w,\pi_{0})\to\tilde{C}(Y,w,\pi_{1})
which is an
SO(3)
-equivariant homotopy equivalence. Passing to the homology of
u\tilde{C}[-3]
, one may construct a canonically defined
I_{*}(Y,w,\sigma)
which depends only on
\sigma
by the mechanism of Definition
5.5
.

When
(W,c):(Y,w,\pi)\to(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime},\pi^{\prime})
is a nearly good cobordism whose only obstructed reducibles are bad reducibles, we showed in Section
6.1
that there is a corresponding
SO(3)
-equivariant chain map
\tilde{C}(Y,w,\pi)\to\tilde{C}(Y^{\prime},w^{\prime},\pi^{\prime})
which is well-defined up to equivariant chain homotopy and which composes functorially up to equivariant chain homotopy. Applying
u\tilde{C}[-3]
and passing to homology, we get the claimed functoriality results.
∎

While the groups
I_{*}(Y,w,\sigma)
do depend on the signature data
\sigma
, we can take advantage of the proof of Theorem
5.5
. If
\sigma_{0}<\sigma_{1}
are adjacent signature data on
(Y,w)
which differ on
\rho\in\mathfrak{A}(Y,w)
, we proved that theorem by establishing the existence of an
SO(3)
-equivariant homotopy equivalence

CM_{*}\mathcal{S}_{\rho}\mathcal{I}(Y,w,\sigma_{0})\simeq CM_{*}\mathcal{I}(Y,w,\sigma_{1}).

Writing
S_{\rho}\tilde{C}(Y,w,\sigma_{0})
for the former complex and
\tilde{C}(Y,w,\sigma_{1})
for the latter, we thus see that

I_{*}(Y,w,\sigma_{1})\cong u\widetilde{C}(Y,w,\sigma_{1})[-3]\cong uS_{\rho}\tilde{C}(Y,w,\sigma_{0})[-3].

To understand how
I_{*}
changes in passing from
\sigma_{0}
to
\sigma_{1}
, we merely need to understand the latter complex.

Proposition 7.3
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be an
SO(3)
-equivariant flow category of the type considered above (for instance, any framed instanton flow category). The Morse complex
C^{\textup{irr}}_{*}(\mathcal{S}_{\rho}\mathcal{C})
is obtained as an algebraic mapping cone. Precisely, there is an isomorphism of (relatively graded) abelian groups

uCM_{*}(\mathcal{S}_{\rho}\mathcal{C})[-3]=uCM_{*}(\mathcal{C})[-3]\oplus\mathbb{Z}[\widetilde{i}_{\mathcal{C}}(\rho)-1]

so that

d^{\textup{irr}}_{\rho}=\begin{pmatrix}d^{\textup{irr}}&0\\
-V_{2}&0\end{pmatrix},

where
\langle V_{2}(\rho),\beta\rangle
is the signed count of isolated irreducible components of
\mathcal{C}(\rho,\beta)
.

Proof.

We have

uCM_{*}(\mathcal{S}_{\rho}\mathcal{C})=\left(\bigoplus_{\alpha\in\mathsf{Ob}^{\textup{irr}}(\mathcal{C})}\mathbb{Z}\langle\alpha\rangle[\widetilde{i}(\alpha)]\right)\oplus\mathbb{Z}\langle S_{\rho}\rangle[\widetilde{i}(\rho)-1];

we obtain the additional generator from
uC_{*}(S_{\rho})[-3]
, as
\widetilde{i}_{\mathcal{S}}(S_{\rho})=\widetilde{i}_{\mathcal{C}}(\rho)-1
. This gives the stated isomorphism at the level of relatively graded abelian groups.

Now it remains to compute the differential, which we may write as

d^{\text{irr}}=\begin{pmatrix}d_{CC}&d_{C\rho}\\
d_{\rho C}&d_{\rho\rho}\end{pmatrix}.

Each component is given by computing
\#\mathcal{S}^{\text{irr}}(\alpha,\beta)
where
\alpha,\beta
are irreducible objects of
\mathcal{S}_{\rho}\mathcal{C}
; equivalently, this is the signed count of the number of components. To enumerate these, recall the definition of the moduli spaces
\mathcal{S}(\alpha,\beta)
from Construction
5.3
. When neither
\alpha
nor
\beta
is
S_{\rho}
, we have an equality
\mathcal{S}(\alpha,\beta)=\mathcal{C}(\alpha,\beta)
, so that
d_{CC}=d^{\text{irr}}
is the irreducible differential coming from
\mathcal{C}
itself.

Because
\mathcal{S}(\alpha,S_{\rho})=\mathcal{C}(\alpha,\rho)\times_{\rho}S_{\rho}
, and
\mathcal{C}(\alpha,\rho)
consists of irreducible trajectories, this space has dimension at least 4 and hence does not contribute to the differential; so
d_{C\rho}=0
. Even more easily,
\mathcal{S}(S_{\rho},S_{\rho})=\varnothing
by definition, so that
d_{\rho\rho}=0
.

The only remaining interesting component is
\mathcal{S}(S_{\rho},\beta)=-B(\rho,\beta)
, where
B(\rho,\beta)
is the real blowup of the moduli space
\mathcal{C}(\rho,\beta)
along a certain
SO(3)
-equivariant section. In the case we’re interested in,
\mathcal{C}(\rho,\beta)
is 3-dimensional — a disjoint union of free orbits of
SO(3)
— this section may be assumed nonzero everywhere (define it to be a nonzero value at a point in each component, and extend the definition everywhere by equivariance). The components of
-B(\rho,\beta)
are thus oriented diffeomorphic to the components of
-\mathcal{C}(\rho,\beta)
, and we defined
V_{2}(\rho)=\sum\#\mathcal{C}^{\text{irr}}(\rho,\beta).
This shows that
d_{\rho C}=V_{2}
, as desired.
∎

Because
I_{*}(Y,w,\sigma_{1})
is isomorphic to the homology of the irreducible flow complex attached to
\mathcal{S}_{\rho}\mathcal{I}(Y,w,\sigma_{1})
, the preceding mapping cone formula immediately implies the following.

Corollary 7.4
.

Suppose
\sigma_{0}<\sigma_{1}
are adjancent signature data on
(Y,w)
, differing at
\rho
. Write the continuation map as
\varphi:I_{*}(Y,w,\sigma_{0})\to I_{*}(Y,w,\sigma_{1})
, and
V_{2}:\mathbb{Z}[\widetilde{i}(\rho)]\to I_{*}(Y,w,\sigma_{0})
for the map sending
1\mapsto[\sum_{\alpha}\#M(\rho,\alpha)]
. Then there is an exact triangle

\cdots\to\mathbb{Z}[\widetilde{i}(\rho)]\to I_{*}(Y,w,\sigma_{0})\to I_{*}(Y,w,\sigma_{1})\to\cdots

Recall from Definition
3.2
that
I_{*}(Y,w,\sigma)
always carries a canonical
\mathbb{Z}/2
grading. When
\rho
is reducible, the absolute
\mathbb{Z}/2
-grading
\widetilde{i}(\rho)
is always even; this can be seen by an application of the index formula to a 4-manifold with reducible connection bounding
(Y,w,\rho)
.

It follows that
I_{*}(Y,w,\sigma)
has a well-defined Euler characteristic, and as the signature data increases, the Euler characteristic decreases:

\chi\big(I_{*}(Y,\sigma_{0})\big)=\chi\big(I_{*}(Y,\sigma_{1})\big)+1.

In the final section, we will use this to define a Casson–Walker type invariant. Through the corollary below, it also shows that our technical passage to the suspended flow complex
S_{\rho}\widetilde{C}_{*}(Y,w,\sigma)
was in some sense necessary; the continuation map is merely a quasi-isomorphism, and we couldn’t have constructed an equivariant homotopy inverse.

Corollary 7.5
.

If
\sigma_{0}\leq\sigma_{1}
are signature data on
(Y,w)
, the continuation map

\varphi_{\sigma_{0}\to\sigma_{1}}:\widetilde{C}(Y,w,\sigma_{0})\to\widetilde{C}(Y,w,\sigma_{1})

is an
SO(3)
-equivariant homotopy equivalence if and only if
\sigma_{0}=\sigma_{1}
, even though it is always an equivariant quasi-isomorphism.

Proof.

When
\sigma_{0}=\sigma_{1}
the cylinder is unobstructed running in either direction, and the corresponding cobordism maps give equivariant chain homotopy inverses.

If
C
is a dg-module over
C_{*}SO(3)
, and
f:C\to C^{\prime}
is an
SO(3)
-homotopy equivalence, then the corresponding map
f_{*}:uC\to uC^{\prime}
is a homotopy equivalence. So by Proposition
7.1
, it follows that if
\varphi_{\sigma_{0}\to\sigma_{1}}
is an equivariant homotopy equivalence, then
\varphi_{*}:I_{*}(Y,w,\sigma_{0})\to I_{*}(Y,w,\sigma_{1})
is an isomorphism.

Now by Corollary
7.1
, if
\sigma_{0}\leq\sigma_{1}
, then

\chi(I_{*}(Y,\sigma_{0}))-\chi(I_{*}(Y,\sigma_{1}))=\frac{1}{4}\sum_{\alpha\in\mathfrak{A}(Y,w)}\sigma_{1}(\alpha)-\sigma_{0}(\alpha)\geq 0

with equality if and only if
\sigma_{0}=\sigma_{1}
. So if the continuation map
\varphi
is an equivariant homotopy equivalence, we have
\sigma_{0}=\sigma_{1}
.
∎

7.2
An instanton Casson–Walker invariant

If
\alpha\in\mathfrak{A}(Y,w)
, there is a corresponding representation
\text{ad}_{\alpha}:\pi_{1}(Y)\to SO(2)
. Recall from Definition
3.5
that the perturbed rho invariant of
\alpha
with respect to a regular perturbation
\pi
is the rational number

\rho_{\pi}(\alpha)=\sigma_{\pi}(\alpha)+\rho(\text{ad}_{\alpha}),

with
\rho
being the relative
\eta
invariant of Atiyah–Patodi–Singer, and
\sigma_{\pi}
is the signature data function of Definition
3.1
.

Notice two things. First, this function depends only on the signature data function
\sigma_{\pi}
, and does not otherwise depend on the perturbation. Second, if
\sigma_{0}<\sigma_{1}
are adjacent signature data differing precisely at
\alpha
, we have
\rho_{\sigma_{1}}(\alpha)=\rho_{\sigma_{0}}(\alpha)+4
. We can thus use this as a correction term to counteract the change in Euler characteristic of
I_{*}
as we move between chambers and obtain a well-defined rational number.

Definition 7.6
.

Let
(Y,w)
be a weakly admissible pair. We say that the
instanton Casson–Walker invariant
is the number

\lambda_{I}(Y,w)=\frac{1}{|\textup{Tors }H_{1}Y|}\left(\chi\big(I_{*}(Y,w,\pi)\big)+\frac{1}{4}\sum_{\alpha\in\mathfrak{A}(Y)}\rho_{\pi}(\alpha)\right),

for any choice of regular perturbation
\pi
.
∎

This is independent of
\pi
because the change in the displayed quantities cancel out as we move between adjacent signature data. We expect this to be an instanton-theoretic definition of the Casson–Walker invariant.

Conjecture 7.7
.

For any rational homology sphere
Y
, the instanton and Casson–Walker invariants coincide; we have
\lambda_{I}(Y,w)=\lambda_{CW}(Y)
for all
w
.

In particular, this would mean that
\lambda_{I}(Y,w)
is independent of
w
.

Remark 7.8
.

The Casson invariant and its relatives have a long history. Soon after Casson defined his invariant of integer homology spheres, Taubes
[
35
]
gave a definition in terms of signed counts of flat connections modulo gauge; the signs amount to saying that the Casson invariant computes the Euler characteristic of Floer’s instanton homology. Around the same time, Walker published his generalization to rational homology spheres
[
36
]
; a signed count of points no longer gives an invariant, and instead one must add canonical rational-valued correction terms (as we do above) to get a well-defined invariant.

The Seiberg–Witten analogue of the Casson–Walker invariant is defined in
[
3
]
. There, even in the case of integer homology spheres (indeed, even in the case of
S^{3}
) one needs to introduce correction terms to get a well-defined invariant; these correction terms only fail to be integers for rational homology spheres. The resulting quantity is indeed equal to the Casson–Walker invariant, as proved in
[
21
]
for integer homology spheres and
[
24
]
for rational homology spheres.

Finally, a version of the Casson–Walker invariant in terms of a precursor to Heegaard Floer homology was provided in
[
29
]
; here again one uses a topological correction term, and their Casson–Walker invariant is given as a sum over spin
c
structures.
∎

As yet we have neither a surgery exact triangle or a connected sum theorem, so we cannot say much presently outside of some specific examples; but we can at least verify it in those few examples. To set convention, the lens space
L(p,q)
is
-p/q
surgery on the unknot.

Theorem 7.9
.

Conjecture
7.2
is true when
Y
is an integer homology sphere or a lens space
L(p,q)
.

Proof.

When
Y
is an integer homology sphere, the sum over abelian flat connections is tautologically zero and the choice of signature data is vacuous, so we simply have
\lambda_{I}(Y)=\chi I_{*}(Y)
. The main result of
[
35
]
is that we have an equality
\chi I_{*}(Y)=2\lambda_{C}(Y)
with half of the Casson invariant of
Y
. Walker’s normalization conventions have
\lambda_{CW}(Y)=2\lambda_{C}(Y)
, giving us the desired result.

For lens spaces, the trivial perturbation is a regular perturbation, so we may take
\sigma=0
. Then the invariant
\lambda_{I}(L(p,q),w)
is given as

\frac{1}{4p}\sum_{\alpha\in\mathfrak{A}(L(p,q),w)}\rho(\alpha).

Write
H^{2}\big(L(p,q)\big)\cong\mathbb{Z}/p
. In the case
w=\varnothing
, the abelian reducibles can be identified with those integers
\ell
with
0<\ell<p/2
, and have adjoint representation given by
\text{ad}_{\ell}(i)=e^{2\pi i(2\ell)/p}
. In the case
p
is even and
[w]
is nonzero in
\mathbb{Z}/2
-homology, the abelian reducibles can be identified with the odd integers
0<2\ell+1<p
, and
\text{ad}_{\ell}(i)=e^{2\pi i(2\ell+1)/p}
.

Let us handle the first case. Using the formula of
[
14
, Proposition 2.18]
, we have

\rho(\text{ad}_{\ell})=\frac{4}{p}\sum_{k=1}^{p-1}\cot\left(\frac{\pi k}{p}\right)\cot\left(\frac{\pi kq}{p}\right)\sin^{2}\left(\frac{2\pi k\ell}{p}\right).

Summing over
0<\ell<p/2
and multiplying by
1/4p
, we see that
\lambda_{I}(L(p,q),\varnothing)
is

\frac{1}{p^{2}}\sum_{0<\ell<p/2}\sum_{k=1}^{p-1}\cot\left(\frac{\pi k}{p}\right)\cot\left(\frac{\pi kq}{p}\right)\sin^{2}\left(\frac{2\pi k\ell}{p}\right).

Let us compute the identical sum

\frac{1}{2p^{2}}\sum_{\ell=0}^{p-1}\sum_{k=1}^{p-1}\cot\left(\frac{\pi k}{p}\right)\cot\left(\frac{\pi kq}{p}\right)\sin^{2}\left(\frac{2\pi k\ell}{p}\right);

this coincides with the sum above because the
\sin^{2}
term is symmetric with respect to swapping
\ell
and
p-\ell
, and when
p
is even the term corresponding to
\ell=p/2
automatically vanishes.

When
p
is even, notice that every term corresponding to
k=p/2
automatically vanishes because
\cot(\pi/2)=0
. Further, observe that
\sum_{\ell=0}^{p-1}\sin^{2}\left(\frac{2\pi k\ell}{p}\right)=p/2
whenever
0<k<p
and
k\neq p/2
: this follows by the double-angle formula
\sin^{2}(x)=\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2}\cos(2x)
and the fact that
\sum_{\ell=0}^{p-1}\cos(4\pi k\ell/p)=0
, because this latter expression is the real part of the sum
0=1+\zeta+\cdots+\zeta^{p-1}
. Here
\zeta=e^{4\pi ik/p}\neq 1
because
0<k<p
and
k\neq p/2
.

Combining these two facts, we may write

\lambda_{I}(L(p,q),\varnothing)=\frac{1}{4p}\sum_{k=1}^{p-1}\cot\left(\frac{\pi k}{p}\right)\cot\left(\frac{\pi kq}{p}\right),

which is a Dedekind sum traditionally denoted
s(q;p)
.

Finally, we have
\lambda_{CW}(L(p,q))=s(q;p)
, by
[
36
, Proposition 6.3]
; in comparing to the reference, note that Walker’s
L_{p/q}
is what we call
L(-p,q)
, and hence
\lambda_{CW}(L(p,q))
is written in Walker’s book as
\lambda(-L_{p/q})=-\lambda(L_{p/q})
, as desired.

The computation for the case
[w]\neq 0\in H_{1}(Y;\mathbb{F}_{2})
is similar, with the relevant
\rho
invariant being

\rho(\text{ad}_{\ell})=\frac{4}{p}\sum_{k=1}^{p-1}\cot\left(\frac{\pi k}{p}\right)\cot\left(\frac{\pi kq}{p}\right)\sin^{2}\left(\frac{\pi k\ell}{p}\right),

for odd
0<\ell<p
, so that

\lambda_{I}(L(p,q),w)=\frac{1}{p^{2}}\sum_{j=0}^{p/2-1}\sum_{k=1}^{p-1}\cot\left(\frac{\pi k}{p}\right)\cot\left(\frac{\pi kq}{p}\right)\sin^{2}\left(\frac{\pi k(2j+1)}{p}\right).

This simplifies to the same quantity
s(q;p)
. The argument is nearly identical: eliminate the terms with
k=p/2
, and sum over
j
to get rid of the final factor; here one uses that when
\zeta
is a
p
’th root of unity for
p
even we have
\zeta+\zeta^{3}+\cdots+\zeta^{2p-1}=0
.
∎

Remark 7.10
.

Using the computations in
[
28
]
, together with further computations of the rho invariants for abelian connections on spherical manifolds, one can also verify the conjecture for any 3-manifold of the form
SU(2)/\Gamma
(with
w=\varnothing
), where
\Gamma
is a finite subgroup of
SU(2)
. We will not carry out this computation in detail here.
∎

Appendix A
Stratified-smooth spaces

In this appendix, we construct the model for singular chains
C_{*}(X)
we use in the main body of the text, where
X
is a smooth manifold, which we will call
geometric chains
; this complex should be functorial for smooth maps and have homology groups canonically isomorphic to singular homology. Let us briefly outline the desired construction.

This geometric chain complex should be free on a certain generating set, the
nondegenerate, nontrivial probes
, where these probes are something analogous to
(P,\phi)
a compact oriented smooth manifold with corners
P
with a smooth map
\phi
to
X
; the boundary map should be analogous to the passage to
\partial P
in the case of smooth manifolds with corners.

For our purposes, these probes must be more general than smooth manifolds with corners: they must include the moduli spaces of instantons
\breve{M}^{+}_{\zeta}(\alpha,\beta)
discussed in Section
3.4
; in Definition
3.4
, we named the kind of object we obtain a
stratified-smooth manifold
. When proving the excision property, one needs to be able to take transverse intersections, which leads to the more general notion of
stratified-smooth spaces
below, roughly the kind of space obtained by taking transverse intersections of stratified-smooth manifolds.

The first two sections of this appendix develop the bare minimum of foundations and orientation theory of stratified-smooth manifolds needed to define geometric chains. In Section 5 of the main text, we also need a certain blowup construction on these stratified-smooth spaces, which we set up in Appendix
A.3
, and carry out some necessary computations. In Appendix
A.4
we construct the geometric chain complex and verify an appropriate version of the Eilenberg-Steenrod axioms, and therefore verify that the resulting homology groups are canonically isomorphic to singular homology. In the final section, Appendix
A.5
, we perform a truncation procedure on the geometric chain complex; this is used in Section
4
to ensure we only need to construct moduli spaces within a certain dimension range.

A.1
Foundations of stratified-smooth spaces

Smooth manifolds with corners should be special cases of stratified-smooth spaces, so first we must decide on a preferred definition. There are a number of inequivalent definitions (see
[
15
]
for a survey). The more general definitions have more complicated boundary operators, which cannot be defined solely in terms of the codimension-1 strata. This is harder to work with effectively when defining and computing the boundary of a stratified-smooth space; we prefer to be more restrictive.

With eyes towards the moduli spaces in the main text, we instead use a version of manifolds with corners and stratified-smooth spaces where one is stratified by a set of faces, which need not be connected, whose closures are all again manifolds with corners (resp. stratified-smooth spaces). To do so, we record the stratification with an appropriate kind of partially ordere set.

Definition A.1
.

A graded poset is a finite partially ordered set
(\Delta,<)
equipped with a ‘dimension’ function
d:\Delta\to\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, which is order-preserving in the sense that
s<t
implies
d(s)<d(t)
.

We say that the largest integer in the image of
d
is the dimension of
\Delta
.
∎

The simplest example of a graded poset, and one which will be very useful for us, is the cube poset
\{0,1\}^{n}
for some natural number
n
. Writing elements as functions
s:\{1,\cdots,n\}\to\{0,1\}
, the ordering has
s\leq t
if
s(i)\leq t(i)
for all
i
, and the dimension function is
d(s)=\sum s(i)
.

For the objects of interest, the graded poset
\Delta
will have the property that the sub-poset

\Delta_{\geq s}=\{t\in\Delta\mid t\geq s\}

is always isomorphic to a cube poset
\{0,1\}^{\dim X-d(s)}
, with dimension function shifted up by
d(s)
.

Partially ordered sets are relevant because the natural relation between the different strata of a stratified space is a partial ordering. Below, we use a definition of stratified space which explicitly records the relevant partially ordered set.

Definition A.2
.

A
stratified space

(X,\Delta)
is a pair of a poset
\Delta
and a topological space
X
equipped with, for each
s\in\Delta
, a locally closed subset
X_{s}\subset X
; these subsets are required to satisfy the property
\overline{X_{s}}=\bigcup_{t\leq s}X_{t}.

An isomorphism
(X,\Delta_{X})\cong(Y,\Delta_{Y})
of stratified spaces is a pair
(f,\bar{f})
, where
\bar{f}:\Delta_{X}\to\Delta_{Y}
is a poset isomorphism and
f
is a homeomorphism with
f(X_{s})=Y_{\bar{f}(s)}
.
∎

Next we record the naive version of a smooth version of stratified spaces, where one merely equips each stratum with a smooth structure.

Definition A.3
.

A
naive stratified-smooth space

(X,\Delta)
of dimension
n
is a pair of a graded poset
\Delta
and a second-countable Hausdorff space
X
stratified by
\Delta
, where each stratum
X_{s}
is given the structure of a smooth manifold of dimension
d(s)
.

A
naive stratified-smooth map

f:X\to M
from
X
to a smooth manifold
M
is a continuous function for which each
f_{s}:X_{s}\to M
is smooth; we say
m\in M
is a regular value if it is a regular value for all maps
f_{s}:X_{s}\to M
.

An
isomorphism

(f,\bar{f})
between two naive stratified-smooth spaces
(X,\Delta_{X})
and
(Y,\Delta_{Y})
is an isomorphism of stratified spaces so that the induced maps between strata
f_{s}:X_{s}\to Y_{\bar{f}(s)}
are all diffeomorphisms.
∎

Let us discuss one standard example of a naive stratified-smooth space, as well as two important constructions which will be used in the definition of the local models below.

Example A.4
.

The space
[0,\infty)^{n}
can be given the structure of a naive stratified-smooth space. The stratification is by the cube poset
\Delta=\{0,1\}^{m}
; the faces are products of
\{0\}
and
(0,\infty)
. More precisely, write

F(i)=\begin{cases}\{0\}&i=0\\
(0,\infty)&i=1\end{cases}

Then the face associated to the stratum
s\in\{0,1\}^{m}
is the subspace

(0,\infty)^{s}:=\prod_{i=1}^{n}F(s(i));

that is, the
i
’th factor is
\{0\}
if
s(i)=0
and is
(0,\infty)
if
s(i)=1
.
∎

Example A.5
.

Let
(X,\Delta)
be a naive stratified-smooth space, and let
U\subset X
be an open subset. Then
U
also inherits the structure of a stratified-smooth space, taking
\Delta(U)\subset\Delta
to be those
s
for which
U\cap X_{s}\neq\varnothing
with strata
U_{s}=U\cap X_{s}
.
∎

Example A.6
.

Let
(X,\Delta)
be a naive stratified-smooth space of dimension
n
, and let
\psi:X\to\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
be a stratified-smooth map with zero as a regular value. Write
\Delta(\psi)\subset\Delta
for the sub-poset

\Delta(\psi)=\{s\in\Delta\mid\psi^{-1}(0)\cap X_{s}\neq\varnothing\},

with grading function
d_{\psi}(s)=d(s)-\ell
. Then
(\psi^{-1}(0),\Delta(\psi))
is a naive stratified-smooth space of dimension
n-\ell
.
∎

We combine these into one type of model space; a stratified-smooth space will be a naive stratified-smooth space locally isomorphic to one of the model spaces.

Definition A.7
.

Let
V\subset[0,\infty)^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
be an open subset containing zero, and let
\psi:V\to\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
be a naive stratified-smooth map with zero as a regular value. Then
Z(\psi)=\psi^{-1}(0)
carries the natural structure of a naive stratified-smooth space of dimension
m+n-\ell
. We call such a naive stratified-space
Z(\psi)
a
cutout locus
.
∎

Notice that the assumption
0\in V
implies that
\Delta(V)=\{0,1\}^{m}
; every face has a point near
0
and hence a point in
V
. Thus the stratification poset only changes when passing to
\Delta(\psi)
.

Before moving on to the definition of stratified-smooth space, we make a brief observation on the combinatorics of this poset
\Delta(\psi)
. The following argument is inspired by
[
33
, Section 2c]
, which we follow again when discussing orientations later.

Lemma A.8
.

The poset
\Delta(\psi)\subset\{0,1\}^{m}
is an upward-closed sub-poset; that is, if
s\in\Delta(\psi)
and
t\in\{0,1\}^{m}
has
t>s
, then
t\in\Delta(\psi)
as well.

Proof.

Suppose that
Z(\psi)_{s}\subset(0,\infty)^{s}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
is nonempty.
11
11

11

Recall that here
s\in\{0,1\}^{m}
and the exponent means the
i
’th factor is either
\{0\}
or
(0,\infty)
, depending on whether
s(i)
is
0
or
1
.
Because
Z(\psi)_{s}
is the preimage of a regular value, it is a smooth manifold of codimension
\ell
inside
(0,\infty)^{s}
. Choose a small sphere
c:S^{\ell-1}\to\big((0,\infty)^{s}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\big)\setminus Z(\psi)_{s}
which has linking number one with
Z(\psi)_{s}
, so that in particular
\psi c:S^{\ell-1}\to\mathbb{R}^{\ell}-\{0\}
is not null-homotopic.

It follows that in fact
c:S^{\ell-1}\to\big([0,\infty)^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\big)\setminus Z(\psi)
is not null-homotopic (for otherwise
\psi c
would be null-homotopic, which it is not).

Now for
s<t
, we may homotop
c
into
c^{\prime}:S^{\ell-1}\to(0,\infty)^{t}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
by an arbitrarily small homotopy; taking this homotopy sufficiently small, its image will not meet
Z(\psi)
, as
Z(\psi)
is closed and the domain is compact. In particular,
\psi c^{\prime}
is homotopic to
\psi c
as a map to
\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{0\}
, and therefore
\psi c^{\prime}
is not null-homotopic; thus
c^{\prime}
is not null-homotopic in
\big((0,\infty)^{t}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\big)\setminus Z(\psi)_{t}
. In particular,
Z(\psi)_{t}
is nonempty, which is what we wanted to show.
∎

Remark A.9
.

It follows that
\Delta(\psi)
is a
cubical poset
, which means that for each
s\in\Delta(\psi)
, we have
\Delta(\psi)_{\geq s}\cong\{0,1\}^{m}_{\geq s}\cong\{0,1\}^{m-d(s)}
; the poset of strata lying above a given stratum is isomorphic to the cube poset of appropriate dimension. Soon after we define the boundary operator, we will see that this observation underlies the relation
\partial^{2}=0
.∎

We now define the geometric objects of interest.

Definition A.10
.

A
stratified-smooth space
is a naive stratified-smooth space
(X,\Delta)
so that, for each
x\in X
, there exists an open subset
x\in U\subset X
and a cutout locus
Z(\psi)
so that
U
is isomorphic to
Z(\psi)
as naive stratified-smooth spaces. We call a choice of open set
U
and isomorphism
U\cong Z_{\psi}
a
cutout chart
for
x
.
∎

Note that in particular we do not have an ‘atlas’ of such charts in any sense; we merely demand that they exist.

Example A.11
.

In
[
15
]
, a sequence of increasingly more strict notions of manifolds with corners are defined, and
\langle N\rangle
-manifolds
defined there give examples of stratified-smooth spaces. Our Definition
3.4
of stratified-smooth manifold is very closely related to Joyce’s definition
\langle N\rangle
-manifold, though ours is a weaker object, as the smooth structure on the top stratum of a stratified-smooth manifold does not extend up to the boundary.

In particular, the simplex
\Delta^{k}
is a stratified-smooth space for all
k
, as are all products of simplices.
∎

Example A.12
.
